POULTRY PEN.

WHEN BIRDS DIE SUDDENLY. In some cases birds apparently in a healthy condition, which die suddenly, are found to have very rotten kidneys, liver, and spleen, says "The Poultry World." In other cases subjects of disease of their kidneys become very anemic, and may become almost little but skeletons before they die of kidney disease. On the other hand, birds which are heavy for their breed may be the subjects of disease of kidneys, but they rarely have bold thighs, therefore soft and flabby thighs in a bird that is heavy for its breed may be more than suspected of having unsound kidneys. An excess of urea being passed is a certain symptom of disease of kidneys, when it is persistent. When the excrement is of a dirty whitish colour, or has a yellowish tint, it is evidence of disease of liver and kidneys, ane, to an uncurable extent. When the birds are dull, having a slecy ap- pearance, it is the result of the blood being more or less poisoned by the retention of urea in it. When chickens do not thrive it is generally the result of disorder of kidneys, in some cases merely /owing to them being overtaxed, which is generally the case, owing to the chickens being supplied with a diet containing an excess of digestible solids in proportion to the flesh. forming elements, and the diet containing an excess of limesalts. This is supplied to chickens In some cases under the very erroneous impres- sion that the limesalts are necessary to promote the formation of bone. In the foodstuffs usually fed to chickens there is an excess of limesalts, without any addition of such substances as bone, or grit containing limesalts. When birds are not producing ego, as a rule they have an excess of limesalts supplied to them, and the foundation of disease of their kidneys is laid.

it, and thus waste is saved and profit is the result.

Then another great advantage is, there is no need to put up pens to keep the breeds apart. The Dairy Farmer Gets All The Profit.

The middleman is avoided. There is no time spent on packing for market, no expense with boxes, &c., and no extra expenditure in the way of labour in sending the eggs out. The milkman can always take them on his rounds when he delivers the milk, and at the same time solicit orders for dead poultry. This also can be dispatched in the same way. So the farmer gets the full retail prices for his stuff. People will always readily buy new-laid eggs from a farm, and the idea of poultry coming direct without being first exhibited in shops recommends itself at once.

Yet another question of waste that can be turned to profit is the spare milk. There is nothing like milk for fattening young stocli for table, and it can be used sour or fresh; also with a mixed flock, there is seldom any difficulty in getting a broody hen when the hatching season commences, and on such unlimited space the youngsters can be feeding and scratching all the time, the different sheds and buildings pro- viding them with excellent shelter from wind and rain in the early spring.

POULTRY HOUSES AND APPLIANCES. In the course of a leaflet recently issued on "Poultry Houses and Appliances for Allotment Holders and Cottagers," the Board of Agricul- ture deals with the principles that should be observed in the construction of poultry houses. They say : Simplicity of construction, mobility and economy of material are the desiderata in houses, runs and appliances used by poultry- keepers of all classes. Fowl-houses can be built inexpensively when time is available for the necessary work. Timber already used for other purposes is often at command, or may be pur- chased cheaply, and, in the neighbourhood of great centres of population, large packing cases can sometimes be bought at low prices. Timber merchants are often able to sell cheaply such cuttings and shorter length of deals as are not generally required by their customers, and which can be fitted together for boarding the walls and roof of the poultry house or for mak- ing coops and runs. It is necessary, and is usually most economical, to buy new scantling for the framework of the house; the cost of this, however, bears a small proportion to the whole.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
General Principles.

In building a poultry house the following im- portant points should be observed: (1) With regard to material, the timber should be sound and dry. The coil of deals is largely governed by the extent to which they have been seasoned. If newly cut there will be shrinkage and warp- ing as the natural moisture evaporates. It is therefore true economy to pay, say, 10 or 15 per cent. more for thoroughly seasoned wood. (2) Narrow deals are cheaper than broad ones. (3) Deals are sold by timber merchants by the square, that is, with a surface of 100 square feet; scantling for framework and joists is sold per 100 feet run. (4) In regard to construction, the back, two sides nd roof of the h9use should be solid, and air and water tight. For this pur- pose well-seasoned, tongued and grooved deals are preferable, if ' tightly clamped together. These should be fixed to the frame perpendicu- larly. If fixed horizontally special deals must be used, each overlapping that below. These are more expensive, having to be specially cut, and a large amount of timler is required. •

===== 0002601_19150515_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
Ventilation.

.(5) Ventilation must be adequate. It is best I secured by making the house, which should I always be the maximum height in front, what is called "open-fronted," that is, a space of 15 to 30 inches above the floor should be hoarded and the rest covered with wire netting. This ensures a constant circulation .of air without draught. The inmates are quite comfortable, even on the coldest night, and the air is not at such times saturated with moisture, which is the case if ventilation is .insufficient. To prevent rain driving in, a sloping or sliding shutter should be fixed outside. Open-fronted houses do not usually need windows, as the lighting is abundant. °Sunlight purifies the atmosphere and kills many forms of organisms. (6) The internal fittings should be as simple as possible, and capable of easy and immediate removal. Perches may be made of 2in. by 2in. scantling with the corners smoothed off, resting on sup- ports fixed .to the sides, 15iu. to 21t. above the ground in poefable liuses, or 31t. above the ground in fixed houses, with, in the latter case, a dropping-board below, in order to give a greater amount of scratching space. Nest boxes in portable houses should be raised 12in., and one compartment should be alloWed for every four or five hens. With a little alteration orange boxes are excellent for this purpose.

(7) Cleanliness is supremely irnpoitnnt. The whole house—roof, walls and floors—should be regularly •brushed over; nesting materials should be renewed frequently, and lime-wash- ing or spraying with a good dkinfectant twice A. year is essential.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0013.txt | included=true | included =====
POULTRY KEEPING FOR DAIRY FARMERS.

This seems a branch of business that is neg- lected by a largo number of dairy farmers, says "The Feathered World." Such a small outlay is needed that with ;just a little Management it should prove a very profitable side line. At the present time there: is an enormous demand for eggs and poultry owing to the cessation to a very large extent of Continental supplies, and this, of course, is likely to last as long as the war lasts, and for some time afterwards. Time and space will not allow me to enumer- ate all the advantages the dairy farmer has over the ordinary utility poultry farmer.

The First Item Is Rent.

No extra ground is needed for the birds, consequently there is no rent to pay. Free range is an excellent thing for the utility poultry, and on a dairy farm they certainly get it. Then they practically half keep them- selves having all this space to roam about, as they are always on the scratch and forage. This keeps them in the condition necessary for egg production, and prevents them becoming mopish. Anyone with the smallest experience knows the amount of food there is always about where cows are kept that otherwise 'would be wasted. 'Chickens will always find

STRONG CHICKS, cross-bred, for laying and table; day to week old, 68. dozen. Orders taken for month old, Bs., carriage paid.—Atkins, Windy- gates, Frodsbam.

QVCCESSFUL CHICKEN REARING de- pends in a great measure upon careful atten- tion to detail, as clean drinking pots and fresh water at least twice daily, clean litter in coops, a con- stant supply of small grit and shell, and newly- limewashed coops and brooders before and after each hatch. Good sound wholesome food is also of equal importance. ENTWISTLE BROS.' DRY CHICK FOOD has long been recognised as the sweetest, by far the most nutritious, and evenly balanced ration offered to Poultry Keepers, and the one most suitable for young Chickens. TRADES COUNCIL'S ATTITUDE.

Women Who Resister Should Jo!

Unions.

At their monthly meeting on Tuesday even- ing, the delegates to the Warrington Trades and Labour Council decided to endorse the conclusion arrived at by the National Con- ference on War Service for Women, arranged by the Workers' National (War Emergency) Committee. Those conclusions included one to the effect that a condition of the employment of women for war service shall be that they should immediately join the appropriate Trade Union in • the trade for which they volunteer service. . Another clause stipulates that where a woman is doing the same work as a man she shall receive the same rate of pay and, a third, that .in no case shall any woman be employed at less than an adequate living wage, and that the stereotyping of sweated conditions must at all costs be avoided. "

HAVE YOU NEURASTHENIA?

Neurasthenia is a-condition of exhaustion of the nervous system. The causes are varied. Continuous strain, mental or physical, without proper rest or holidays, without proper 'atten- tion to diet and exercise, also worry .over the

struggle for existence, ail the most "common causes. Excesses of any kind may produce it. Some diseases, like influenza, will cause it. So will a severe shock, anxiety,_ or grief._ _

The symptoms of. Neurasthenia include over- sensitiveness, irritability, sleeplessness, weak- ness, with an absence of all energy, headaclles, and often nausea. - .

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 15, 1915.

STORIES FROM TyE TRENCHES "GETTING SHELLED OUT." 'Terrier "• Tells Why Billets Are Worse Than Trenches.

Signaller A. Waywell, who is attached to the 4th (Territorial) Battalion of the South' Lancashire Regiment, writing to his mother, who lives at 32, Forster-street, Warrington, on April 28th, says:— "We are in billets again . . . and as usual we are getting shelled out by the Ger- mans, who are at least six miles away, but who have got the range of this village tO a T.' Yesterday a fresh batch arrived from England and they had only been here an hoar or so when shelling commenced, and, I am sorry to say, that six of them got killed and several wounded, and all that before they had ever seen a trench, or had had time to settle down. The chaps at the farm-house where we are noiv billeted have made quite a safe barricade and whenever we have cause to think that the usual is coming off we run to it—our 'dug-out' we call it. At times the enemy drop their shells right in the midst of the village and cause a panic amongst the women and children.

Villagers Who "Stand the Racket." "If you could only _hear them—or, for the matter of that, see them—you would pity them. A number of the villagers have packed up their belongings and gone, but there are some who would not leave, whatever happened. I cannot understand them at all! I am sure that if the same thing were happening in Eng- land the inhabitants would be off in quick time. . . . You may think it queer, but it is safer to be in the trenches than it is in this village. We come out here to rest from the trenches, but we get many a run for safety Once we are in the trenches we are nearly as safe as being at home. An occasional shot comes through the barrier of sand-bags We did very well last time in, on account, I think, of the trench and parapet having been improved every day. . .

Souvenir-Hunting.

"Everything possible is done for the safety of the troops, but nothing can prevent those horrid shells coming. .. . When shelling is over the chaps go out collecting souvenirs. Shrapnel shells contain anything from rusty nails and old iron down to lead bullets, about the size of 'glass alleys out of ' pop ' bottles. . . . . Quite a number of civilians were killed last night with these things. .. . Our boys are getting quite used to the game now; in fact, they are hardened to it. We have some good leaders, and that is everything. I was with the battalion in the trenches last week, and we spent a good time. The weather was splendid; except for one night, when it poured with rain; but, fortunately, it soon dried up again. . . . When we are in the trenches we are so near to the Germans that we could almost speak to them; indeed some of the chaps do occasionally shout over the sandbags to them.

German Inquires After His Family.

"The other day I heard a story of a German who called from his trench to know whether there was anybody in the British trench who came from Birmingham, and added that he had a wife and two children there. And the reply was 'Keep your head down or there will be another widow and more orphans there!' Down went his head in quick time. The Germans do not show themselves very much; in fact, in the daytime, we never see one at all. At night working parties may been seen outside their parapet working away- Then, all of a sudden, up goes a ' star ' light from our trenches and— well, you would laugh to see them scatter. Numbers have been 'popped off' in the act."

Armoured-car Raid.

In a postscript the writer tells of another bombardment of the village in which he was quartered, as a result of which the inhabitants fled, and fresh billets were found for nearly all, the troops. 'lt was said, he added, that an armoured motor-car, by some means, got through and threw scores of shells into the village, killing several and injuring many more. Signaller Waywell has been in the Terri- torials some seven or eight years, for three of which he was a member of the cyclist corps, and for two a signaller. He is married and has two children.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
WHEN THOUGHTS TRAVEL

HOMEWARDS.

A Warrington Territorial officer writes It is Sunday evening, and one's thoughts travel homewards. Three of us huddled to- gether in a little " dug-out "in our support trenches. It is just dark, and the bullets are whistling over from the fire trenches, whilst the shells continue to screech overhead, and from the same old direction comes the everlasting boom of the guns.

A Dug-out Is Not a Dug-out.

- - - I said we were in a " dug-out "; perhaps a description is useful. The average dug-out is not a dug-out at all, but merely a shanty built from filled sandbags and roofed with materials gathered anyhow from ruined buildings, and then sandbags placed on top. Our present one is comparatively palatal, and was only built last time we were here, and for the present it is fairly sweet and clean. It is about 7ft.. by lift. by 3ft. 6in. high. On top is about 2ft. of earth, making the structure fairly proof against shell splinters. It is dug down about 2ft. into the earth; the remaining height is made up of sandbags. There is just a little door opening, through which you have to crawl, and I think a dog entering its kennel is the simplest resemblance of our getting in here. Once in the kennel it is snug and comfort- able, and allows of a little security for rest. In the support trenches, although only 200 yards behind the firing line, you have a chance of rest, but once in the fire trench you ard on the go all the time, particularly at nights, when rest is impossible. We are constructing a series of new dug-outs,

and if they get completed we shall all be more snug after the openness and monotony of the fire trench.

Fowl Shooting, But No Casualties.

Just- before dark we had ,some wild fowl shooting in a pond to our rear. The water hens appear at dusk. We thought we had procured a decent bag, but on investigation we -could find nothing in the way of feathered casual-

The ground in the vicinity of the trenches, where, of course, untrodden, is clothing in green, and the hedges are all on the burst, but somehow it does not take on the character of an English springtime. I have received all your parcels. Our little company mess all join in thanks for the tobaeco. The cigarettes were distributed and much appreciated, they wish me to convey their thanks.

We hear little news other than rumours, but perhaps they are a little more authentic than some you are getting in England. Such daily papers as we see give us an inkling as to what is going on, and generally they tally with the news that pass current here.

The Gas Fumes.

You will have read about the German gas fume k We have experienced them to a small degree. •We have taken steps to nullify their effects. It has 'roused our men to a state of fury. Our rest camp last time was not a Suc- cess, practically every day we were shelled and lost several men, some killed and others badly wounded.

Our billet had a near shave. One large ,shell dropped within fifteen yards of the front door; how it got over the ridge of the house to drop so close was a marvel.

We had plenty of shrapnel over the house, some knocl-"d at the front door, and other bullets came through the roof and rolled down the stairs into the kitchen. A Trench Incident.

Yesterday men sweating and filling sandbags in the usual soldierly language : "When I gets into a bed when I get home and finds a hard pillow I shall ask the missus' who put this 'ere

===== 0002601_19150515_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
Hard At Work.

REJECTED, BUT PERSEVERED.

)etermined " Terrier's " Call To Younfj

Men.

The following letter has been sent to Councillor Cunnington, of 32. Longford-street. by Private W. McDonough, of the 2/ 4th South Lancashires, who joined the colours three weeks after the outbreak of war, but was rejected after five weeks' service. Since then he has tried again, and has been accepted for the Territorials.

"An Awful Thing for a Britisher to Say." . "Why do so many big, able-bodied men still remain at home," be asks, "when their.country need them, and appeal for them most urgently in a cause which is just and honourable? No man should say when asked to join the Army, ''l Will when there is no work. to do,' or 'Let the loafers from the street corners go!' I think that is an awful thing for a Britisher to say; to depend an the loafers of the town to safeguard the children and homes of our Empire. I think that no Britisher who is medically fit, should need much asking to fight for the honour of his country at a time like this. No man. who honours his country Should wait for a Conscrip- tion law to pass before he dons the uniform, for Lord Kitchener believes that one volunteer is far better than two pressed men. I hope the country will never have need for the conscrip- tion law, for I think we will prove ourselves British in the long run.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
A Lesson in Store for the Hun.

"The Belgians and French have proved their worth, and now our armies are trying to do the same; and with the help of the remaining able- bodied men we will do so, and show the German church destroyers and Red Cross nurse killers what, it is to fight with honour for a brother country, and to avenge an insult. I -say to all able-bodied men:

"'Think of little Belgium! how her towns were destroyed and her women and children tor- tured and made homeless. Think of this occur- ring in England; then ask yourself if you ought not to join .the Army, to stop this wretched work from going on further. New, then, show your breed and •go straight to the nearest re- cruiting office and sign on. If you fail to pass, try to get. others to go, and then you will be deserving Of honour. as much as those at the Front.'

===== 0002601_19150515_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
Going Out With the Next Draft.

After signing the foregoing "A Discharged Man," the writer adds a postcript, in which he says: "I have joined the Army at last, and I am going out from Tunbridge Wells with the next draft, to strengthen our First Battalion. It is the hope of my life, to help my country in such a good cause. I have two brothers already at the Front, and I would like to give them or anybody else a helping hand, for I know they are needing it."

===== 0002601_19150515_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
POISONOUS GAS SHELLS.

" Terrier " Has A Slight Experience Of

Them.

Writing to his grandmother, Mrs. M. Bond, of 9, Admiral-street, Howley, Warrington, Private John Henry Carter, of the 1/4th South Lancashires, says Last week the Huns started shelling our . . All the people were running to and fro, when I met a poor woman with a. child in her arms and two others ,pulling at her wrists. Although I had my rifle in my hand I tried to carry on of the youngsters in my other arni. I think if the young bids of Warrington, 'Who still remain at home, could see what I have .seen they would not need any persuading before they joined.. . . Our lads haye 'gone through it' since they have been out here. . . I ,thank all at home. who have written to me. It helps us to forget the hardships, and to remember why we are undergoing' such hardship. when ,we receive a few lines from home. . . . I don't sthink the war will last long now! . . Last week the Germans sent some shells emitting poisonous gases into our trenches, and we felt the effects• slightly. . . . .We can't expect holidays at a time like this, and, of course, we do our little bit with a good heart."

"In another and later letter to Mr. and Mrs. Lloyd, the gallant " Terrier " refers, with regret, to the fact that his . cousin— Private Joe Lloyd—had been wounded, and goes on to say "We have brought down a couple of aeroplanes this last week. . . . have come across Joe Wadsworth and Bob Sisson. Both were 'in the pink and both have horses. . . . We are looking forward to another good time at Blackpool. .• . . The weather is grand, and the condition Of both men and horses is im- proving. . . . in fact, the sun has made us as block as the fire-grate itself. . . . lam glad to hear that ,Warrington Bridge is completed at last, and I'll bet it looks grand. . . . The Germans seem to be using up their last resources; indeed, they must be in a bad Way when they have to resort to pojsonous gases."

===== 0002601_19150515_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
LIKE BEING AT BELLE ATE.

Local Lancer Describes The German • "Fireworks." Writing to his aunt, Mrs. Sykes, of 52, Gorsey- lane, Warrington, Private W. Peake, of D Squadron, 17th Lancers, rays: "Another big battle has just finished, and we have been in support again, lying just behind the firing line, watching the German fireworks going off again. It was just like being at Belle Vue, and our cannons were giving them socks somewhere, for it was a continual roar for days and nights. We had a splendid view of one of our aero- pianists getting shelled by the Germans. He was fairly pulling their legs; dodging from place to place, only to have a few more having a go at him. But he got through it all right. Canadians Bear the Brunt of Fierce Attack. "We did not get going again at all, but the Canadians were fairly in the thick of it, for the main German attack was centred against them, and I. believe they lost heavily. We are in a different part of the country now, and it seems funny that after being able to make people understand at our last place, they cannot under- stand a word here. Perhaps you can guess the place, but I must not tell you. We expect to move from here any time, as we are under a moment's notice to go to some other part of the line, but I don't know where it will be. I have lost my feather bed in quick time, and have not had my boots off for a week: Also I have had to be satisfied with one blanket, and a grass bed to sleep-in. But all's fair in love and war, and we are having lovely weather now, so we cannot grumble." Private Peake served in the South African war with the 3rd Cheshires, and holds the medal for that campaign. Afterwards he joined the 17th Lancers. He was born at Houghton Green and was educated at Risley and Winwick.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME B t AUGUST.

" Terrier's " View As To The Duration

Of The War.

Driver W. G. A. Beckett, of the 87th Battery of the Royal Field Artillery, writing from France to Mr. and Mrs. Lloyd, 36, Wakefield- street, Warrington, whose son is in. the Terri- torials and knew him, gives some news re- garding himself and other Warrington "boys," whose parents, he thinks, will be glad to hear of them through "The Examiner." "The South Lancs.," the writer says, " are making steady progress, and also a good name for them- selves. . . . Tie question 'How long will the war last? is a very difficult one to answer. My own answer, and it is also the answer of a good many others out here, is that those of us who are alive will land back about next August. Out here we do not trouble ourselves as to how long it will last, for we know that we are sure to win. Still we shall prefer to see it finished to having it go on indefinitely.

.. . I have not seen ioe Wadsworth for fire long months. . . . but I hope he is still in the land of the living. . .

What About Some Cigarettes?

- " Even when we are in the firing line we have plenty of harness to clean, as well as our boots, spurs and buttons, but we don't mind, for it passes the spare moments away when one has no inclination for letter writing. . . . and am looking forward to some proper cigarettes, for, at the present moment, there isn't one in the whole battery. We are doing out best with some hand-made ones, but more often than not we make a proper mess of them when we are trying to roll them. •. . The Examiner' is a very welcome paper out here; in fact, we prefer it to any other, for there are always a lot of familiar names in the -matter which appears about the South Laucashires, and we like to read of one another's doings."

Old Warrington " Pals" Meet After Long Separation.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
ANTWERP'S PASSIVE RESISTANCE.

King's Birthday Celebration.

From the " Daily Chronicle " Special Corre-

spondent.

NORTH OF FRANCE, May 4

A Belgian officer has -received from one of his friends who resides. in Antwerp the follow- inn' touching description of the ,manner in which the people of Antwerp displayed their patriotism on the day of King Albert's birth- day. The letter, by the way, took three weeks to reach its destination.

"We have lived our first happy day, since we were left here in the midst of the enemy," says the writer of the letter. "It was last Thursday, April 8, the birthday of the King., Without any organisation, but with' one cord, the entire population decided to celebrate it with the utmost solemnity. The evening before, the newspapers announced that they would not publish next day, and .that the Town Hall, the banks, the schools, &c., would he closed. As soon as the 'Bodies' got wind of this they ordered 'the employes of .the Town Hall to attend to their work as usual. Then they hurried to the Echevin of Public 'lnstruc- tion, but they were less successful. He replied that it was holiday time, and that the schools would not be opened. As to the banks and newspapers, of course, nothing could be done. "The day was a perfect orgy of the national colours. There was a veritable shower ,of tri- colour bows—everyone wore one. The men had them discreetly in their buttonholes, but the women wore th,em boldly, each according to, her fancy. One saw the three colours blos- som forth in enormous `ohoux." on bats, blouses, and coats. Some people even wore tricolour stockings. Without knowing one an- other passers-by smiled at the sight of the ensign. They all undersimood. A malicious joy took possession of. us that day, for the demon- stration was a gauntlet thrown down in .defi- ance of Prussian authority. "Three young girls, about 16 years of age, walked along the Place de Meir together; one of them was dressed entirely in red; the second in yellow, and the third all in black. Their little walk was one of the most admired and successful events of the day. "The afternoon brought with it its usual festive crowd dressed in its best. Antwerp promenaded its patriotism silently; dignifiedly, and proudly. Before this calm but firm mani- festation the `Boches' deemed it wise to place machine guns at the railway station, each served by two men.

"I think our King was never so near a hearts."

THE LIVING BANNER.

Germany May temporarily overrun and over- ride the soil of Belgium, but she cannot kill her spirit or her wit, says the "Daily Telegraph" referring to the incident of the girls dressed in the national colours. . . . So far the demon- stration was negative, but in the early after- noon the population of Antwerp, which, as a rule these days, keeps at home, came out and thronged the streets.. It appeared as if these orderly thousands waited for some pageant to pass. _ _

Then suddenly three little girls appeared, walking Tide by side in the very centre of the silent crowd. One was dresoed all in black, and the second was dressed all in 'yellow, and the third was a flame of red. So the living flag moved/ on, a symbol of unconquerable patriot- ism, moved on through the streets and past the very muzzles of the sullen machine-guns which the Germans have placed in the great station square.

Eyes were wet and beads bared as the pageant, fragile as a flower, went by. •

SANGER'S CIRCUS.

Hall-an-hour With An Elephant's

Dressmaker.

" Few people who visit the circus realise the amount of money that is spent upon an ele- phant's wardrobe," said Lord John Sanger— whose Hippodrome is to visit Warrington on Thursday next—to our representative. "In the course of a year we spend something like £5OO to provide just plain costumes for our performing animals, to say nothing about more elaborate affairs for 'best '-wear. "To make a single costume takes several weeks. This will be obvious when it is stated that the average-sized elephant has a delicate ' waist ' of about 13it. in circumference, and he measures about loft, from shoulders to hind-. quarters, and about the same height. Forty- six yards of material, exclusive of trimmings, is required to complete the 'costume.' "We can only use the finest and most costly materials," sail Lord John; sadly, "or the , elephant would be always tearing the garment to pieces. Just this plain article, made of silk or satin, will alone run into £l.O, and the trim- mings of gold lace and other decorations will run away with another five-pound note. It will then only last the animal -three weeks. "The elephant's clothes have to be cut and fitted in just the same way as a, lady's would be in Bond-street, with the exception that we can- not use a paper pattern, and require a ladder to fit tlfe elephant. We have to substitute a canvas pattern for one of paper. "At first, however, the animals strongly object 'to being dressed, and much prefer to appear in the arena in Nature's garb. We have to get the animals' used to it gradually. When they are in their quarters a piece of canvas is thrown ,carelessly over their backs. This is nearly al*ays instantly torn off, but we repeat it time after time until the animal patiently submits. "Should the animal still resist, however, we resort ,to flattery, just as the fashionable costumier does when she makes up her mind to persuade a customer to purchase an expensive garment she doesn't want. We get one of the older animals and dress him in the most striking colours possible, and lead him to a huge mirror. The new candidate is allowed to see the pretty effect the clothes have on its mate, and will afterwards submit to the most troublesome dressing." Included in Thursday's performance, as will be seen by our advertising columns, will be a great Russian Cossack display in three parts, terminating with a realistic exhibition of Cos- sack trick riding. Sangers' marvellous :comedy sea, lions which perform extremely clever tricks are another feature of the "show," while the inimitable " Pimpo " the clever clown, will provide the usual round of fun. The pro- gramme contains an abundance of other divert- ing items, all of the first-class order which is associated with Lord John Sanger and Son. There will be 'a street parade prior to the afternoon performance.

RATE INCREASE IN ST. HELENS.

The St. Helens Gas Committee decided on Wednesday that it would be necessary td in- crease the pike of gas 4d. per 1,000 feet on account of the advance in the price of fuel. The committee are unable to give the usual' £5,000 for the relief of the rates, 'and as all the other committees are overspent, the out- look suggests that a further large increase of probably 6d. or 7d. will be necessary in the rates.

FARM AND GARDEN.

PROFITABLE PIG-KEEPING.

The outdoor system of pig-keeping is an admirable one, especially where pigs are bred mainly for sale as breeders. It is a system which 1 followed for nearly fifty years, and one to which I attribute a considerable share of the satisfaction expressed by my customers in all parts of the world with the healthiness and thrift of the pigs sold to them. There is little doubt that a very considerable proportion of the ill-success attending the purchase of pure bred pigs arises from the unnatural conditions under which so large a number of the pedigree pigs are reared and fed. But in the production of pork, whether for the London purveyor, the country butcher, or even for the bacon-curer, outdoor life is not one which gives the greatest amount of profit. A pig having its complete liberty will consume a greater weight of food and manufacture a proportionately smaller weight of 'pork than will a pig comfortably housed. Time is money in the making of pork. The earlier in life a pig attains to the suitable weight, other things being equal, the greater profit will be left. In order to secure the best returns, the two processes of growing and fattening must be carried on concurrently.' One of the sources of profit in pig-keeping is the manurg. Under Mr. Edge's system of keep- ing his pigs in plantations, this would appear to be lost. I would suggest that he could obtain the same advantages as to cheap food, exercise and health for his pigs if he kept them in an orchard, since the manure would be beneficially employed, and both the quantity and quality of the fruit be improved. I could cite several in- stances of this; one was reported to me from the owner of an apple orchard of some thirty acres, to whom I sold breeding pigs, of which he kept large numbers in his orchard, the trees in which showed signs of exhaustion. He declared the effect to be. simply marvellous. Another instance was that of a Kentish grower of cherries. Ile informed me that keeping fat- ting pigs in his orchard resulted in the growth of a greater quantity of finer cherries.—Sanders Spencer, in "Country Life."

WAR AND MILK PRICES.

It is claimed that shortage of labour, due to recruiting, has affected work both on the farm and on the city milk rounds. In one case a , farmer with a dairy of thirty .cows was unable to obtain a milker and sold his stock. "That sort of thing," a "Daily Chronicle" represen- tative was told, "is happening all over the country." Then within a week a city firm which had bought milk from that farmer lost ten employes, all of whom had enlisted, and it was unable to replace them. "As a consequence, we hardly know how to make our deliveries from day to day," said the head of the firm; "and we have had to refuse orders on the outskirts of rounds. The experiment has been tried in some directions of engaging women to do the rounds, but it is not a success. There is a difficulty about getting the right type of woman. You have to remember that the milkman turns out about 4.30 in the morning to be at the shop at 5 o'clock, and on his walk about half an hour later. All this means that the milkmen remain- ing have to be given more money; one, I know, had a rise of ss. .a week when he spoke of going to another firm." Much depends on the weather. If there is a dry season milk will cost more.

SEDIMENT IN MILK. Varying Reports By Experts As To

Quantity.

The case against a leading local firm of dairy- I men for selling milk containing a sediment I was resumed at the Colwyn Bay Petty Sessions on Saturday. At the previous hearing, at the close of the case for the prosecution, it was ordered that the third sample should be for- warded to Somerset House for analysis •-y the Government analysts, and the case was adjourned for a month. Mr. T. Garth Jones again appeared to prosecute for the County Council, and the defendant firm was repre- sented by Mr. T. S. Steel, of Warrington. The evidence given on the previous occasion was read over, from which it appeared that the County Analyst had certified that the sample " contained sediment 27.8 parts per 100.000, which consisted chiefly of cow-dung, equal to 191 grains per gallon. The certificate of the Somerset House Analysts was read, in which it was stated that they found sediment amounting to about 15 parts per 100,000, measured by volume. It was impossible to determine with accuracy how much of the sediment was attributable to cow-dung, but from a microscopical examination they were of opinion that roughly one-half of the sediment was due to cow-dung. _

Mr. Steel said that when experts disagreed it was difficult to know who was to decide. He commented upon the great differences in the result of the two analyses, and stated that the experts he had intended to call would have shown a greater difference still, as the defendants' portion of the sample contained practically no sediment at all. In _view, how- ever, of the certificate, from Somerset House, he presumed the Bench would feel inclined to accept their finding, and in that case it would be useless calling any expert evidence on be- half of the defendants. Mr. Steel then addressed the Bench in miti- gation, pointing out that the milk in question had been obtained to meet an increased demand in consequence of several thousand soldiers having been billeted In the //town, and not being obtained from the usual source of supply, the defendants had been unable to obtain from the seller the usual warranty. The defendants had done all that could be reasonably expected of them in sieving the milk, and 'although they took every care in seeing that their customers got. the best milk procurable, in this case the defendants were the victims of circum- stances.

The defendants were fined £l, to include all costs other than the analysts' and advocates' fee.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
MILK WARRANTY.

Somewhat unusual proceedings were taken at the Colwyn Bay Petty Sessions on Saturday, when a Staffordshire farmer was summoned for giving a false warranty in respect of a quantity of milk consigned to a Colwyn Bay milk seller. Mr. T. Garth Jones, of Ruthin, prosecuted for 'the Denbigh County Council, and the defendant was represented by Mr. T. S. Steel, of Warrington. It was stated for the prosecution that a sample was taken of the defendant's milk con- signed to a Colwyn Bay milk seller, and on analysis it was found /to contain an amount of sediment consisting partly of cow-dung, equal to 4.7 grains per gallon. The milk seller had been summoned for selling the milk in ques- tion, and pleaded that he sold the milk under a warranty that the milk was pure, from the present defendant. The milk seller was dis- charged, and the present proceedings had been taken against the farmer. Mr. Steel, for the defence, said that the de- fendant, when he gave the warranty, had reason to believe that the statements were true. He had nearly 100 milking cows, and the udders were washed before each milking. The cans in the shinpons were covered with lids, and be- fore being dispatched the milk passed through no less than three gauze and muslin sieves. The defendant's farm was worked on the latest scientific lines, and the defendant was at a , loss to understand the presence of sedi- ment. Mr. Steel took the point that if the Bench were satisfied that the defendant had reason to believe that the warranty was true, the defendant was entitled to an acquittal. Having heard evidence bearing out Mr. Steel's statement as to the methods adopted at the defendant's farm, the case was dismissed.

HOME FROM THE WARS.

\\Torrington's Welcome To Wounded.

Warriors.

SCENES AND INCIDENTS.

The neighbourhood of the newest Warrington Irospitat for wounded soldiers, the Whitecross HosPital, was the scene of a great patriotic demonstration on Wednesday evening, whefl ninety-four wounded warriors from French's "contemptible little army," were welcomed as. returning heroes and admitted for treatment and rest. Some of these men will not be in e the final victory, but they will have laid the foundation upon which that victory wig assuredly rest.

Fresh From France.

Departing from Southampton on the previous. day, these war-worn members of French's- original arrived at Manchester late in the after- noon, and were accommodated for the night at the Western General Hospital (No. Dy leaving there in motor ambulances on Wednes- day afternon, between four and five o'clock, en route for Warrington. The privileged street that leads to the hos- pital seemed to know that there was something in the wind, and long before the first ambulance nosed its careful way round the corner, the pavements of the approach were studded with groups of talking women and the roadway occupied by children, some whiling away the, time with the games most fashionable at the moment in juvenile circles, others too excited to do more than knock their heels together and listen to the conversation of their elders.

"See The Conquering Heroes Come:"

- - - . - At last the patient vigil-keepers in the drizzling rain and the cold were rewarded. Amid a hum of expectation, the 'first of the expected cars swung round the corner and whirred its way up the street. The back of the covered-in vehicle was open, and in a moment a score of hands and handkerchiefs were waving a welcome to the pathetic figures at full length on the stretchers within. And a few of these gallants found the strength to raise themselves on elbow and wave feebly in return. Another and another ambulance glided round the bend and passed up the street, and meanwhile the spectators ,gathered more thickly on the kerbs, and the handwavings in- creased with the increasing numbers, and the children got more and more exciteorl. To be sure, they did not fully understana, but they' did know that brave men had come back from the land of battles with scars of honour to mark how they had fought and bled. Even the babies in their mothers' arms crowed and chuckled, and boldly imitated the wavings of the others.

Flinging A Flower. After this incident, no one was timid, and when after a wait of ten minutes there was a fresh flutter of excitement, and another ited Cross motor passed along on its way to the gates, there was a stampede in pursuit of the van, and the recumbent soldiers were treated to a fierce bombardment of cigarettes andl chocolates. Sometimes there were disappoint' ments, for in a number of instances the ends of the vans were not onen. Kissing the Baby. All the patients in the motors were not en their backs; some, indeed, were so far recovered as to be able to lean out and shake hands with the only-too-eager crowd. One fellow, with his head out of the window, was merrily hand-shak' ing all round, when he espied a baby in its mother's arms. "Let's kiss the baby, ma," be begged, and " ma " brought baby over, and, 85 baby didn't object, the transaction took Place' This, of course, occurred while the car was sta- tionary, quite a number of the vehicles being brought to a standstill by tbye press of enthusi- astic numbers,. bringing oranges, bananas, cigar' ettes. and other trifles.

IS GOD GOOD?

A Unitarian Reply:

The Ultimate,

Benevolence of Pain.

'IS it possible, under the present terrible circumstances, to speak believingly of God's goodness, love and care "? This question was asked by the Rev. Irvine Lister, at the Cairo- street Unitarian Church, on Sunday morning. The reverend gentleman, dealing with the Problem of pain and suffering, a problem of 'which the war was only a part, suggested a Possible solution. We were, he said, free human beings, and in the use of our freedom there was a certain element of venture. We Stood on the shore of unknown waters and ventured forth though ignorant of the result; and the penalty of wrong choice was pain and soffering and sometimes death. Pain and :liffering had been factors in man's progress r°ro the first. God used pain and sufferini, to 4,llsitruet, develop and educate his children. not ',a, at first sight, wholly evil things, were to bieneumpatible with the love of God. It was that feared that we had not yet arrived at wh, Stage where it was possible to dispense R-t;;;It. War, which was the outcome of selfish `'vings. The horrors of war were a punish- to__ ent for following a wrong course, but, when ,e. learned to strive for something worth ,7 triving for, such happenings as those of the Present would be impossible.

ST. MARY'S HALL.

Opened Amid Celebration By Abbot of Ampleforth:

===== 0002601_19150515_art0044.txt | included=true | included =====
"LAITY'S UPPER HAND."

There is a meeting ground outside the church for the clergy and laity, where the laity have in one sense the upper hand— where they have to provide all the arrange- ments, and where the success of the venture rests principally upon them.

So spoke the Abbot of Ampleforth (the Right Rev. J. 0. Smith) on Monday evening. The occasion was the opening of the new St. Mary's Hall and Rooms, Buttermarket-street— to celebrate which an excellent concert and a dance were held.

Early in the evening the Abbot blessed the imposing new structure, room by room.

BUILDINGS DESCRIBED.

The buildings consist ,of a large hall (which will accommodate 600 persons) and a series of handsome clubrooms for the St. Mary's branch of the Catholic Young Men's Society. They have been erected during a period of nine months by Messrs. Travis and Wevill (of Liver- pool, who in 1906 also built the new church tower); Messrs. Pugin and Pugin (Liverpool and London) designed them. The congregation of St. Mary's and many friends besides are working hard to raise funds to defray the cost. of the undertaking. A big bazaar (with Mr. A. Kilburn as hon. secretary): was to have been held by now. It has, however been decided not under any cir- cumstances to hold it until the war is over. The site on which the new premises stand, together with the land adjoining—covering alto- gether about 2,000 square yards—was acquired nearly eighteen months ago. The situation has been well chosen, for it is a.convenient centre for the parish social activities—being only a few yards from the church itself.

Should further developments be found desir- able, there will be ample space on the adjacent ground.

Capital Club Rooms.

The lighting system is of incandescent . gas, and. hot-water pipes run throughout the build- ings—to which there are two large entrances and exits, with ladies' and gentlemen's cloak- rooms and lavatories on either side.

The spacious hall (98ft. long and 37ft. wide), besides providing accommodation for concerts on its large platform, is excellently suited for dancing also. The clubrooms, situate on the second floor, are approached by a handsome stone staircase overlooked by a small balcony. They consist of an imposing room containing two large-size billiard tables—for the use of senior members of the C.Y.M.S.—and another for the junior members.

Probably the small library in the reading- room will presently be considerably developed. Linoleum is being laid throughout the rooms, and chairs and benches installed.

The Young Men's Society (to which Mr. J. Dolan acts as president, and Mr. R. Price as vice-president and hon. secretary) are also to have a miniature shooting range outside their rooms.

On the ground floor is a large room for re- freshments, and beneath this are a small kitchen, cellar &c.—which also have a separate exit.

It is interesting to note that several perform- ances in aid of the building fund have been given on various occasions at the Star Kinema House, Church-street.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
OPENING CEREMONY.

After Mr. T. Biake-";.-band had given a rousing march, "Sounds of Peace," the formal opening ceremony. took place. The Rev. Father Wilson presided, and, besides the Abbot, there were on the platform the Revs. Fathers Whittle, Basil Primavesi, Swarbreck, Dawson and Mawson, Messrs. A. Kilburn, J. Eckersley, .T. Dolan and R. Price. Dr. Wilson, of Liverpool, was unfortunately pre- vented from attending.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LAITY'S CHANCE.

The Abbot, on rising to speak, was enthusi- astically received. Having declared the hall open, he went on to say that he was sure Father Wilson, the priests and the congrega- tion of St. Mary's were proud of it. In parish work the principal part fell to tlke clergy; but in an institution such as this new one was given to the laity the chance of doing some- thing within their power which did not encroach upon the work of the Priests. He remembered a famous clergyman of the Church of England coming into their church and ask- ing Father Marshall what was the position of the Catholic laity. Father Marshall replied: "Sitting before the pulpit, and kneeling before the altar." (Laughter.) But there was lay work which they could chi without .either listening or kneeling, and the success and value—or the failure—of this latest under- taking depended chiefly upon the congregation. By a small amount of sacrifice on their part lasting success would be assured.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
Enemies' "Shocking Conduct."

"It is, " concluded the Abbot, "very sad to think we meet together on such a day as this —when the whole civilised world is mourning the shocking conduct of our enemies. But we shall be able to bear even such distresses as that if we hold together in our mtitual inter- course, rather than keeping apart. We shall be able to help ()tilers more in that way to bear such national loss as we had only yesterday. (Applause;)

•_ The Chairman heartily thanked the Abbot for lils "very able words, and the manner in which he had set forth to them the purpose which was the moral if not the material founda- tion of the institution." He apnealed to the older men who did not already belong to the society to join without klelay, adding their knowledge and prudence to the innocent amusements which were there indulged in. "We shall be very glad, of course," added Father "to accommodate the fathers of other parishes—for a consideration, of course!" (Loud laughter.) He also cordially thanked the hand and all other friends who were rendering their services for the evening. (Applause.)

CAPITAL CONCERT, WHIST AND DANCE. A capital concert which followed com- menced with the orchestra selection from "Faust." Madame Sarah Silcock sang very creditably " Pleading " (Elgar) and "At My Window" (Parker). Mr. Herbert Geary scored a decided success with his violin solos " Berceuse de Jocelyn" (Goddard) and " Perpetuo Mobile" (Carl Bohm); as did also Mrs. Haviour, whose violin solo in the orchestral " Rendezvous " was much appreci- ated. The band also rendered " Intermezzo " in fine style. As in the case of Madame Silcock, Miss A. Eckersley accompanied. on the piano Mr. J. W. Bibby's splendid solos: "Ship- mates o' Mine" (Sanderson) and "Young Tom o' Devon" (Russell). The entertainment con- cluded with two rousing patriotic items from the orchestra, "Boys in Khaki, Boys in Blue" and "Tipperary." For the dancing—which continued until two a.m., and in which several hundred joined— Messrs. Henry Houghton, John Eckersley, A. Kilburn and Frank Bradshaw acted as M.C.'s. Fifteen dances were fully enjoyed before the singing of the National Anthem—which con- chided the celebration.

For the whist—which was played upstairs— Mr. 'Names Dolan made an efficient

THE KING'S BIRTHDAY.

Celebrations To Be Dispensed, With. The "London Gazette" on Tuesday night contained an announcement by the Lord Cham- berlain that by the King's commands all cele- brations of his Majesty's birthday, both at home and abroad, with the sole exception of the flying of flags, will be dispensed with this year owing to the war.

PEARLS IN YOUR MOUTH.

' No longer need our lady readers envy the dazing teeth of society ladies and actresses, for at last the secret is out, and any lady may vie with the most renowned amongst them. The regular polishing of teeth by the use of Aura Dental Paste is the means by which the striking result is secured, for a beautiful set of teeth is now to be obtained by all. An adver- tisement in our columns gives fuller particulars.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 15, 1915. LIBERAL ROLL OF HONOUR.,

Ward Workers Who Have Become King's Workers.

Mr. E. A. Sanders, Liberal Agent, has com- piled a list of ward workers and sons and brothers of ward workers who have joined the forces. The names have been divided accord- ing to the wards to which the men belong, and Mr. Sanders has inscribed them in colours on nicely illuminated cards, which will be framed and hung in the rooms either of the Liberal Club or the John Crosfield Memorial Hall. The lists are necessarily incomplete, and Mr. Sanders will be glad to receive any additions and corrections that may be found necessary. We give the lists as they are vt the present, and additions should be forwarded at the earliest possible moment, so that the rolls may be framed and hung. The roll is as follows:

WALKING DAY.

Workers' Delegates Favour Holding

Festival "As Usual."

TRADE UNION FEELING TESTED.

At their monthly meeting on Tuesday even- ing, the Warrington Trades and Labour Coun- cil discussed at considerable length the ques- tiok of Walking Day, and ultimately decided by 20 Totes to four to instruct their delegates to the pending conference on the matter to vote in favour of holding the event as usual;---on the usual day; viz., Friday, and at the usual time of the year, and not as had been suggested in other quarters, on August Bank Holiday.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT THE TRADE UNIONS THINK.

The Secretary (Mr. Chas. Dukes) reported that he had circularised the lAal branches of the various Trade 'Unions. The Basket Makers had declared themselves in favour of remain- ing at work on what was customarily set apart as Walking Day, but they thought that the children should have the usual holiday: The Bookbinders held a similar view. Warrington No. 2 Branch of the, National Union of Rail- waymen also favoured the holding of Walking Day as usual, as did the Compositors. The Painters and Decorators thought file event should be postponed until August, and the Plasterers were of opinion that if the children had their usual festivities on Saturday, July 3rd, and " walked " on the following. Sunday, Government contracts would not be upset and no time need be lost in the works.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
August _Bank Holiday As Walking Day.

The -Dock Workers' iavourecli going on as ordinarily, but the Musicians plumped for August Bank Holiday, as also did 'the Millers. The Wiredrawers saw no reason why the festi- val should not be kept as heretofore, seeing that the bulk of the large firms took stock about the time Walking Day was usually held, and the men had compulsorily to make holiday. They had no reason to believe that the tem- porary cessation of work during stock-taking was to be dispensed with this year, and they thought the holiday event could. go on as usual without impairing the output of Govern- ment stores. The Patternmakers not only thought that the event should be held as usual, but also favoured going on with the wakes week proposal. The Tramway and Vehicle Workers' secretary wrote that his members were of 'opinion that "seeing that Walking Day was the children's day, it should be celebrated in the usual way and at the usual time," and the Stove Grate Workers took a similar view. The Plumbers favoured Bank Holiday, as also did the Enginemen, the Tailors and the Postmen. The Co-op Employes suggested that Walking Day be held on a Saturday instead of a Friday. View of Majority Of Organised _Workers.

His own UniOn, tile Secretary added, thought the festival should go on as usual. The result of the census taken showed that the larger Unions took the view that the festival shauld go on as in former years, and he pkoposed to communicate that fact to the Mayor, at the same time giving his Worship some sort of idea as to the number of men holding the different views brought to light through the various Unions. 'Twelve Societies desired to Fo on as usual, seven would prefer that Walk- ing Day should be held on August Bank Holi- day rather than abandoned, and two Unions favoured it being held on the Saturday only. WILL THE GOVERNMENT APPLY PRESSURE?

The Chairman (Mr. Hamblett) wondered' whether the Government would bring pressure to bear in the direction of compelling work to go on.

- A Delegate: It looks to me as if the wire- drawers will be forced to "play ".a day or two as usual, and if that is so, the holding of Walking Day can make no difference so far as they are concerned.

WILL- THE MEN TAKE THE DAY OFF AS USUAL?

The Secretary: If the men have saved up the usual copper or two each week they will have the holiday as usual! That's what it amount to. Then we shall hear that hoarse cry again about the workers drinking and neg- lecting the interests of the nation, and all that sort of stuff and nonsense. The people who talk like that forget that you can't work a man for ever, and that men will have their usual holiday—their little "burst "—whatever other people may say about them.

A Delegate thought that by seeing to it that the children of the workers had their Walking Day festival as usual, the town would be pay- ing a compliment to the workers who were at present lining the trenches in France. (Hear, hear.) He, however, certainly favoured post- poning the event until August Bank Holiday. That, he took it, would be a general holiday, and the " walking" could then be held and enjoyed by young and old alike without the possibility of interfering with the output of war supplies. "A REAL GOOD HOLIDAY AND DONE

===== 0002601_19150515_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
WITH IT."

The Wiredrawers' Delegate said he thought the workers were more entitled to be considered than the parsons. If the workers were going to have a holiday he thought they should have a real good one and have done with it, and not keep having a series of little holidays. The Secretary said that if he thought that the holding of the holiday was going to be detrimental from the standpoint of the prosecu- tion of the war he would be against any cessa- tion of work. But he did not thtnk it would be detrimental in, that direction. Of course, a wakes week, under existing circumstances, was quite out of the question.

Mr. C. PlinstOn thought the better way would be to hold Walking Day in the usual way. The holiday was bound to be held—it was necessary not only for the children, but also for the grown-ups. He considered the week-end a better time for a public holiday than the beginning of the week. _

Another delegate.said he failed to see any reason why Walking Day should not be held as usual, for there were not a thousand men in the whole of Warrington employed on war work.

BIG MAJORITY FAVOURS FESTIVAL "AS USUAL."

On a vote being taken twenty of the delegates declared themselves in favour of holding Walk- ing Day as usual, and four in favour of post- poning it to August Bank Holiday. Messrs. Hamblett and Dukes were then appointed as delegates from the Council to at- tend the conference on the subject which the Mayor has promised to call.

SUNDAY SCHOOL UNION'S

VIEWS.

The local Free Church Council has not dis- cussed the question of the Walking Day date, but the Sunday School Union thinks it would be a wise policy to hold it as usual. The date really depends upon the works' decision as to closing or otherwise. on. the Friday. Should they decide not to do so, the Rector's suggestion that Saturday should be the day, looks as if it will have to be adopted.

I .N.A."I-I'iNDEillo MOTOR CAR.

Sunday Night Street Obstruction.

A Blackburn motorist, Fredk. Fernworth, of 13, Mount-street, in that town, was at Tuesday's Borough Police Court fined 40s. for obstructing Bridge-street, Warrington, on Sunday, May 2nd, by leaving his motor car unattended therein for an unreasonable period. Defendant did not appear, but sent a letter, in which he apologised for having left the car in the street for a matter of twenty minutes. P.C. Ballard, however, told the Court that he first saw the car outside the Lion Hotel at 6 p.m. It was then unattended, and he watched it until seven o'clock, when the defendant came out of the hotel. Witness then drew his atten- tion to the fact that the car was obstructing the roadway, leaving only just sufficient room for tramcars to pass, and diverting all the other vehicular traffic. A quarter of an hour later defendant removed the car into the hotel yard.

The Chief Constable said there were three previous convictions for motor car offences.

BEG, BORROW, OR STEAL.

"A Copper Till Dinner-Time."

A lady who, according to the character attributed to her from official sources, is enter- prisingly versatile in the way she regulates her course of action by the exigency of the moment, was in the dock at the Warrington Police Court on Monday, before the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), Mr. S. Wright, Alderman Smethurst, Alderman James Evans, Alderman Bolton and Messrs. Wm. Furness and George Pilling. The prisoner was Elizabeth Lavin, a married woman, of 34, Portland-street, and she was charged with begging in Green-street on Satur- day.

Whisky or Money.

Detective A. J. Skelton stated that at 11.5 on the morning in question he was on duty in Green-street, when he saw prisoner going from shop to shop, apparently begging. Knowing her character, he made inquiries in the shops and found that his suspicions were justified. It appeared that prisoner had been asking for "a copper till dinner-time." Witness ques- tioned her and she admitted trying to "borrow a copper." She had a habit of going from place to place asking people either for whisky or money.

When Opportunity Offered. The Chief Constable stated that there were previous convictions against prisoner, who, he added, entered various shops, &c., begging, and, if she got the chance, stealing. Prisoner was sentenced to fourteen days' im- prisonment.

WHEN IHE SLEEPER AWOKE.

Somnolent Driver \Alio Was Hard To

Arouse.

At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, George Pollitt, of 11, Industrial-street, Stock- port-road, Manchester, was summoned for hav- ing been asleep whilst in charge of a horse and cart in Marsh House-lane on April 27th.—He put in an appearance just as the magistrates were about to give their decision, and admitted that he fell asleep in the cart. • Asleep in the Bottom of His Cart.

The evidence of Acting-Sergeant Edwards was to the effect that at 1.35 a.m. on the day in question he saw a light furniture cart proceed- ing without a driver along Marsh House-lane, towards the town. He ran after the hbrse and stopped it, and then noticed the defendant fast asleep in the bottom of the cart. He tried to rouse the man, but failed to do so, and drove -him to the Police Station, where witness and two other constables by dint of much shaking managed to wake him up. He seemed to be suffering from the effects of drink. A Danger to the Mails. -

The Chief Consta-ble said the was a bad one, the man being practically drunk and ex- ceedingly fast asleep at the bottom of his cart. There was no doubt that his horse, wandering along at its own sweet will, was a source of con- siderable danger to the mail vans and other vehicles using the road in the early hours of the morning.

It was at this juncture that the defendant turned up and admitted that he did fall asleep in the cart. He added that he had tried to get a bed at several places, but without success. He denied that he had had too much drink, and said that he was simply tired. P.S. Kenwright, who helped to awaken the man at the Police Station, said he appeared to be just coming out of a drunken sleep. He was ordered to pay an inclusive penalty of 20s.

A RUNAWAY HUSBAND.

Allowed Four Weeks To Provide For

His Family.

Before Councillor H. Roberts and other magis- trates,- on Tuesday morning, William Wallace Fail, of 1, Crow-lane, Earlestown, was charged with leaving his wife and two children charge- able to the Warrington -Union. Mr. Shearer, for the Guardians, said that the Guardians had given the man some grace to see whether he would turn up and look after his wife and children, but he did not do so, and consequently • a warrant was taken out. The man was found at Earlestown, and was brought back to Warrington the previous evening. The wife and children had been in the Workhouse about thirty days. The husband was an iron moulder and earned good money.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
Denied That He Absconded.

Prisoner: In the first place, I admit that I went away; but I did not run away. I was out of work at the time, and I took the first job that was offered me, and it happened to be at Earlestown. As to there being two children, I only have one child. of my own—a boy aged eight—and I supported him up till May 4th, through my father, whom I have paid for his keep. As to the other, child, I deny that it is mine. My wife left me on May 25th of last year, and she stayed away three months. She came back, in fact, on August 23rd, and I took her into the home again, and the next day she told me that she was in a certain condition. The child was born on March Bth, as you will see, it was quite impossible for me to have been the father of it. I am quite willing to make my wife an allowance.

BEER AND BREAKAGES.

Husband Fighting—Wife Drunk.

Jane Patten, of 6, Heaton's-square, wife of Patrick Patten, who is fighting for his country, was charged at the Waieington Police Court on Wednesday, before Messrs. Hayward (in the chair), Jackson Holmes and Williams, and Alderman Tinnion, with being drunk in Pocket-street on Tuesday, and also with break- ing six panes of glass in the window of a lodging-house in the same street. P.C. Sankey gave evidence as to finding prisoner lying on her back in the road, very drunk. Elizabeth Steen, who keeps the lodging- house mentioned in the charge, spoke to eject- ing prisoner, who then broke the window, doing 7s. 6d. worth of damage. Superintendent Williamson stated that the woman was in receipt of the usual soldier's wife's separation allowance. , A fine of 6s. was imposed, making a total, with the damage, of 13s. 6d.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
A TERROR AT TWELVE.

Magistrate's Sympathy With Bo Who

Had "No Chance."

A dilapidated urchin of twelve stood weeping in the Borough Police Court on Wednesday, and wiping away the tears with his screwed-up cap. He. had stolen something; it was said that he was a very terrible youth, who led other boys into mischief. And the Bench were told all about his shiftless parents, which caused the Chairman (Mr. Hayward) to observe that the boy had had no chance. He was assigned to an industrial school.

How Parents Relieve Themselves of Responsibility. The Director of Education (Mr. J. Moore Murray) said that the maintenance of boys given into their charge under similar circumstances to those of the case under notice was becoming a serious charge on the local education authorities. Each boy cost them from seven to eight shillings a week. There was an increasing number of parents who thought this was a good way of get- ting rid of their children. He thought it was absolutely essential that this boy should be sent to an industrial school, but later on they might appeal to the magistrates to be relieved of the responsibility of the lad's maintenance. The Chairman announced that the boy would be sent to the Boys' Refuge.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
£,3 FOR las. (?). Two Men Charged With Defrauding

A Labourer.

John Tynan, of 20, Hewitt-street, Gaythorn„ was charged at the Borough Police Court on. Wednesday with obtaining, by means of a trick, the sum of 10s. from Thomas Monks, in the Central Hotel, on Friday evening. Superintendent Williamson briefly stated the facts. Monks, he said, was a labourer, and he was in Winwick-gtreet on Friday evening, when he was accosted by the prisoner and another man, who asked him if he knew a certain number in the street. They then asked him to go and have a drink. He consented, and in the Central Hotel they told him that if he would put down half a sovereign he would get £3 in return. They each made out a ticket and Monks handed over the desired half- sovereign. Leaving the hotel, they all walked down the street. The police got wind that there was something afoot, and followed the trio. The prisoner and the other man made off. The prisoner was captured, but the other man escaped, and an adjournment was asked for, so that the two men could be charged together. The adjournment was granted.

P.C. AND J.P. TOO

How Magistrate Separated Two Fighters And Arrested Them.

By arresting two brawlers, frog-marching them to the Police Station, and, after giving evidence against them, helping his colleagues on the Bench in the imposition of a suitable sentence, Alderman Tinnion has surely achieved something in the nature of a loca! record.

Noticing a sound of. commotion outside his house in Palmyra-square, on Tuesday evening, Alderman Tinnion went to the window and found that a large crowd had gathered round a couple of men who were fighting. No one seemed to show any inclination to act as arbitrator, so the worthy Alderman snatched his hat and bolted out of the house. Seizing the combatants, he separated them and demanded to know the cause of the strife. One of the pugilists replied that the fight was for possession of a shirt. They refused to listen to the counsel of the would-be peacemaker, who thereat collected them, one coat collar in each hand, and pushed them along to the Police Station, where they were charged with dis- orderly conduct and consigned to The cells. They were John Saunders and John Hollowell, spinners, of no fixed abode, and they were duly brought before the Borough Bench on Wednes- day on a charge of street-fighting. When he had given his evidence, and had explained that he took the men to the Police Station for their own good, Alderman Tinnion.

disappeared from the Twitness box and turned up on the Bench to assist in the adjudieatory part of the episode in which he had already played so prominent a part. Saunders was let off with a fine of 6s. (or coven days). brit TTollowell. who was described by Alderman Tinnion as the more troublesome of the pair, was ordered to pay 95., or go to prison for a week.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
The Retort Courteous.

A young man in a hurry went through the left side of a pair of swinging doors in the Senate wing of the Capitol at Washingt9n last session and almost knocked over t late Senator Pettus, of Albania, who was about to push through the right side. The young man apologised profusely. "I'm very sorry—l didn't know I was—l am in a great hurry." "That's all right, son," said the venerable Pettus. "But let me give you a piece of advice about going through doors like these. Always go through on the right side and turn to the right. Then, if you meet anybody coming through and bump into hint you needn't apologise. He'll be a durned fool and it won't be necessary. Good morning."

Ever notice how many friends you have when you don't need them?

There are three kinds of lies—white lies, black lies and society fibs.
