THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, MAY 15, 1915.

THE SUPER-CRIMINAL.

Germany has boasted of its super this and super that until there seemed to be few, if any, fields left to conquer; but she has within the last fortnight advanced substantially and has become herself the super-criminal of the world. No one can ever compete with her now in infamy if the earth lasts on into eternity. The horrors of Louvain, of Dinant, were glossed over—by lies we know—in con- venient phrases about punishment for offences against the invading army; the shelling of Rheims Cathedral was excused by the allega- tion that the tower had been used for mili- tary purposes; even the raid upon Scar- borough provided a morning's exercise for a fleet that was getting rusty for want of work. But the poisoning of wells, the gassing of men, and the sinking of a passenger steamer as a part of the set policy of a government and of the strategy of its naval commanders bring us face to face with a state of affairs that finally places Germany outside the pale of civilisation. When the Lusitania sank last Friday in circumstances so callous and inhuman that the wonder is that not more than 1,142 perished in a total of 1,906 on board, Germany, perjured Germany, struck harder than she knew—against herself. The Allies, strangely tolerant and strong in their belief in the justice of their cause, had

trusted that they knew the worst of the Ger- man character. By means of these two black crimes, the poison and the wholesale murder on thb high seas, Germany has not only con- vinced the Allies that she will stick at noth- ing, but will go to any diabolical length; but she has surely convinced the nations that are at present only spectators of the most ghastly war of all time that an enemy of mankind is afoot, and that unless that enemy is effec- tually scotched civilisation itself will stand in danger of annihilation.

Will the neutrals stand aside and let others fight their battle, or will they themselves lend a hand to strike for freedom? That is their own affair, of course; and appearances lead to the conclusion that we and France and Russia—with little Servia and Belgium in the foreground to show others the glorious heights to which even the smallest nations can attain—will have to trust to our own re- sources in order to win the measure of liberty which twelve months ago we fondly believed to have been firmly secured. The outrage, however, will have a tonic effect upon the peoples of the Allies, who will proceed with greater determination than ever towards the goal that means the total extermination of the Prussian military caste. That we shall have to apply ourselves with greater energy to the achievement of that great end becomes more clear as time goes/ on. It would be un- wise to console ourselves in the belief that Germany having become desperate is running amok. What Germany is doing is clearly the result of well-thought-out plans—and she dare hardly have put them into practice had she feared an approaching day of reckoning with

her opponents. There is no " need for vain speculation on such remote points as these,

and we cannot afford to waste time upon them. All we know is that the news of tile sinking of the Lusitania was, according to the "Cologne Gazette," "received by the German people with unanimous satisfaction since it proves to England and the whole world that Germany is quite in earnest in regard to her submarine warfare." The only answer to a nation in that bloodthirsty frame

of mind can come from the recruiting offices and the workshops of this country; and that answer will undoubtedly be given.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
WILL FOOTBALL BE RESUMED?

There appears to be every probability that the force of circumstances will settle the ques- tion, Should football be played during the war? So far as the Warrington Football Club is concerned, and the other clubs are in

similar positions, the question has become, Should professional football be played dur- ing the war? A glance at the past season's returns will show that it is doubtful whether play by paid players will, or can, be carried on any longer. At the annual meeting of the members of the Warrington Club, to be held next week, the Hos. TREASURER (Mr. THOMAS PEMBERTON) will report that "the season has been full of anxious moments for the com- mittee, and they feel that it would be disas- trous to continue football on professional lines during the course of the war." The net " gate " of the season was £972, as compared with £2,527 in the previous season, and, although various economies—loyally joined in by the players who voluntarily gavemp fifteen per cent. of their pay—materially reduced the expenditure, there was still a loss of £521 on the year's working, reducing the club's credit balance from £2,316 to £1,795. If the war continues, next season will be worse than last, and prudence, as well as other weighty con- siderations, suggests that there will have to be substantial alterations in our peace time , methods. The abandonment of professional- ism until brighter and happier days would appear to provide the temporary solution needed. We must have recreation as well as work for our war workers, and there can be no carping at, but only the greatest satisfac- tion with, the playing of a game that keeps men " fit " in body and mind. • The financial side of the question is not the sole determining factor in this matter. Mr. T. P. BRADSHAW, the hon. secretary of the club, provides in his annual report ample material to prove that in .spite of the spin- blame of carrying ,on football "as usual," the actualities have fallen far short of appear- ances. As football spectators have left foot- ball behind them and rushed in thousands to the Front, so have the players gone in large proportion to fight battles on the larger field. No fewer than 25 playing members have joined the colours since war broke out, and on many occasions the committee have had only just sufficient men at their disposal to make up a team. This was one of the reasons, we pre- sume, why the Combination fixtures collapsed. No works competition was held in order that working men might not be attracted from their more serious occupations in the week- day evdnings. In the League table Warring- ton occupies its accustomed place about half- way down, while the team reached the semi- final of the Lancashire Cup and the second round of the N.U. Cup, in depressing circum- stances a very fair record, which was jointly contributed to by the loyalty of the players and the energy of the committee, Mr. BRAD- SHAW, for the second season in succession, hav- ing attended ,every meeting called.

WARRINGTON MEN "GASSED.'

Two Victims Of German Gas Bombs In Whitecross Hospital.

Two of the patients from the firing line ad- mitted on Wednesday into the new Whitecross Hospital at Warrington are local men; both have been "gassed" by the chemical fumes from the German "stink bombs." One of the men is Private F. Williams, of the 2nd Lanes- sihre Fusiliers, whose home is at 36, Dickenson- street. It is interesting to learn that Private Williams originally helped in. carting building materials to the site of the hospital when it was in process of erection. The other local patient is Corporal J. Johnson, of the Duke of Welling- ton's West Riding Regiment. His home is at 72, Priestley-street. One of the men brought in on Wednesday afternoon has eleven wounds, an- other eight and a third seven. It is expected that at the Whitecross Hospital there will be an average of 200 patients. There will be a large field here for the supply of com- forts, and Mr. A. Bottomley, Bewsey Chambers, will be delighted to hear from anyone willing to supply 'cigarettes or make other contributions for the benefit of these men, who have suffered while doing. their share in their country's cause.

THE LUSITANIA.

Warrington Woman's Relative Escapes - Through A Ventilator.

John 3ScStay, a nephew of Mrs. T. Robinson, of Dallam-lane, was on the Lusitania when she received her death-blow. He had a thril- ling experience before he succeeded in escap- ing from the doomed ship and swimming into comparative safety. Mr. 31eStay, who was a steward on the lll- fated vessel, was down below when the blow came, and had to break through a door and crawl on his bands and knees through a venti- lator in order to reach the deck. The full story is not to hand, but it was an immense relief to his relatives to receive the message by wire: "Safe and sound at Queens- town.—JOHN MCSTAY."

Other T.usitania matter appears on Page 6. LOCAL LUSITANtA LETTERS.

This week Mrs. W. J. Parr—it 61, Manches- ter-road, Warrington—has received safely two letters from her son, Mr. Wm. Parr, of Boston, U.S.A., which were rescued from the Lusitania. They are in perfect condition.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMAN INFAMY—continued.

epidemic of cholera were decimating Europe and threatening to extend its ravages across the Atlantic, Russia and France, Belgium and Serbia would be united as they are united to- day with the British Empire in a common effort to defend humanity against so fearful a foe. But they would not act alone. The other nations of Europe would join forces with them, and so would the great Republic of the West. It is no wonder that the intense indignation with which Prussia's reckless and ruthless applica- tion of her vile gospel of "blood and iron" is regarded in the United States of America, as also among the neutral States of Europe, is leading more than one of these, nations to ask how long their neutrality can be preserved; how long it will be possible for any part of the civil- ised world to continue to tolerate conduct so utterly intolerable. It is not merely that Ger- many is tearing up one after another the most important of the Hague Conventions which she undertook to defend; her shameless infamy threatens the most elementary principles of self- preservation, which are the very foundations of our common civilisation. It is for that reason that we are entitled, in Burke's phrase, to "impeach the common enemy of mankind."— Yours faithfully, ARTHUR CROSFIRLD: 41,. West-hill, Highgate, N., May 9, 1915.

THE EI .ER, SATURDAY, MAY 15, 1915.

GERMAN INFAMY.

Mr. Crosfield ,011 a Nation's Indelible Disgrace.

COLD-BLOODED BARBARITY.

To the Editor of " The Examiner."

Sir,—l enclose you extracts from a letter I wrote to my brother, Major Crostield, on April 29thp and shall be specially glad if you have space for them in The Examiner," as they include a message to our Warrin74 on troops at the Front. I have now, heard i ..,m Major Crosfield that he has had opportunities of conveying this message to some of those at the Front, and I am hoping that through the medium of Saturday's " Examiner " it may be conveyed to them all.

Well, since I wrote on April 29th, more in- famous history has been written by Germany— history which the descendants of the miscreants responsible for it will look back upon with shame and 'loathing and horror. Terrible in- deed is the guilty record of a country of which Lord Bryce has just said that : "International law has within the last ten months been more completely disregarded, cast down, trampled under foot than I think itiever was during the last four or five centuries." We have in to-day's papers a fearful exposure of German outrages, among the signatures to which we have the names of men like Lord Bryce, Sir ..'clward Clarke, Sir. Frederick Pollack and Mr. Harold Cox. The Committee which has investigated these outrages has been formed of men of highly trained and judicial mind, capable of weighing all evidence impartially, and. its Chairman; Lord Bryce, is a man of world-wide reputation, and held in universal esteem.

Apparently all these calculated, cold-blooded, infamous barbarities are intended to terrorise people to-day as the Germans tried to terrorise Belgium and France 'in the first weeks of the war. We have only to look at the recruiting records to realise how utterly Germany fails to achieve any such object in Britain; and so we have here yet another fatal blunder on her part, added to all the colossal miscalculations which have gone-before, and which collectively are involving the Huns in their coming doom. As outrage is piled upon outrage, on both sides of the Atlantic indignation and intense feeling rises higher and higher. Where that finds expression .in the looting of shops such as we have had here in London within the last twenty-four hours, or in attacks upon isolated individuals, which may often be utterly unjust—as, for instance, where a British citizen of German name was bitterly attacked the other day until it was discovered that he had just lost a sou fighting in the trenches for the Allies at the Front, while two other sons were also serving with our Army— all one can saY is that such incidents are to be strongly deprecated and greatly regretted. We do not want to begin competing with the Huns in their barbarities; but, on the ether hand, these foul outrages for which Germany is responsible unite us with our Allies in an absolutely inflexible resolution to crush Prus- sian militarism and the vile gospel of Blood and Iron with which Prussia, and, unhappily, to a large extent, Germany, has become identi- fied.—Yours, &c.,

ARTHUR CR OSFIELD. 41, West-hill, Highgate, N., May ;13th, 1915.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
[Extracts from Letter to Major Crosfield, 29.4.15.]

A TERRIBLE DAY OF RECKONING.

One would not feel any surprise at the cir- cumspect, methodical and scientific German having protected himself on these western lines by every device known to modern war- fare; but how these scoundrels are covering themselves—and even, one must add the entire German nation, which utterly fails to con- demn such practices—witti.infamy and stain- ing the whole German record with indelible disgrace. This infamous use of asphyxiating gas near Ypres, their reckless and equally shameful and outrageous work on the high seas, and last, but in its effect on popular, opinion, not least, the calculated, cold-blooded barbarity of their treatment of British prisoners, is all piling up an overwhelming indictment and a terrible day of reckoning for them when the time comes for reparation. Never in our day have we seen, and -never again, I am sure, shall we see; such intensity of feeling in every fibre of its being uniting the entire nation in a spirit of unalterable con- viction and immovable resolution. Assuredly no sacrifice will be shirked and no effort spared by those of us in the old country whose hearts are stirred as never before by the invincible fortitude, the grim determination and supreme heroism with which our kith and kin at the Front are, thanks to their dash, daring, gal- lantry and courage, breaking the entire records of history.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
DEFEKDING THE TITLE-DEEDS OF CIVILISATION.

Turning to the efforts of our manufacturers, skilled artisans, and labourers, and of the entire civilian population, and as regards ex- penditure from the public purse on a scale without parallel, you all know happily as well as Ido that nothing will be spared here. Be- cause everyone of us is feeling—and one can- not doubt that the same conviction is spread- ing with tremendous rapidity alike amongst the greater and the smaller of the neutral States—that you are not fighting alone for the restitution of her soil and such reparation and compensation as it may still be possible to make to Belgium, and to a very large extent to bthers among our Allies; not alone for the safety and security and free Government of Old England; not alone even for the\ glory and the very existence of our world-wide Empire; you are fighting to defend the very title-deeds of civilisation itself, and with the sternest re- solve to crush and punish those who have sought to tear them up. If I was privileged- at this moment to meet one's fellow-townsmen at the Front, that would be my message to them. But as lam prevented giving it my- self, if by chance at any time you have the opportunity and leisure to convey the message to our Warrington Battalion, I should be truly grateful.

The following letter appeared in Monday's "Westminster Gazette":—

THE ENEMY OF THE HUMAN RACE.

To the Editor of the "Westminster Gazette."

Sir,—ln your admirable leading article of yes- terday there are just two lines with -reference to which I venture to suggest a correction. Allud- ing to the "long chain of cruelty and horror" which is now the "concern of the whole world," you say Germany on these lines is rapidly be- coming " hostis humani generis," the enemy of the human race. She is that already. From the time of the infamous atrocities committed in Belgium and France in the first stages of the war; from the barbarous and disgusting grossieretes for which Germans were responsible in their treatment of French and Russians striv- ing to leave the country in the days preceding the war; throughout the history of the last nine months, and the last few weeks, in which we have witnessed the infamous use of and the torture of our troops by poisonous gases, the poisoning of wells in Smith Africa, and now the culminating crime off the coast of Ireland— through it all Germany has exhibited on a scale without precedent or parallel in history what Lord Macaulay has described as the most fright- kul of all spectacles—" the strength of civilisa, tion without its mercy." We are fighting what was not only a great Power, but a highly civil- ised Power, and yet that Power is now striving to destroy the very title deeds of civilisation itself. Such insane 6riminality is, as you well say, the "concern of the whole world." If an

LOCAL INTELLIGENCE.

Local Cricket Clubs Get To Work.

The Warrington Thursday Cricket Club played their opening fixture of the season on their ground at Arpley on Thursday afternoon, when they had Chester College as their oppo- nents. A close game resulted in the visitors NV inning by two runs, the scores being :—Col- legians, 48; Thursdayites, 46. On Saturday the Cairo-street Unitarians met Wycliffe, the match ending in a win for the Unitarians by 26 runs. Cricket club secretaries will greatly. oblige by sending us reports of their matches as soon as possible after the games.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
To Insured Persons.

Owing to many of the local practitioners having consented to assist the military authcri- ties in the treatment of, wounded soldiers, shortly to be opened at Winwick, it will be necessary to alter and probably .curtail the hours at which insured persons should attend at panel doctors' surgeries, such alteration being displayed at the surgeries. Messages for visits should, if possible, be sent to the surgeries not later than 9 a.m.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Free Chuyeh Intercession.

The monthly meting (under the auspices of the Warrington Free ' Church Council) for intercession and prayer -during the war, was held on Thursday evening in the Bold-street Wesleyan Church Parlour. There was a marked improvement in the attendance. Mr. J. C. Holland presided, and a most helpful address was given by Rev. C. P. Pitt, Elmwood- avenue Congregational Cluirch. The Rev. W. T. Derry and others took part in the meeting.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
Baptist Sunday- School Anniversary.

The anniversary of the Sunday school in connection with the Golborne-street Baptist Church will be celebrated to-morrow, when the special preacher will be the Rev. E. E. Lovell. of Lymm, who will occupy the pulpit both in the morning and the evening. In the after- noon a special united service will be held, when Mr. Nelson will take the chair, and the speaker will be Mr. Arthur Mounfield. Special hymns and other music till be sung by the choir and the children of the Sunday school.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
Typewriting Successes.

The following students of Wood's College, 4b, Bold-street, have been successful in securing their typewriting certificates at an examination held under the auspices of the National Short- hand Association, London :—Marion Twiss, May Heath, Eva Muskett, Marjorie Barrington, Dora Epps. Catherine Luttle, Ethel Births, Helen Wallington, Cissie Charnock, Marion Lowe, Eva Wood, William Hughes, Mabel Isaac, Florence Malkin, Jean Bunten, Annie Chantler, Gertrude Bradley, Winifred Bennett, John Bennett (age 12).

"Remember the Days." The foregoing formed the title of an interest- ing and helpful address given by Mr. George Smiley to the members of the Wycliffe P.S.A. Brotherhood on Sunday afternoon; the Secre- tary (Mr. B. Farrar) being in the chair. The days to be remembered, the speaker stated, were birthdays, days of favour, the days of the new birth, and the day of judgment. Their- days on earth were but few, and so ought they to num- ber them that they might apply their hearts unto wisdom. Mr. A. Heatley and Mr. Payton also took part in the'meeting. An open meeting will be held in connection with the P.S.A. to-morrow, when Mr. E. Lloyd Jones will be the speaker, and Madame Maud Loveless (soprano) the poloist.

Cable Company's War Workers.

In recognition of the great work done in mak- ing comforts for the troops by the ladies em- ployed by the St. Helens Cable and Rubber Co., Ltd., over 100 were entertained by the firm at the Warrington Hippodrome on Saturday. Prizes of handbags were also awarded as follows to those ladies who had done the most work in the various departments

Typists' Department: 1, Miss Eleanor Wright; 2, Miss Hilda Hill. • ' ' Cable and Cotton Covering Department: 1, Miss Maria Bailey; 2, Miss Hannah Parr. Rubber Department: 1, Miss Nellie Plumb; 2, Miss Rose Bradshaw.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
Schoolboy's Death.

The death of James Rowson (8), sun of Wm. (general labourer) and Mary Rowson, of 6, Hale-street, was investigated at the Coroner's Court on Monday afternoon, by Mr. F. A. Jones (deputy-coroner). It appeared that on the previous Tuesday afternOon the boy had complained of being knocked down in the play- ground at St. Ann's School by Reginald Tasker (101, West-street), whom Harry Hard- man (aged 12, 51, Forster-street) was trying to catch. The fall was accidental; his face and mouth were bleeding, and were bathed. He died the next morning.—Dr. McKee deposed that death was due to acute pneumpnia in the right lung. The fall had nothing to do with it.—A verdict of "Death from natural causes" was returned.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
"Raising the Wind."

"The figures don't come up to anything like those which represented the total last year" said an official of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, at the monthly meeting of that body on Tuesday evening, in reporting upon the result of the annual draw. "But," he added, "seeing the abnormal times we are passing through, the result must be regarded as quite satisfactory." The receipts from the sale of tickets totalled £l4 is. 9d., and sub- scriptions came to £l. Prizes donated were of the value of El 6s. 51., making the total on the receipts side of the balance sheet, with other small items, up to £l6 15s. 3d. After the expenses had been met there was a balance of £8 14s. 11d, to be devoted to the funds of the Council.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
Concerts for the Womded at the Infirmary.

The usual Sunday afternoon concert arranged by Mr. W. B. Randall (organist), was given to the wounded soldiers on Sunday afternoon, the following artistes taking part: Miss Ella Royston (elocutionist), Miss Emily Taylor (soprano), Miss Irene Harrison and Miss Day (pianoforte duets) and Mr. W. H. Rigby (baritone). All the items were exceedingly well rendered, the artistes having to respond each time. Mr. Randall played the accompani- ments with taste and skill.

One of the best Thursday' evening concerts was given on Thursday, arrangements having been made by Mr. W. B. Randall, with the courtesy of Mr. Jackson, the manager of the Hippodrome, for some of the artistes appearing at the Hippodrome this week to entertain the soldiers.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
Workingmen Help for Infirmary.

A well-attended meeting of the Working- men's Infirmary Committee was held at the Druid's Hall on Thursday evening, Mr. George Walker in the chair. It was decided to hold the annual concert on behalf of the institution on the first Saturday in October.

It was also decided to endeavour to arrange for an open-air musical festival on some Sun- day afternoon about the middle of August. Mr. R. Cook was appointed a representative of the committee on the Board of Management, in the place of Mr. Fred Taylor, who was unable to serve in consequence of pressure of business.

The Chairman made a sfrong appeal for in- creased contributions in aid of the institution, in consequence of the extra expenses entailed in the working of the institution. Mr. T. C. Wadsworth, a representative on the House Visitors and Finance Committee of the Institution, reported on the excellent manner in which the patients are treated, and stated that the institution is managed on the most economical lines consistent with efficiency.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
Belated "May Day."

At a meeting of the Executive Committee of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, it was decided to postpone the customary May Day demonstration until to-day (Saturday).

===== 0002601_19150515_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
Savings Bank Trustees' Visit.

Warrington was favoured by a visit on Thurs- day from the Council of the Trustee Savings Banks Association, who held a meeting at the Warrington Savings Bank. Prior to the busi- ness meeting the members of the Council were entertained to lunch at, the Patten Arms by the trustees of the Warrington bank, Mr. C. J. Holmes presiding over a large and influential gathering.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
St. tarnabas' Sale of Work.

The annual sale of work in connection with St. Barnabas' Church, will be held in the Patten Hall, Winmarleigh-street, on Wednes- day and Thursday next, the proceeds being in aid of the church extension and other parochial funds. Mrs. Parr has kindly consented to open the sale on the first day, whilst Mrs. Segar Owen will perform the ceremony on the second day.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
Open-air. Recruiting Meeting.

A• recruiting meeting was held on Thursday evening .on the spare ground behind the Blue Coat School. The speakers were Lieutenant Williamson, and Lieutenant Burgess, of Tun- bridge Wells, both of whom made stirring appeals for recruits. Lieutenant Burgess also referred to the rumour which was current, that the soldiers at Tunbridge' were ill-fed. That, he said, was completely untrue. The band of the 2/4th South Lancs. Regiment played selections during the evening. Last night, in Victoria Park, a large audi- ence listened to stirring recruiting appeals from Alderman Tinnion and Lieutenant Wil- liamson, on behalf of the 3/ 4th South Lanca- shires—for which recruits are urgently needed. Reference was made to the favourable oppor- tunities and splendid prospects of those • now joining.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
HOUSE FAMINE

Possible In arrington In The

===== 0002601_19150515_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
Near Future.

HOW MAY BE AVOIDED.

Warrington was much in evidence at the Housing and Town 'Planning Conference held at the • School of Civic Design in Liverpool on Wednesday and Thursday. The Borough Street Improvement Committee Was represented by Alderman • Bennett (chairman), Councillors Gough and Pemberton, the Borough Surveyor (Mr. A. M. Ker) and the Deputy-Borough Sur- veyor (Mr. A. M. Piercey). The Rural District Council was represented by Mr. William Fur- ness (chairman), Mr. George Mather (deputy- chairman), and )Ir. B. S. Newcombe (surveyor). Where Unity of Design Is Desir-!ale.

In the course Of a discussion on \;".:(lnesday as to the Rinslip-Northwood Scheme, which has now been officially sanctioned, Alderman Bennett commented favourably upon a clause which enabled the authority con- cerned to exercise control over the character of the buildings erected in at.; particular area, and said that it was exceetlittgly desirable, on the one hand, to aim at a general unity of design in. any particular street—instancing Buttermarket-street as a case in point—and, on the other hand, to avoid the hideous and un- relieved monotony which was so characteristic of the residential streets in all the northern towns.

Difficulties Which Beset the Builder.

On Thursday Councillors Gough and Pember- ton called attention to the various difficulties which beset the builders, Councillor Pemberton giving some striking figures as to the practical cessation of building in Warrington and plainly intimating that unless drastic steps were taken there was a serious danger of a house famine in the early future. lie also quoted some pointed figures showing the way in which the builder was taxed and handicapped, a matter which Mr. W. Furness had previously touched upon.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
Not a Matter of Party Politics.

It was suggested that questions of this kind were outside the purview of the conference, as they verged on party politics, but Alderman Bbnnett said that when .a Parliamentary Bill liecame an Act it ceased to be a matter of party politics, and if it could be shown that any law, however well intended, had the effect which Councillor Pemberton had indicated, it was such an important factor in the honsing problem that he thought the conference ought to tackle it. They were not there as politi- cians, but as municipal administrators, and he hoped they. would banish the noxious germ of party politics from their minds—especially at such a. critical time as the present—and face these problems boldly with a serious desire to solve them. Moreover, there were certain mat- ters which had nothing to do with party poli- tics, and on which the conference might well express an opinion.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
Some of the Heavy Handicaps.

He referred, particularly, to the Leavy han- dicap placed upon the builders by stamp duties and law costs, and to the present inequitable) mode by which municipalities raised their re- venue. It had been pointed out that the rates accounted for no less than 40 per cent. of the rent of the average house, and he was strongly of opinion that rates should he abolished and that a municipal income-tax should take their place. Whilst, personally, he was a supporter of municipal building and a warm believer in public utility societies, he did not think it was quite fair to the builders—whose principal diffi- culty was a want of capital—that they should be able to borrow from the Government on such favourable terms, and suggested that similar facilities might be offered, under proper safe- guards, to the private investor. The Council promised to give the various sug- gestions which had been put forward their most careful consideration, and the delegates were invited to communicate specific instances of hardship to the secretary. Conference's Suggestions to the Government,

As a result of the discussion a resolution— ;moved by. Mr. Bryce Leicester (of Liverpool) and seconded by Mr. G. Mather (of Warring- ton)—was carried, -setting forth that the confer- ence realised that unless timely attention be given to the matter there was every prospect of the country shortly being faced with so serious a house famine as to menace the industrial effi- ciency of the whole nation. The conference therefore urged the Government at once to. for- mulate the terms and conditions on which it would, at the conclusion of the war, be pre- pared under the }lousing (No. 2) Act, 1914, to assist financially and to encourage housing schemes undertaken by municipalities, public utility associations, or private enterprise, and to state what legislation, if any, it was prepared to introduce to remove any existing obstacles to cottage building. The conference further em- phasised its belief that such action by the Gov- ernment was the only way of averting the diffi- culties of the situation and of encouraging all parties interested in the problem immediately to formulate building schemes in timely antici- pation of the first opportunity when the same might be put into operation, and by those means avoid the crisis now threatened.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Some Helpful Designs.

A set of designs prepared by Mr. ker and based upon actual local conditions was ex- hibited and excited much attention. They clearly proved that under a proper scheme it was possible to build twenty houses to the acre, at about the same price per house as it cost under present conditions to build 36 houses to the acre, the saving being affected by the re- arrangement of the streets, the abolition of back passages and an improved method of paving, with great advantage to the health of the inhabitants and the amenities of the dis- trict.

hear about me being wounded, but don't bother

yourselves, as I am- going on fine. The doctor told me this morning that he would have me out in a very short time, as the bullet had gone through the fleshy part of my leg. We were in the reserve trenches last Wednesday, after being three days in the firing line, when the order came down for rifle inspection at six o'clock. I had just cleaned mine, and was talking to a chap named Gayter, when all ,A a sudden I heard a rifle go off, and Gayter shouted 'l'm hit.' I watched him hop about ten yards, and then I noticed the blood com- ing out of the bottom of my pants, and I shouted I am hit.' The lads ran to see me, and dressed my wound. I went through more pain during the two hours I lay in the trenches than I have gone through since, and I am suffering now, I can assure you. 'Dave' Clayton helped to carry me out of the trenches to the dressing station. Sammy Watson helped to dress my wounds, and gave me a drink of tea to buck me up a bit. I have been treated champion in the hospitals, especially this one. We are getting the best of food and a good bed to lie on. It ,beats all the trenches."

===== 0002601_19150515_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
A Keen Follower Of Football.

Private Gater has many friends-in town, with whom he is exceedingly popular on ageount of his jolly nature. He is very keen on football, which he played as a youth, and he is among Warrington's most ardent supporters, never missing a match when his work permitted. Another member of the family is in the Army —Colour-Sergeant Enoch Gater, who came home from India to go to the Front, but who was, instead, drafted to a big training camp on the East Coast, in order to act as drill and musketry instructor to recruits.

An Earlier Escape.

A narrow escape ivhich Private Minshull had from a bullet was described in an earlier letter:—" We have had an awful time this last fortnight, what with shells hitting our billet and others dropping within a few yards from us. The nearest I have been of being hit was last Tuesday night. We were out trench- digging, and just as the order was passed down to fall in and march back to our billets, a bullet hit my cap."

Six Years a Volunteer.

Private Min.shull, who is 24 years of age, is unmarried, and has been in the Territorials for about six years. After leaving lleathside School, he worked for Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., where he was employed in the tin department at the outbreak of war. He has a brother, Sergeant J. Minshull, with the 2/4th Battalion at Tunbridge Wells

PRIVATE WILLIAM CATER.

Writes That Ile Is "Finishing His

Education " At Oxford.

Private William Gater, who figures in the list of wounded, having received a bullet- wound in the left lower leg on May sth, is only 22 years of age. He is a single man and Makes his:home with his parents at 34, Erwood- street. He was educated at the Wycliffe Day School, and ever since leaving worked at the Bewsey Forge until the call for volunteers came and he offered himself for active service on August 31st last. His father, Mr. Elijah Gater, is also employed at the Bewsey Forge. "Got A Nasty Shock" From "A Very Stray

===== 0002601_19150515_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
Bullet."

Private Gater now lies in hospital at Ox- ford, from which place he humorously wrote home that he was "finishing his education." In a previous letter he described, how he got wounded as follows :—"I got a nasty shock last Thursday evening. I got in the way of a very, very stray bullet, and it jolly soon shifted me, I can tell you, as that said bullet happened to be going at a speed of about two

PRIVATE GATER. Wounded.

thousand yards a second. I can assure you that it did not stay to be reckoned with, but took a bit of my leg, and now I am out of action for a while." In another letter 'he said: "You'll be wondering what part of me is missing when you learn that I have been wounded. Well, a bullet has taken part of my leg." On a field post-card he conveyed the intimation that he hoped to be discharged soon.

THE EXAIINER. S ATURDAY, MAY 15, 1915.

—Privates Joseph and William—whilst a third brother—Private John—is training with the Territorial Reserve Battalion in the South, and is looking forward to being shortly drafted to France. Private Smith has taken little in- terest in sport, but was enthusiastic about his Territorial work, to which he devoted most of his spare time. He is spoken of as a good soldier, a good workman 'and .a good fellow, and he is popular among a wide circle of acquaintances, several of whom have sent him little tokens of their friendship since be went on active service.

Pathetic interest attaches to two birthday cards which the mother-in-law carefully. pre- serves under a glass case, and which were sent by the gallant private to his wife shortly before her death, and whilst he was quartered at Tunbridge Wells. By the way, his wife was only 24 when she died, and it was a great com- fort to her husband that he was able to attend her funeral. He was last in Warrington shortly before being drafted to France, when he came home on a few days' leave, together with other members of B Company, to whicli he belongs.

PRIVATE F. S. PERCIVAL.

Unexpected News Of Former Cadet's

Misfortune.

Until the third official casualty list reached Warrington On Thursday afternoon, no news of Private Frederick Samuel Percival s wounding had been received by his parents at 81, Priestley-street. The particulars given in the list showed that the gallant Territorial received a bullet wound in the head on Mak 9th. •

So recently as Wednesday morning a letter arrived from him, written on the previous Saturday. He was then quite safe. Writing home on April 25th, the Private remarked :

" I shall have my eighteenth birthday in the firing line! The trenches where we were are very comfortable to what they have been. Where we had to stand knee-deep in mud and water a few weeks back is now quite dry; the weather is fine. We have paid the Huns a bit of what we owe them. We caught a working party in front of their trendes, and I give you my word we scattered a Mv before they got back to the safety of their own trench! . . ." Further news is anxiously awaited.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
Wounded Man's Career.

The son of Mr. and Mrs. Henry Shaw Percival, the .Private was born in Cartwright- street, Warrington, eighteen years ago; and was educated at St. Barnabas' School, Under Mr. Turner's headmastership. Leaving at the age of thirteen, he proceeded as a wirewiuder to Bewsey Ropery—where he remained two years. He then transferred to Whitecross. where he remained until returning to Bewsey Ropery just a week before enlisting. , It was in August that he decided to don the uniform of the 1/4th South Lancashires, in response to the appeal .for three hundred men for foreign service. After six months' training at Warrington, Dunfermline and Tunbridge Wells, he, at the end of January, crossed over to the war zone—after three days' fullough at home. For two years private Percival was a mem- ber of the Latchford Cadets .(under Mr. J. B. Starkey)—with whom he was very popular.. He attended many camps, and became proficient in

===== 0002601_19150515_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE F. S. PERCIVAL. Wounded.

signalling and many other practices in- valuable to the soldier. His father is employed in Messrs. CroAeld's glycerine department.

PRIVATE FRED SNIITII.

Lost Els Wife Whilst Undergoing War Training.

Private Fred Smith, of the Warrington Terri- torials, has had two war-time ekperiences that he can never forget. One was that shortly after he went to Tunbridge Wells to complete his training his wife was taken ill and died, and the other is that on the 7th of May he received a bullet-wound in the neck, Whilst engaged in the trenches.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0135.txt | included=true | included =====
An Enthusiastic "Terrier."

Private Smith is 26 years of age and he re- ceived his education at the Wycliffe Day School. 'After leaving sett( ol he went to work for Messrs. Pearson and Knowles at the Dallam Forge, and he remained in the employ cf that firm up to the time of leaving Warrington with his bat- talion. When the call for men was put before the Territorials he was among the first to re- spond and Ji went up to Scotland for the first part of his war training, the 'finishing touches to which were applied at Tunbridge Wells. lie' is an old and enthusiastic citizen-soldier; und after he .had completed his first four years in the Territorials he signed on for a further term and he was fulfilling that when he offered for active service.

A Motherless Child. After the death of his wife at the end of la,t

PRIVATE SMITH. Wounded.

November, Private Smith broke up his home, which was at 8, Annie-street, and his little child went to live with his mother-in-law, Mrs. Booth, just off Kerfoot-street, Winwick-road. He had been married barely two years when he became a widower, and the child of the marriage is still being cared for by Mrs. Booth, who is passionately fond of it and delights to dilate on its many precocious little ways.

PRIVATE 11. MILLER.

Billie! That Came Throtuth The

Sandbags.

Private .liarry Miller, of the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment, has been wounded. The news was received during the week-end by .his mother, Mrs. Miller, at her home, 52,

Slater-street, Latchford. Major C. F. Carey, of A Company (Private Miller's unit) wrote : I regret to have to inform you that your son, Private IL Miller, was wounded to- day (Mays 2nd). I have not seen him, as he is in another part of the trenches, but from a message I 'trot, it would appear that his life is .not in danger. •

By a subsequent post, Mrs. Miller had the following letter from her son :—'` I have been slightly wounded. There is no need for you to upset yourself in any way whatever, for I shall be all right again in a very short time. I am at present in hospital, not very far from where our battalion is stationed. I went into the trenches last Saturday, May Ist, and at

PRIVATE H. MILLER Wounded.

5.15 p.m. the following day a bullet came through the sandbags, striking me in the back and then glancing off over my shou!der."

A Great Cyclist.

Private Miller is 25 years of age. He has been in the Territorials for seven years, and has always displayed marked interest in the work. His principal hobby is cycling, and belonged to one of the regimental cycle corps that used to exist. In company with a friend, he has on a number :of oe,..asims ridden grcat distances in one day. He.was fleeted with All Saints' Mission Sunday School and Bible Class, and with the choir. He also attended, in recent times, St. Hilda's Mission, Latchford. He was educated at the Heathside School, and, on leaving there, was engaged by Messrs. J. Cros- field • and Sons, Ltd., working in the milling department. At one time a member of the football team connected with the works, he holds a medal won in a competition the club took part in.. He used te play also for the All Saints' A.F.C.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
AVortliily Follows Example Of His _Ancestors.

I expect you will be seeing my name among the casualty list, but it is nothing to worry about—as I only got a slight graze in the arm, and will only be on sick leave for about a couple of days. . For the last day or two we have had to stop in the fields, as the Germans have shelled us out of our billets. We go back there at night-time, after they have finished shelling. . •

So wrote Private Norman Wilson, a week on Tuesday, to his mother at 6, Selby-street, off Caitwright-street—breaking to her the news that he was amongst casualties. No further news reached the town until Thursday, when the official list. contained his name.

Just before. being wounded, the Private had written to say— -- " I have not told you yet that I . have been officer's servant to Captain month. .. . We get plenty of good 'grub,' so you see I am not doing so bad!"

===== 0002601_19150515_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
Fighting Ancestors.

, The familiar little red paper shield in the Window of 6, Selby-street, bears the information: "This house has sent three soldiers." Such is the case; for the wounded Private has two brothers (Alf. and Fred) respectively in the 2/4th South Lancashires—now in the South— and in the Royal Field Artillery, also in train- ing. The one was called up, and the other enlisted in August. . But the family's military record does not end at that, for Private Wilson's grandfather served forty years in the Army as Bugle-Major Whitelegg; whilst his grandmother was Nurse Wilson, under Florence Nightingale. Her first husband also lost his life in the Crimea, and in her day she was the only lady in Pontefract to draw the Army pension!

===== 0002601_19150515_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
Wounded Private's Life.

Born in this town nineteen years ago, Private Wilson until 1909, attended St. Barnabas' School, under the headmastership of Mr. Turner. He then went as a cooper to Messrs. Crosfield's, and passed through the caustic department—in which he had served five years when enlisting twelve months ago in the Territorials. At the outbreak of hostilities he happened to have been in camp two days, and from there he entered into training at Dun- fermline and Tunbridge Wells. In February he crossed to the Front.

For two years Private Wilson has bel .nge.l to an amateur rugby football club in this dis- trict, taking in it a great interest. He is known at St. Barnabas' Sunday School. Also he has attended if eathside Evening School, under Mr. T. Edden's headmastership.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
PRI k E 11,BERT lATHER.

The Second Casualty In The Same Family.

Private Albert Mather, whose home is at 369, Winwick-road, is suffering from a bullet- wound in both thighs, received on May 2nd.

He is the son of Mi.. and Mrs. Joseph 3father, his father being an old and esteemed employe of Messrs. Thewlis and Griffiths, of Mersey- street. His brotherl Private Ernest Mather, who also served in the Territorials, was killed on Mardi 10th.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
Four Years A Territorial.

Private Albert Mather is twenty-one years of age and he has been in the Territorials for four years. In a letter received on Sunday morning he wrote that he received his wound whilst taking food into the trenches. He was very keen on his work in the Territorials, and he was an excellent shot, having won a number of money- prizes on the rifle range, as well as forming one of a squad that for twelve months held the battalion silver challenge cup for squad firing. He was educated at the Ragged School, which he attended at the, same time as the brother who was recently killed. Up to the outbreak of war he was employed at Messrs. Peter Walker and Son's Brewery.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
COitHilt 'II. J. V. TAILOR.

News was received from the Front on .Tues- day afternoon, by Mrs. Elizabeth Taylor, of 32, Crosfield-street, that her eldest son, Corporal John Victor Taylor, of B Company, 4th Batt. -South Lancashire Regiment, had been wounded. The field card which conveyed the intimation, stated nothing as to the nature of the wound, but said that he had been sent to the base hospital, and was "going on well.' Although only connected with Warrington for about eight or nine years, he is very well known. Educated at the Liverpool Grammar School, he went to work, upon the family deciding to come to Warrington to reside, at Messrs. N. Green- ing and Sons, Ltd., wire manufacturers, Bewseyload. Corporal Taylor has been in the battalion for about five years, and on the' out- break of war, he was among the first to volun- teer for active service. He did his war train- ing in Scotland and Tunbridge Wells. He is very popular among the members of the Conservative Club, of which he is a mem- ber. He is a brother of Miss S. Taylor, of the Conservative Association Offices, Sankey-street.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
Smoking and Joking When Wounded.

Private A. Williams, of Latchford, a comrade of Private Miller's, and an. ex-workmate from Crosfield's. wrote to Mrs. Miller, telling of how his chum fared after being hit. 'it occurred," he says, "on Sunday, May 2, at 5.15 p.m. No sooner had the accident taken place than a companion stripped him and dressed his wound, and within a few minutes Harry was in his usual humour, smoking and joking, none the 'worse in health, in spite of his wound. . . . My comrades and I have felt the loss of Harry's company. He was a specially good mate to me, and a cheery soldier.'''

===== 0002601_19150515_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
pitn .vrE A. 11. HI \l'.

Ruth I passes irlirough His Side, Stirprising Comrade.

It was on Saturday morning that Mr. and Mrs. Seth Hunt, of 40, Cartwright-street, first received the news that their son—Private Albert Henry Hunt—had been wounded at the Front. That news was contained in. a letter sent from the Etretat Hospital, in which it was stated:—

"I am going on all right. It is a lovely place here, just beside the sea. I got -wounded at 11.30 p.m. on Wednesday, the 28th. We were out digging a communication trench, and it was a moonlight night—which gave the enemy a jolly good view of us. I had just started work when they gave me this gentle reminder: a bullet through my sside; but it is nothing for you to worry about. The doctor says I was lucky, but that is all in the game.. "It had been a lively week up to Wednes- day. We were playing football last Monday, when without warning they gave us a dose of six-inch heavy shrapnel. We had a team of thirteen a side, and two shells bursted exactly over, our heath! Not one of the players was hit, but two civilians—who were watching the game—were killed and about fifty wounded. On Tuesday the enemy shelled our billet, and planted a shell through the roof; but again we got off free. Alex. Herschel! and Norman Wilson got wounded about ten minutes after me. Alex. is _ here with me, but Norman's wound as not serious. .. . All our chaps are keeping up well. The Canadians gave the Germans ` humpy ' last Wednesday; we were not far away. .. . Cheer up! I may be in England soon!" Private N. Wilson,-himself wounded, writes to a friend :

I was talking to A. Hunt just before he went down to the base. I don't think he is seriously wounded. . . . I had been speaking to him for a few minutes before it. happened, and when I turned round again they had him on the stretcher.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
All About Him.

Now aged 21, and a native of Warrington, Private Hunt was educated until aged thirteen at the Heathside Schools. He then went to work for a short time in Messrs. Monks' filing- shed and bar-drawing department; afterwards joining the Firth Company, Howley, as a

strand-machine worker. Here he remained until being called up in August—having pre- viously belonged to the " Terriers " for nearly four years. He volunteered for foreign service, and trained for some months in Scotland and at Tunbridge Wells, cheerfully proceeding to the actual. war scenes in February. Writing from the Front a week or so ago, the Private jocularly said: "It was rotten—my 21st birthday in the trenches! But if I live to come home all right we'll 'fetch up for that. The Germans have given me a very nice re- minder—a bullet passing through my side; but don't upset yourself!"

Private Hunt attended the Bank! Quay

Ex-Longford United Footballer Shot In

'rue Neck.

I Private Harry Barton, of C Company of the ' Warrington Territorials, who formerly resided with his wire at 28, Cyril-street, is in hospital suffering from a bullet wound in the head and neck, received on May 3rd. He is 23 years of age and prior to' offering himself for active service he worked as a blacksmith's striker at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles' works, where he had been employed a matter of about three years. Prior to that he was engaged at Messrs. Rylands Bros'. • The wounded man is a son of Mr. Samuel Bar- ton, of Longford-street, and he was educated at the Silver-street school. He has been married about sixteen months, and is the father of one little child, who lives with its mother at 54, West-street.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
A Comrade Conveys the News.

The news that Private Barton had been wounded- was first conveyed to his •wife in a letter written by Private John Clarke, a stretcher-bearer, who is also serving with the 4th South Lancashires. The letter read as follows:

"I am sorry to inform yo that Harry came in [hospital] last night, May 3rd; with a slight wound in the neck. He asked me just to let you know and to tell you not to worry, as the hurt is really ,very slight. I was with him for some time before he left here, and then went to him in the motor ambulance. . . . I can assure you that there is no need to worry, and we are expecting him back again in a week or so." .

Rejoined " Terriers " on Outbreak of War.

Private Barton is keenly interested in manly sports, and was an enthusiastic playing member of the Longford -United F.C. He had completed one spell of four years in the lerritolials, and rejoined when the war :broke out. He under- went his war training in Scotland and at Tun- bridge Wells.

l'iti% ITE T. CUNNINGHAM.

The Bullet That Hit Him As A Souvenir

News reached his wife at 39, Forshaw-street, Warrington, on Wednesday of last week, that Private T. Cunningham, ,of "Tr Company, 4th Battalion, South Lancashire Regiment, was wounded in action on Saturday, May Ist.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
Says He Is Doing Campion."

-The intimation, which was conveyed on a post-card, and was written on behalf of Private Cunningham, by a comrade, ran as follows : " I have been wounded in the right arm, so .cannot write myself. I am, however, doing _champion. I was hit on May Ist, about 10 p.m., when I was just going into the trenches. Don't worry yourself about we, as I can thank God it is no worse. This is being , written in the train, going down to hospital. I am hoping to see you soon."

Later, M-rs. bunningliam received a post-card, dated May 4th, and .bearing the heading "No. 2 Military Hospital, Fakenham, Norwich." It ran as follows :—" I have, arrived here and I -am pleased to say lam doing champion. Do not let it worry you, as I am all right."

Successfully Operated Upon:

. _ . Mrs. Cunningham later received another letter which ran as follows :—" The bullet is in the muscle of my shoulder, and has gone through ,to the back. I shall have to undergo a slight operation to have it taken out. The wound was very painful at first, but it is easier now. I don't think any bones are broken. I shall be able to tell when I can get home, after the operation is over. The nurse cannot say for a day or two, because there are many bad cases in hospital. I shall be glad of a few cigar- ettes."

In a letter from hospital, dated May 11th, he says :--" I went under an operation on Sunday, and have got over it all right. It has, however, been very painful. They got the bullet out and lam keening it as p. souvenir. I was talking to our ' Pat ' (another brother in the Terri- torials) ten minutes before I was shot, and he was then doing all right. He was going into the trenches with my company, so he would soon get to know about me." Private Cunningham, who is the son of Mr. Martin Cunningham, of Orford, is a married man of twenty-five pans of age.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
Seven, Years in the Old Volunteers.

He resides in peace times with his Wife, at 39, Forshaw-street. He served for seven years with the old Volunteers, and re-joined the civilian force on the outbreak of war. Born at Orford. he attended the St. Benedict's Roman Catholic School, Orford-lane, and after leaving at the age of 13, he went to work at Mortimer's Tannery, Orford, where he was employed as a ".scudder" until the outbreak of war. .He attended St. Benedict's Church. He was a very keen bowler, and had won many prizes. He has played for the Orford Tannery team in the Works' Competition. He was drafted to the Front on February 16. Several relatives of Private Cunningham's are serving with the colours. His brother, Patrick, is serving at Front in the same company as himself, while two brothers-in-law—Private H. Bailey and Private S. Thomas—are also in the Territorials. Yet another relative is under- going training with the Royal Field Artillery.

LANCE-CORPL. R. L. HAWTHORN.

"H The Young Men Knew, They Couldn't Stay Al Home."

Mrs. Hawthorn, of 40, Wellington-street, Warrington, has received news that her hus- band, Lance-Corporal Robert Lester Hawthorn,

of the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regi- ment, has been wounded. The news came to hand on Wednesday morning in the form of a letter from Sergeant-Major Davis, of the com- pany to which Lance-Corporal Hawthorn be- longs. The letter stated that lie had bePn wounded by a bullet which passed clean through his right hide.

Further Promotion In Sight.

It also mentioned that Lauce-CO-rporal Hat:- thorn had been recommended for further pro- motion.' Later, Mrs. Hawthorn received a short note from her husband, written in the trenches uhilst he was ewaiting the coming of the motor ambulance which was to take him to the Base. He spoke cheerfully of being unly slightly wounded and said be was looking forward eagerly to coming home and seeing his wife and eight-Year-old girl and his fortnight-07A taby

===== 0002601_19150515_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
Good Swimmer and Bowler.

Lance-Corporal Hawthorn was educated at the Wycliffe School and the People's College. He entered the employ of Messrs. Riehmond as a range-fitter. As a bowler he was very skil- ful and enthusiastic, and he held a medal for swimming. He is 30 years of age, and was cne of the old Volunteers. One of the first to join the Territorials, he was a keen member, from first to last, in time of peace, and, when war broke out, was in the vanguard of those who early answered the call.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
An Interesting and Cheerful Letter-Writer.

He has written many long, interesting and cheerful letters' to his wife. - We quote from two of the most recent:—"lf all the young men of Warrington could see what's going on out here, they couldn't stay at, home!" And, again, after describing the trench: "Just as I started writing, one of the chaps on the other side got it into his head that we wanted our sandbags on\the top ripped. He was pump- ing lead into them. I had to get up and let him have ten rounds before he gave up the idea. You see, if you catch the top of one of the enemy's sandbags with a bullet, it throws sand all over the chap on the other side. Many a time As we happen to be cooking our break- fast we get a shower of sand into the bacon; and then the Germans get called some fancy names."

In a letter written in the Royal Hospital at

Salford, Lance-Corporal Hawthorn says: "I suppose you will be coming to see yours truly?. Lance-Corporal Hawthorn has two brothers in the Army, one in the R.F.A.—Bombardier Hugh Hawthorn—and another in the A.S.C.— Private Francis Hawthorn.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
Two Brothers and Three Brothers-in-Law.

Mrs. Hawthorn has three brothers with the colours, viz., Sapper James Nicholson, Private Edward Nicholson (in the Northumberland Fusiliers), and Bombardier Nicholson. Private Edward Nicholson has been wounded and lies in hospital at Newcastle.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE B. JOLLEY.

Did Not At First know He Was Wounded.

Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Jolley, of 64, Forshaw- street, have received word that their son, Pte. B. Jolley, of A Company of the Warrington Ter- ritorials, has been wounded, receiving a shrap- nel wound in the left wrist on April 26th.

The news was first conveyed in a brief note which stated that the wound was not serious.

The Bullet Was a Big `Lin.

Later came a letter from the- wounded man, who is now in the Mater Miserecordian Hospital,. Dublin, in which he says : " I had the bullet taken out on Friday. It Was a big 'un, too! They won't let us leave this place until we are fit. You don't need to bother yourselves about me. . . . There were two of us together when I got hit—Jack Wright and myself. We both threw ourselves at full length on the ground before the shell burst, and he did not, at the time, know that was hit. It was just the same as if someone .had hit me with a stone. When we got up I ran one way and Jack ran the other. When I was running across some fields I met Jack Smith, and.he asked me whether I was hit. I

replied 'Aye!' and then a ' Scottie ' hit! ndaged the wound up for me and took me OWL [dress- ing] station. . . . As soon as the shell burst I was up and away, and I did not know that I had got wounded until I saw the blood, and then I had run a matter of fifty yards or so. . . . I am a bit safer here than in Bel-

glum.'

Two Cousins With the " Terriers."

Private Jolley is 29 years of age, and is a single. man. He was born in Warrington, and was educated at the National .Schools, Church- street- Prior to volunteering for active service he was employed as a warehouseman at Ran- dall's Tannery, in Tanners-lane. He went to

the Front with the first batch of "Terriers" to cross the Channel, having done his training partly in Scotland and partly at Tunbridge Wells. He has two cousins serving with the "Terriers," and he i 4 making most satisfactory progress towards recovery.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
LIEUTENANT HATTON.

The Men l'ho Carried Him To Safety.

Latest reports show that Lieutenant Hatton is progressing very satisfactorily, though it will be many weeks before the use of the wounded leg will be recovered. Last week Their Majesties the King and Queen visited Viscountess Ridley's Military Hospital, Carlton House Terrace, London, where Lieutenant Hatton is lying and had a long chat with the wounded Warrington officer. We learn that the men who left the safety of the trench under fire to bring in Lieutenant Hatton when he had been shot down were Sergeant Hansford and Private Caesar. - They are not Warrington men.

RU kVE WARRINGTON LAD.

Wounded Charging Germans.

Private Wm-. Preston, Loyal North Lancai shire Regiment, stepson of Corporal Wm. Taylor, King's Liverpool Regiment, has been wounded. Private Preston, who is only nine- teen years of age, ,has been at the Front since September 9th. A friend, Private Harry Car- rington, writing to his mother "to break the news," said Preston was wounded in the big battle on Sunday, the 9th. He got hit in the face and on the arm in the second charge upon the Germans, and "all the lads gave him good credit for the way he went -over the trenches after them." They were "all talking about him."

RIFLEMAN .1. ROBINSON. Hoping To Be Home In Three Weeks' Time. News was received by Mrs. T. Robinson, of Dallam-lane, Warrington, on Wednesday, that her son, Rifleman Albert Robinson, of the Rifle Brigade, had been wounded in the right hand. _ _

_ In a letter to his mother, Rifleman -Robinson describes the nature of his injury, and says: "Don't worry; it's not much. lam in a Cana- dian hospital—and very nice, too! I can't write much, as it pains my hand." This letter came on Thursday, and on the same day an- other message came from Rifleman Robinson saying that he was in "a nice private house, and hoping to be home in three weeks' time."

Rifleman- Robinson, who is twenty years of age, joined Kitchener's Army in August, relinquishing a post in the employ of the Cable Works Co., in order to do so. He was educated at Wycliffe School.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
Carter's Terrible Fate.

The circumstances attending the death of John Shaw, a carter, of 57, Wellington-street, Warrington—who was run over by the furni- ture van which he was driving at Partington

on Friday of last week—were inquired into by Mr. Coroner Yates at Brook Farm, Partington, on Monday. The deceasM was employed by Messrs. John Buckley and Co., Ltd., furniture removers, of Church-street, Warrington, and the accident which resulted in his death, occurred near Brook Farm, on the road leading from Warburton to Partington. He hit the chain-horse with the reins to urge it up the incline. The horse gave a short jump forward, and tightened the chains, but Shaw kept hol# of the shaft-horse, which he endeavoured fo hold back. The action of the front horse caused the second animal to slip. The shaft-horse pulled to one side, threw its forefeet out, and struck Shaw, causing him to fall on his back. The near front wheel went over his chest, and the hind wheel passed over his foot. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death," and expressed sympathy with the relatives.

The Reason Why. A wanderer through South Carolina watched', an old negro fishing in a brickyard pond for forty minutes, during which time the hook was not pulled up. _ _

Do you think there are any fish there," he asked at last. "No, sah, I reckon not."

"But you seem to be fishing." "Yes, sah." ,

But ..perhaps you are not fishing for fish. What is your object?" " Do objick, sah, of my fishin' foh fish wah dey hain't any fish is to let de ole woman see that I ain't got no time to dig up de potato, patch."

- Private H. Cad m an.-7 Another card has reached Lymm this week to say that Private Henry Cadman (sth .Cheshires) is making rapid . progress towards recovery from his wounds. He will not cross to England. ,

" Fit For Anything!"—Private Joseph !hue writes:—" We are all in good fettle—fit for anything. We are doing some hard and good work:" His brother, Sergeant Clare, says much the same in a letter to his mother.

Journeyings of Wounded.—A large number of wounded " Tommies " have passed in motors through Lymm this week. On Thursday six- teen ears laden with wounded travelled through, an hour or two later returning empty.

inister-Secretary.—At the May meetings of the Manchester District Synod, the Rev. R. W. Ferguson *as appointed secretary, and was also elected a delegate to the conference which will be held at Reading, commencing on June 16th.

Stack Fire Leads To An Arrest.—Shortly after two o'clock on Monday afternoon a hay- stack at Dunham Massey was discovered to be on fire, and was burned clowrL In connection with the fire a young man, names John Worrall, whose home is in Rush Green, has been arrested.

Late Private Jackson and " Terriers."—" We were all very sorry" writes a " Terrier " to Mr. D. Daniels, to hear of Walter Jackson getting killed [reported in our last issue]; I am sure it will be a very hard and trying-time for his wife and children. But it must have been God's wish, so we must not be downhearted."

Postman' Weds.—At the Parish Church on Wednesday afternoon the Rector (Rev. G. B. Thurston) officiated at the wedding of Mr. Alber.,,Heathcote, of Pepper-street, and Miss Talbot, of Rush Green. The happy couple now reside at Rush Green. The bridegroom is em- ployed A Mr. Wareham's, Eagle Brow, and also acts as a postman.

Mistaken Notion.—ln many parts of Lymm the idea is prevalent that Mr. H. E. Odber, butcher, of The Grove, is of German extraction. This is not so. Both Mr. and Mrs. Odber were born in England of English parents, and Mr. Odber can trace his English ancestry to the building of Glastonbury Abbey. He is pre- pared to give a substantial reward to anyone who can supply information as to the origin of the rumour.

ef ugees' Departure. —To " Beechwood," Lymm home of the Belgian Refugees, farewell was said on Tuesday by M. Ponyarts and family —who have been provided with a flat at Hollo- way—and M. Von Carricover. The latter had only been with his Lymm friends a fortnight, and goes to a situation in London; his hotel at . Ostend is at present in the charge of his wife and father.

Mr. H. Leather Dead.—With regret we record the death of Mr. Hugh Leather, aged 22 and unmarried, which took place from consumption at "Brookfield-terrace," Booth's Hill, on Thurs- day evening—despite the efforts of Dr. de Jong. 'Mr. Leather had been suffering patiently for three years, and a fortnight ago was compelled to take to his bed.—He was a gardener for Mr. Houghton, " Westlea," and was widely re- spected. Son of the, late Mr. and Mrs. James Leather, of Rush Green, he leaves four sisters and three brothers to mourn him.—The Rector is to conduct the funeral at the Parish Church to-day.

COUNCIL OFFICE INQUIRY.

Permission Granted For Sewerage And Sewage Disposal Loans.

For the purpose of inquiring into the appli- cation of the Lymm Urban District Council for permission to borrow, for sewerage and sewage disposal, ' a further sum of £3OO, Mr. R. H. Bicknell, an inspector of the Local Government Board, on Tuesday afternoon attended af " Whitbarrow," the Council Offices. There were also present Messrs. R. H. Gotobed (Council chairman), W. Mullard (clerk), W. M. Beckett (engineer for the scheme), D. Mort (surveyor), and Dr. de Jong (medical officer of health). In reply to his question, the Inspector was informed that the amount of the sewage loan up to date was £9,672, which sum had been over- spent by £3OO. The proceedings were of a purely formal character. At the close of the inquiry Mr. ,Gotobed, on behalf of the Council, tendered his cordial thanks to the Inspector for his courtesy, and expressed the hope that he was of the opinion they had not embarked on any useless expendi- ture. Mr. Bicknell briefly replied, and announced that the application of the Council would be granted. He paid a high tribute to the man- ner in which the scheme had been prepared by the Council officers.

METHODIST SCHOLARS

Celebrate Anniversary With Service

And Entertainment.

Large congregations gathered for the cele- brations of the Lynim Primitive Methodist Sunday School anniversary held on Sunday and Monday. On the former day the preacher was Mr. J. W. Storey, a student from the Man- chester College. In the afternoon, Mr. G. L. Welford (Sunday School superfhtendent) :presided over a special service. Suitable hymns, were rendered by the scholars and infants, and the lessons were read by Eva Tonge and Lois E. Priestner. The following recitatio,ns were nicely gone through: "Given in Charge," Hilda Tongue; "The Little Bits of Things," William Sharpies; "The Children's Friend," Annie Struthers; "A Little Star," Vera Tongue; and "A Little Girl's Address," Marjorie Priestner. Mr. Storey gave a helpful address.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
The War and Faith.

On Monday evening some 130 sat clown to partake of an excellent public tea—which was followed by a "children's evensong," Mr. Wel- ford again occupying the chair. The Rev. R. W. Ferguson led the prayer. The Chlirman said it was no easy matter to speak on Sabbath school work when the pre- sent grave matters pressed upon their minds. It was indeed difficult to understand how such a highly-educated people as the Germans could resort to such dastardly practices as the use of asphyxiating gases and the torpedoing of the Lusitania. Despite all these appalling facts, he believed victory would ultimately rest with the Allies; and appealed for the prayers of all on behalf of the loyal and noble sons on sea and land, and for a greater faith in God. (Applause.) Mr. Ernest Moss (school secretary) heartily thanked. all who had contributed to the success of the function, and highly complimented the scholars on their music. He had had more applications than he could find posftions for— owing to the better mutual understanding be- tween teachers and scholars. Many had been busy with their collecting-books, and the order of Royalty was: Queen, Emily Warham, 11s.; princesses, Lois Priestner„tl 3s. 6d., and Cissy Tonge, 145.; princes, Phil L. Welford and 0. S. Welford, 14s. each. Other collectors were: Hilda Tonge, us. 9d.; Arthur Foolka, Cis. 3d.; William Skarples, 75.; Bessie Cross, 75.; Stanley Knowles, 6s. Bd.; Edith Sharples, ss. 6d.; Annie Struthers, 3s. 10(1.; and Mrs. Edwards, ss.—total, .t 1.2 12s. 6d. They wished every success to teachers and scholars absent through sickness or the war. (Applause.)

Music and Recitation.

All were capitally given. For the singing the children had been trained by Mr. J. Edwards. The proceeds totalled ,R,28 7s.—an increase on last year of ,t 2 13s.

LYMM TERRITORIALS.

FIRST COMPLETE HISTORY OF THEIR DOINGS ABROAD.

No further news of casualties, among the Lymm Territorials now at' the Front, has this week reached the township. On Tuesday morning Sergeant It Clare's communication, written a week ago, said all were well and were entering the trenches again for the usual two days. The weather out there, he added, was very hot. The Lymm lads are lucky to be in good health, for they hake been having some exciting times with the poigonous gases now used by the enemy. A striking example of how a rumour can spread is afforded by Private Joseph Clare's letter home. This states: "Jack Moston had a letter from his father saying that Mr. T. Ridg- way has been killed. Is there any truth in it? I' expect it is another rumour. . . ." As we have already reported, there was not the slight- est truth in the rumour. Lance-Corporal S. Linley on Tuesday even- ing arrived. home. The wound in his back is now practically healed.. This description also applies to the injured. arm of Private A. Haddon, uho was accidentally hurt, and who arrived—from hospital—at his home on Mon- day evening. On Tuesday he returns to a section of his regiment. Lance-Corporal E. Blease, who was wounded, expected to arrive at his home yesterday. From the first of these wounded men "The _ .

Examiner" has just obtained the first com- plete account of the Lymm Territorials' ex- periences during their first two months in the war zone. We publish it below. In the course of a highly gratifying tribute

to "The Examiner's" local war service, our interviewe says that this paper is most eagerly lookLd forward to by the men at the Front. A weekly copy is sent, he adds, to practically every Lymm " Terrier " at the Front; they quite enjoy reading news of the _home happenings in theit native township.
