FIRST TWO MONTHS ABROAD.

Lymin Men's Experience In The War

Zone.

Interviewed by "The Examiner" representa,-

Lye as to the local "Terriers'" experiences in

the war zone, Lance-Corporal Linley- said: We went out to the Front on February 14th (St. Valentine's' Day). with about five hundred of other men on board. We had a splendid pas- sage. In France we marched to a rest camp, and while marching through had a good recep-

tion—especially from the children and womeii. I noticed that the children 9ould ask for his- cults!

Y.M.C.A. at Rest Camp.

The rest camp was on a high plateau, and during our two days there we had a rough time; for it was very wet. On the plateau was a Y.M.C.A. building and staff, which was doing very good work for the soldiers by way or re- freshments, entertainments, and services.

Cattle Truck Concert. After dinner on the third day we marched to the station and entrained in cattle trucks—about forty in each, like herrings in a tin. We didn't mind a bit, however, and beguiled the time with a concert, Sergeant Clare presiding. All the popular songs were given by different ones, and above the noise and the din it was a job to make yourself heard! But we enjoyed it. I couldn't sing, so I made a bit of a speech. I tried to encourage them, and said we were.fighting for the cause of liberty and honour, and that we had come out to do our best for home and loved ones.

We were thirty hours on the train, and had no food during that time—except what we happened to have with us. You see, they had no opportu- nity of dishing out the rations. Slept in Poisoned Vineries.

At length we arrived at our destination— Bealieue. This is a large town, and what we saw of it was in good condition, although there were a few buildings shattered by shells. We marched straight from the train for about ten minutes, to some vineries—where we slept the night in glass houses. Then we found the Ger- mans had poisoned the vines and destroyed the plants; some men who were working near to told us.

It was here that we met the Warrington Ter- ritorials, who were staying in the same vineries. Here, too, we first heard the sound of the. guns as they rumbled away ,in the distance.

Convent After Hard March.

In the morning we set out for N (or some place like that; these names puzzle us a bit !). It was a very heavy march, and some had a hard job to stick it; for, although the distance was only seven or eight miles, the roads were ex- tremely bad. We were all "dead beat" when we arrived there. When we did so we were under shell fire, and were billeted in part of a convent. We slept on the floor with two blan- kets apiece. Next day we were out drilling. Altogether we were billeted at N - for about five weeks. Sometimes the days were fine, but at others it was very cold and rainy, and when we went into the trenches we had some snow.

_ But we didn't go into the trenches straight away. After a week our non-commissioned officers had a turn in them, along with the. Regulars, and we followed after a fortnight— also with the Regulars, so as to gain experience We could go in "on our own" after two or three turns with them. First Impressions of Trenches. It was rather a novel experience when we first went into the trenches, and one which you hardly know ,how to describe. Of course, it was very funny to find yourself behind a lot of sandbags; and when you had a peep over you couldn't see anything. The scen.6 was made more strange by the fire-balls from the Germans. Now and again they would send them up, and if anyone happened to be show- ing over the trenches at the time, then rifle fire began—sometimes heavily, and at others just from a sniper. - • We weren't exactly afraid 'of the, fire, bit the novelty of the thing seemed to. give you a kind of awe, and when the bullets began flying around we were wondering where they came from, for you couldn't see the, enemy—at least, I couldn't!

Well, we kept going into the trenches for over three weeks, in periods of twenty-four or forty-eight hours at a time. The clays we weren't in, we were doing fatigue work—carry- ing rations to the men who were in the trenches. That is more dangerous work fhan being in-the trenches. However, the Lymm lads had no casualties there (although some of the battalion were wounded). First Lymm Casualty. _ _

We moved from that place, after five weeks. to a village which was continually under fire. 'There it was that we had some of °fir hottest work to do—as those trenches were only forty or fifty yards from the German lines. We had Jim Taylor wounded whilst on fatigue work about a week after our arrival there. He got shot in the left arm. . We were not billeted in that village; for after doing our four days in the support trenches we used to march for billets to L—, which is about five miles away. The weather by now had greatly improved; it was a good deal warmer—of course, we had got to the middle of March! Well, for a fortnight we went on with our four days in and four days out of the trenches, marching back to Jr.-- each time. One•day, whilst behind the trenches, Private Arthur Hadden slipped into a " Jack Johnson" -hole and hurt his arm, • Easter Meeting—Easter Greeting.

It was now drawing near to Easter, and on Wednesday, March 31st, the church chaplain held a service for those (including us) who were going to be in the trenches over Easter. The spirits of the men were very good, and their health was the :same. We had .plenty of fun amongst ourselves, .and were always,play- ing practical jokes upon one another. Also Ave met an old Lymm friend—Private 'James

Booth, of Statham. He belongs to the Welsh Fusiliers,. and had no idea we were out -there. When he heard we were billeted in the same village as he was, he was very surprised and pleased. SO were ' we! He came to see us, looking remarkably well; and we all had a chat with him. The Death Of Private Leigh.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 15, 1915.

take our turn in the trenches. It was quiet that night, but all our lads were busy filling sandbags and strengthening the trenches. Next 'day was quiet also, and we got pretty well of rest in our, turn—till about 4.30 in the after- Then the shelling began, and the second shell that came killed Peter Leigh, and wounded me in the back. (Private Richard Shaw was wounded just after I left). Peter Leigh was killed instantly. Just before he had been in the dug-out, asleep. I had made some tea; and, after having a drink and something to eat, called to him to come and have his tea. So he changed place's with me. I told him I would get up and have a smoke while he had his tea, and I was just preparing to do so when the shell came. It hit Peter on the head and, he fell on the ground. I shouted for the ambulance at first, but then saw it was no use. He never moved, and two or three lads came and laid him out. _ _ _ _

LOOll.

- The news that Private Leigh had been killed quickly passed along the line—for we only occupied about a hundred yards. For a tiine it damped all our spirits, but sow- -fter we commenced to sing hymns—" Conn' Your

Blessings" and "Abide With Me," and other favourites. When I left them they were all quite cheerful again.

Leaving The Trenches

I was wounded at 4.30, and.loft the trenelfes

'two hours after. I could walkaway; but four others of the battalion who were wounded had to come on stretchers. There were sixteen of the volunteer stretcher-bearers with Us, of whom two or three were Lymm lads. I don't think Pri6tes Henry Brooks or Richard Shaw were amongst theta, but I should like to say a word about their services as stretcher-bearers out at the Front. Both—although not having had much training in ambulance work—did their duties really splendidly. Under fire they were very cool whilst dressing the men's wounds, and are very popular indeed with all of us. .

Hill 60 battle. There are over six hundred G;:fir•an dead on the hillside; the smell is: /1 V In]. round this part. Our men got put in a hole made by a German, 17-inch shell." . Writing two days later, Private Gibbs says:— "We are still in the dug-outs. We have been here now seven days; When we shall get re- lieved, I don't know. Private Joseph Clare writes to his mother at Booth's Hill :—" I. only wish I was billeted at Cambridge now; the Germans are giving us some more to contend with, in the shape of their poisonous shells!. But we have been sup- plied with breathing-pads. . . ."

===== 0002601_19150515_art0177.txt | included=true | included =====
Back By Exactly Same Route.

'The next experience I-had was that of being conveyed in the motor-ambulance, which, along with others, I entered, upon arrival at the dress- ing station after a twenty-five minutes' walk from the trenches. The ambulance took us to Locoh Hospital, where we stayed the night. Next morning it brought us down to Bealieue— where we again had our wounds dressed. Bealieue, by the way, had not been shelled any more than when we first saw it: of course the fighting had been drawn away from that region.

When our wounds were dressed, we were taken to the station—when a hospital train carried us to Le Havre. The journey occupied about twenty-seven hours—three less than when we went—and we stayed at the port for the night. On Easter Sunday morning we embarked on H.M. Hospital Ship Oxfordshire-- which is a very large vessel decorated on each side with large red crosses, and which shows lights all night to let the war vessels know what she is doing. _ _ _

The arrangements in the hospitals, trains and ships—both on the journey back and at home —were particularly pleasing. As far as you could see, everything that possibly could be done had been done. We were all very .thank- ful.

We had ' a beautiful sail across. The Channel was like a pond, and the weather lovely. On the Monday we landed at South- ampton—thankful, I can tell you, to be safe back in England!

===== 0002601_19150515_art0178.txt | included=true | included =====
A Grand Reception.

When we entered Bristol station a grand reception awaited us—we numbered about a hundred. It was a holiday, and the people were cheering us all the way through the streets to the place where the Red Cross Society had provided a splendid meal. It was really like a party! After Ave weeks at the Bristol Hospital I was moved to Hinton St. George, Crewkerne, where I have been until coming home to Lymm. Your paper has already given capital accounts of my experiences there.

In reply to a questiop, the Lance-Corporal said the officers at the Front were very well liked indeed. Captain Hartley was popular because he was always kind and considerate, whilst Lieutenant Walker—who is also in charge of the Lymm lads—follows the Captain's example by doing all in his power to make comfortable his men.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
Novel Religious Services.

It will be noticed that Lance-Corporal Linley modestly omitted to mention the religious ser- vices which, all the time,they were in the fight- ing area, he conducted amongst the Lymm lads. It should be said that these were most highly appreciated by the "Terriers," who even now frequently mention in their letters home how those services are missed. The Lance-Corporal is, of course, a well-practised Wesleyan local preacher, and seems fully to have met the requirements of his comrades in that respect.

One "'Terrier" declares that the enjoyment of the services was not rendered less by the fact that frequently during the cold weather they had to be participated in by the men lying upon the ground wrapped in their blankets!

Lance-Corporal Linley shotwed "The Exam..

Second Casualty In His Family.

SCENES NEAR HILL 60.

The Lymm lads in the firing-line have by now experienced the effects of the asphyxiating gases -used by the enemy.

Private Stanley Gibbs, Henry-street, writes : "I am up here in the dug-outs. We have been here four days now, and I think we are going into the trenches to-night for two days-. The worst of it is we get- letters up here, big no parcels; so we have to wait until we go do-, n to the town again. . . Well, you will see that the Germans are using those poisoncPs gases; so we have to have mouth-protectors. Our eyr.4 fill with water.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
"Never Know the Moment."

To Mr. D. Daniels, confectioner, Booth's Hill, Private J. Clare, a short time ago, wrote: "We must not he downhearted. We never know the moment when He will Call us now that the Germans are using those poisonous shells. We have not, so far, had any over the trench that we are in, and none we want; hut we have

been supplied with breathing-pads in case of emergency. . ."

Driven Into Gas By Shell-Fire.

• Private Jim Leah, of the transport section, says: "You say there is a tremendous lot of suffer- ing and death here. There might well be, for the -cowards are using gas in their shells—that is how we are losing so many. They have had a try with us. They started to shell us; and as we thought we were going out of the way, we were going into it. "They seemed to follow us, and with shell wounded four of our transport men, killed one ' horse right out, and wounded three more horses —we had to shoot two of 'them. . .

felegram Received By His Mother

PATRIOTIC FAMILY.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
LYMM POET DESCRIBES DEATI OF FIRST LOCAL WARRIOR.

Mr. James Appleton, aged 19, composed the following in two hours' time whilst following his work as a tool-maker under Mr. G. L. Wel- ford, Statham. It refers to the death of Private Peter Leigh, who—like the writer—lived at Statham:—

It Was a cold night in the trenches; The German shells burst all around. A group of the Lymm Territorials Were trying to gain the lost ground; And as they lay there in the trenches, Thinking how long it would last, The dreadful news reached them: A comrade— A comrade was dying fast.

They hurried to his side, but th-ey found that he was gone: They could tell that breath had left him as his face they looked upon. A brave hero was he, for he went there to .do or die, And I hope that God in Heaven took him into Eternity. ,

Some pals were wounded beside him— Some of them seriously hurt. And these are some of the happenings Caused by Kaiser Bill's dirty work. Well, though he may think he is "top dog," The grand time will shortly come When he'll learn that Britain is master, And that Germans are all "on the run!"

Just a group of lads, ever willing to do their share. With the leader, Captain Hartley, they are ready to do and dare; And if he orders "Charge !" they will do so with a will— For they've sworn to smash the ambitiqn of the wicked Kaiser Bill.

The Kaiser, with his army, may think he'll rule the world; But when he awakes from his dreaming our flag will still be unfurled. With his aeroplanps and Zeppelins he fancies he's "the boss And when the war is over we'll make him an Iron Cross. •

For we'll take old Kaiser Bill far away from the danger-zone; We'll put him on an island, and let him sing "Home, Sweet Home." And there we'll let him be, with nothing to see but nen— We must never let Kaiser William get back into Germany.

PADGATE.

Wesley Guild.—The monthly devotional I meeting of the Wesley Guild was held on Monday evening and was presided over by the Rev. A. J. Morton. A very interesting address was given by Alderman A. Bennett, the sub- jet being "True Christianity."—Miss L. Mason was the soloist and she gave a sweet rendering of "The secret of a happy day."— Miss E. Bennett was the accompanist. Wesleyan Anniversary.—Anniversary ser- vices in connection with the Wesleyan Church Sunday School were held on Sunday., when the Rev. W. Baby, of Aspatria, Cumberland, was the special preacher. At the morning service the preacher took his text from Luke iv. 18 and 19. He pointed out that the usual ;dap .about the survival of the fittest did not apply in the Christian life. Christ made Christians of the most unlikely human material. He, took hold of the broken pieces of humanity and made them citizens fit for the Kingdom of Heaven.—ln the evening he preached from Philemon, verses 10 and 11, anti showed how sin was the greatest depiecator in the country, and made cowards of all. The Church of Christ made men of character, and though sin dis-crowned* a man, yet the Gospel of Christ re-crowned him.—ln the afternoon a _flower service was held, presided over by Dr. Bennett. The Doctor said he took great in- terest in children and he made a special appeal to the parents to realise the benefits of fresh air for the children, showing how many lives might have been spared if only more notice had been taken of this important factor in the young lives of the rising genera- tion.—The Rev. W. Ruby gave a short, racy address to the children, his subject being "Heroes" He urged them not to be afraid in the days that were to come, but to do everything possible to face all difficulties, and thus become heroes.—The singing of the chil- dren was a feature of 411 the services, special anniversary music being rendered at each. Recitations were given by some of the scholars in the primary department at the afternoon service, and Miss j. Stewart was the. soloist: Duets were also rendered by members of the Sunday School and choir. Mr. W. J. Bate was the conductor and Mr. -W. IL Ward presided at the organ.—The collections, ,which were in aid of the Sunday School, Funds, amounted to ..C2l us.

PEN KETH.

Infants' Enthrone Their May Queen.—On Friday afternoon of last week the infants of

the Penketh Council School held their annual May festival. There had been some slight hesitation originally as to whether the teachers should allow • the festival to drop this year owing to the war, but, finally, 4 was decided that the children need not be disappointed of their fun. The choice of a May Queen is always decided by the little ones themselves, and this year fell on Dorothy Gregson. Great credit is due to Mrs. Bell and her assistant teachers for the 'capable manner in which they discharged the hard work—which is also a ' pleasure--necessary to teach such small chil- dren to perform so many clever little dances, &c.

"The Land of the Royal Fern."—A very in- teresting lantern lecture -entitled "The Land of the Royal Fern," was delivered by Mr. C. Wilcockson, of Warrington, in the Wesleyan School, -Penketh, on Thursday evening of last week, when there was a large and appreciative audience, who followed, in imagination, the lecturer through some of the most beautiful parts of Ireland. The lecture was on behalf of the trust fun* of the chapel.

STOCKTON HEATH.

Successful Scholars.—The following scholars of St. Thomas' School, Stockton Heath, have passed the first examination for county free admissions to secondary schools: Norman Pierce, Leslie Whyman, Hilda Hallows and Minnie Howard. •

A 1-torse Unattended.—At the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, Frank Simpson, of Ashfield Farm, Whitley, was fined 3s. 6d. for leaving unattended a 'horse and cart, of which he was in charge.—The evidence was to the effect that the horse, which was drawing a float, wandered a distance of two hundred yards while the defendant was making a call at a shop.

Sunday School Club and Soldier Members.— At the annual meeting of the St. Thomas' Sunday School Sick Club it was resolved that on and from the Ist inst. all members who have enlisted or who may enlist for the period of the war, be exempt from contributions dur- ing that time, and that they shall be kept in full benefit.

Daresbury Petty Sessions.—Mr. F. A. Frost was the chairman at Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday, and there were also on the Bench: Messrs. Roger C. Parr, W. Fletcher, T. Baxter, C. Jackson Holmes and L. Greening. No Collar, No Licence.—Oswald Ball, of Whitley Superior. who was summoned at the Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tuesday for allow- ing a dog to be at large without a collar and for keeping another dog without a licence, did not appear, and was fined 2s. 6d. and 108., re- spectively, for the two offences.

Cremation for Cottages !—Should old cottages be cremated or pulled down in the ordinary way? At the annual meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee on Tuesday even- ing, a letter was read from Mr. M. A. Hard- castle, a resident of London-road, complaining of the pulling down of some old cottages near his house. The writer pointed out It it was a very unhealthy way of destroying the house, as all sorts of germs might be scattered about in the dust that was bound to be disturbed by the pulling down process. He suggested that the better plan would be to set fire to the old building and have the fire hrigade standing by. (Laughter.) Mr. F. Pearson, who read the letter, stated that the house referred to had already been pulled down, and the debris all cleared away.—The Chairman: And they've taken the germs away? (Laughter.)—Mr. W. Mclntyre said he thought there was no indi- cation of any justification for the complaint that had been made.

Wesleyan Sunday School—Anniversary ser- vices were held on Sunday, when large con- gregations assembled at each service. The preacher morning and evening was Rev. W. L. Doughty, of Huddersfield. In the afternoon a flower service with cantata entitled "The Children's TribUte," was given by the scholars, whose singing and reciting reflected great credit on those who had trained them. The solos and duets were sung very sweetly by I. Clarke, L. Brookes, E. Whitfield, E. Houghton and B. Fair- hurst, whilst the recitation parts were admir- ably rendered by M. Ramsbottom, A. Rams- bottom, E. Rustage, W. llooper, E. Todd, D. Summers, I. Summers, E. Williams, J. Williams, 0. Ditchfield, E. Travis, R. Powell, S. Gamble, C. Worrall, E. Worrall, 0. Cooper, M. Williams, A. Adamson, M. Adamson. The , chair was taken by Mr. Jos. Monks. The children sang special hymns at the morning and evening services, and the choir rendered the anthems "How Lovely Are Thy Habita- tions" and "Blessing and Glory" in a praise- worthy manner, under the conductorship of Mr. F. E. Penketh. Mr. G. A. Whitfield ably presided at the organ. The collections for the day amounted to £22, a considerable increase on last year.

RE-ELECTED TO THE CHAIR.

Mr. G. Pilling To Again Preside Over

Parochial Committee.

The elections of officers and sub-committees took place at the annual meeting of the Parochial Committee, held at the Council Schools on Tuesday evening. The Deputy Clerk (Mr. W. E. Hough) took the chair at the commencement of the meeting, and there were also present: The retiring Chairman (Ur. G. Piling), the retir- ing Vice-Chairman (Mr. J. H. Clarke), Messrs. F. Pearson, 11. J. Westbrook, W. Mclntyre, J. McDermid, C. W. Hallows and A. Bottomley, together with the Surveyor (Mr. S. Wright), and the Clerk to the Parish Council (Mr. E. Bradshaw).

===== 0002601_19150515_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
" Had Given Perfect Satisfaction."

Mr. Westbrook proposed the re-election of Mr. Pilling to the chair. Mr. Pilling, he said, had 'presided for many years and given everyone perfect satisfaction. They hoped he would be given health and strength to continue in office for many years to come. Mr. Pearson, in seconding, made similar com- plimentary observations. By his businesslike _ _ _ _ _ _

Methods he had facilitated the. work of the committee.

Mr. Westbrook proceeded to propose a vote of thanks to Mr. Pilling for his services. Mr. Bottomley, in seconding, paid a tribute to the manner in which Mr. Pilling had con- ducted the affairs of his office, despite his recent family troubles. Mr. Clarke supported, and, in doing so, added his testimony to that already voiced. specially alluded to the admirable way in which Mr. Pilling had laid the requirements of Stock- ton Heath before the District Council.

The resolution having been warmly endorsed by the meeting, The Chairman replied, assuring the members that so long as health permitted, he would be pleased to give his.best services to the interests of the township.

A Most Suitable Man.

Mr. Mclntyre proposed the re-election of Mr. Clarke to the position of vice-chairman, remark- ing that Mr. Clarke took great interest in all matters pertaining to the welfare of the parish, and was altogether a most 'suitable man for the position. Mr. Bottomley seconded. Mr. Clarke returned thanks, and said he hoped he would justify the confidence the corn- mittee had placed in him.

A SEWAGE PROBLEM.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0193.txt | included=true | included =====
‘1 Warrington Help The Heath Ou

Of A Difficulty?

There was Some discussion at the annual meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Com- mittee, on Tuesday evening, of the township's sewage problem. Temporary assistance from the Warrington Corporation would be a boon to the suburb. Such assistance has been solicited by the sanitary contractor to the corn, mittee, who has been compelled by unforeseen

circumstances to give notice of cessation of contract. - - - -

A FARMER'S OBJECTION. Mr. F. Pearson announced the receipt of a notification from Mr. Spensley, ,sanitary con- tractor to the committee, to the effect that he wished to tender six months' notice in regard to his contract, as the tenant of the farm on which the refuse had hitherto been deposited, had raised an objection to the continuance of the practice. Mr. Spensley had written to the Warrington Corporation, asking that body if it was willing to allow the Stockton Heath refuse to be carted to the Warrington depot for the purpose and there dealt with. He had received no reply. A suggestion was put forward by Mr. Pear- son that the committee should lease or buy some land in the locality for the deposit of the refuse. They were strong enough to branch out and purchase a small estate of their own, and then they would not be up against a diffi- culty of that kind again—or at least for a good many years to come. They would have to do that or else build a destructor. Mr. J. H. Clarke said he thought that the committee ought to wait till the Town Council had had time to reply. No doubt the com- mittee to which the lettcsr would be submitted had not mettsince the contractor wrote. An Unhealthy Practice. Mr. A. Bottomley expressed the opinion that it was a good thing the farmer concerned had objected, for the dumping place was too near the town. He hoped no more refuse would be dropped there. They had a scheme in the air, and it was only a question of raising the money. Meanwhile they wanted a temporary measure, and he thought the best course to be adopted was to ask the Warrington Corporation if they were prepared to deal with the Stockton Heath sewage till the parish was in a position to deal with it itself. In any case, the present depository was too near the town. They had had all sorts of complaints about it from resi- dents. They were supposed to be a health authority, and whatever they did in the matter under discussion they should at least see to it that no sewage was ever dropped so near the town again. In reply to a question as to whether he meant that the Warrington Corporation should be asked to cart the sewage away in their own vehicles, Mr. Bottomley said his proposal was that the Corporation be asked to deal with it entirely— collect it and dispose of it with that of their own town. _ _

It was decided to await the reply of the War- rington Corporation to Mr. Spensley's letter.

THE LUSITANIA Death-Roll Of Eleyen Hundred And Forty-two.

The Cunard Company announce that, accord- ing to the latest information available, the number of passengers on board the Lusitania was as follows Saloon Second cabin Third-class Total

.... 1,255

Including the crew of 651, this makes the total number of people on board 1,906. The statement that there were 2,168 souls on board is incorrect.

Late on Sunday night the Cunard Company gave the number of saved as 764, which, deducted from 1,906, leaves an approximate death-roll of 1,142. MR. VANDERBILT IN THE WATER.

Assisted When I n The Water By Frodsham Man.

Mr. George Hutchinson, chief electrician, of the Lusitania, interviewed at his own home at Frodsham, this week, stated that he had just given his lifebelt to a young woman when he saw Mr. A. G. Vanderbilt struggling in the water. The millionaire's lifebelt was wrongly adjusted, and Mr. Hutchinson, who was tread- ing the water, tried to put it right, but could not. "I am Vanderbilt," the struggling man remarked to him. Mr. Hutchinson did his best to keep both of 'theta afloat; but they drifted apart. From his pulpit in New York on Sunday (says Reuter) Dr. Leighton (the Vanderbilts' pastor) urged the people to follow the Scrip- tural injunction, "Let him (Germany) be to thee as a heathen man and a publican." THE RECTOR'S PUSHING LOVE.

The Rev. Canon Willis, preaching in the Warrington Parish Church on Sunflay evening, took for his test: "Ye have heard that it hath been said: Thou shalt love thy neighbour aid hate thine enemy., But I say unto you: Love your enemies,, bless them that curse you, do good to them that hate you, and pray for them which despitefully use you and persecute you; That ye may be the children of your Father which is in heaven; for He maketh His sun to rise on the evil and on the goad, and sendeth rain on the .just and on the unjust." . _

having, spoken of the use of gas and the sinking of the Lusitania by the Germans, and referring to the words of his text, the Rector said: "Does a father love his children any the less because he punishes them? Do we love murderers any the less because we hang them?" The words of our Lord were not in- consistent with fiery indignation against the men who did the horrible things the Germans had done. When the Germans were truly penitent he would forgive them, but they must be punished as they deserved to be. The father who punished his children when they had done wrong loved them much more than the parent who did not correct them.

OLD WARRINGTONIAN GOES DOWN WITH THE LUSITANIA.

The victims of Hunnish infamy, who were drowned when the Lusitania foundered included an old Warringtonian, and one well known in the town. He was Mr. William Lake, of 80, BroOkdale-road, Sefton, Liverpool, and he was by birth a Warringtonian. At the time the torpedoes of the German submarine sent the great liner to the bottom he was acting as second-class bedroom steward aboard her. His name has not appeared among the list of the saved; and his relatives have been informed by the Cunard Company that little or no hope of his having been rescued can be entertained.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
One of Six Sons.

Mr. William Like was one of the six sons of the late Mr. Philip Lake, grocer and provision dealer, of Powys-street, Bank Quay, Warring- ton. Only two of the family of boys now sur- vive, one of them being Mr. Philip Henry Lake, "mine host" of the Blue Lion Hotel, Market- street. The deceased gentleman was educated in Warrington and commenced work in the town, subsequently going to Manchester, *here for a matter of about twenty years he was engaged as a commercial traveller.' He first took an en- gagenient with the Cunard Steamship Company some seven years ago, and had been with them, on and off, ever since.

His First Voyage for a Long Time—and • His Last.

In between he had worked as a salesman for a well-known Liverpool firm, and, in fact, the fatal voyage was his first since exigencies of health compelled him to give up work ashore and take to the sea life again, with the result that he had to make a fresh start afloat in a humbler position than that to which he had been accustomed whilst regularly afloat. The deceased leaves a wife and one son, 24 years of age, to mourn their loss. He was born on January 4th, 1860, and was therefore 55 years of age.

Ketirwrs to the (Ebitor

HONO I(tilt BRAVE HEROES.

Sir,—As we shall have shortly drafted into our local institutions hundreds more of our wounded soldiers, who \nay succumb to their injuries, could I appeal through your columns to those in authority for an honoured space to be allotted in our cemetery? As all creeds and nationalities are as brothers fighting side by side, surely arrangements could be made to lay, them together on one plot, to be known as the soldiers' rest—and not to be laid in the various sections as- unknowns as it were, in the free graves. If this could be carried out, say, just entering the gates, what -a fine monument could be erected to their memories in time to come for the noble sacrifice they have made "even unto death." . .

As one having those near And dear away the ranks, how comforting it would be to those that mourn their loss to know some kind thought was being giVen to the spot where our loved ones are sleeping, should they never return. Trusting this appeal will not be vain.

E. PLINSTON.

42, Bewsey-street

THE VOLUNTEER TRAINING CORPS

Sir,—The Warrington and District Volunteer Training Corps appears to be in rather a bad way. It was launched with flying colours shortly after the commencement of the war, and its membership soon ran up to 500. But it was wet-blanketed, at the outset, by thp War Office, and the unaccountable decision of the Lord Lieutenant of Lancashire that a county organi- sation was not necessary, discouraged it still further, whilst the want of sustained enthusi- asm on the part of many of its members haa almost given it a death blow. The Secretary', stated, at a meeting of the corps on MondaY that the number of members who had actually filled In the form which is required ill order to secure affiliation with the Central Association and so obtain Government recogni- Hon was about 80, and that the circular inviting the necessary support in order to place it upon a sound financial basis had only elicited the miserable response of £25 or so; and, in view Of these disheartening circumstances, it via° actually proposed that the corps should disband' This is not creditable to Warrington, a ton; which has always boasted of its patriotism and its public spirit!

I-have been referring, once again, to the veil ,1 interesting volume entitled "Warrington Volunteers, 1798-1898," by Walter Crompton and George Venn, which everybody in the town might read with much advantage at the present time; and I find that, when "the Bluebacks were formed, for a similar purpose, in 179 g. about 160 turned up at the first muster. We are told that they provided themselves "with arms, accoutrements, clothing and provisions ''; that they "laid aside all distinctions of private life "; that they drilled for two hours, at si% o'clock in the morning, three times a week, in the Old Assembly Room in Golden-square; and that, by Midsummer, they had the sum of 050 standing to their credit "at the Warrington Bank." They were disbanded in 1801, and, two years later, "The Robin Redbreasts" came into existence. 'The number wanted was only 500,, but 1,300 presented themselves for enrofraent, and the net .amount subscribed to meet the el: penses'was close upon three thousand pounds. P may be interesting to add that the regiment 105 under the command of Lieutenant-Colonel Lyon, land that it lasted for five years. The popole" Ition of Warrington would then be somewhere labout ten thousand. To-day, it is well ov'Br.. seventy thousand, and our corps has dwindle° down to 80! Upon the same basis as " Bluebacks," we should number 1,120, and, Ural the basis of" the Redbreasts," well over nine thousand. I have not any figures as to the unto: ber of Warrington men who have joinea the Colours at the present time, and nil comparison, of course, relates to the Volunteers actually raised for home defence hundred years ago and now. The contrast is disquieting in the extreme. What is the mean" ing of it all? Is the danger less acute than in Napoleon's day? It is by far more imminent and vital. Not only the freedom and the public law of Europe are at stake, but the veil existence of the Empire. Thousands of our lads have shown their readiness to face unspeakable hardships and the most deadly perils, but there are thousands more, many of them of military age, who still hold aloof, while there are thousands too old for active service who are content to grin and gape at us from the pave" ments, and who appear to grudge either the time or the money needed to ensure success; At my age, with my physical disabilities an?_ the many public calls upon my time, I cannot hope to be of much practical service to the corps, although I am very proud to belong t° it; but I can at least raise my voice in the hope that it may reach the multitude of rOl, fellow-townsmen who have ample leisure anu are physically fit to make this little sacrifice for their country in the face of this stupendonS crisis. The shades of grand old "Death or Glory" and his brave compatriots will hang, their heads in shame if the old place they love; so well should dishonour their memories °nu, everlastingly disgrace itself `by falling short, such a time; in any service it can render to the State. I am glad to say that the eighty who attended the recent meeting, most of whom have beerL assiduous in the performance of their duties, unanimously decided that the Corps should be continued; and they ask the citizens of WEIL: rington above the military age to join their ranii° and to help them to become a real credit to the town. Surely, when the facts are known, there can be only one reply! ARTHUR BENNEV• Market Gate Chambers, Warrington. 6th May, 1915.

THE MARTYRDOM OF BELGIUM.

Freedom's Champion.

DESPOILED AND BROKEN, BUT STILL QUEEN OF NATIONS.

BY C. E. PARKER.

The defence of Liege torts disputed the German passage and kept back the German invaders eleven days. All know the value to the Allies of this delay. It allowed France to complete her mobilisation, gave time to dis- patch the British Expeditionary Force, and destroyed the legend of German invincibility. But the brave stand at Liege was not all that Belgium did. The Belgians were not bound in honour to the Allies to continue resistance when the Liege passage had been forced. They were not obliged to form a first line of defence for the great Powers. They might well have said: "We have done what we can. We are incapable of withstanding the Germans fur- ther. The more we resist the more our coun- try will be devastated." Anticipating sur- render, the Gerthans sought by bribery to get Belgium to stand aside, but she refused the bribes, rejected all the offers made. The choice she made involved the burning- of her homes, the destruction of her cities, the star- vation and death of tens of thousands of her inhabitants. She put aside political expedi- ency, and took her stand on lofty national idealism, although the other course meant ease, comfort and self-interest on the lower plain. Belgium did not pause to calculate the cost of loyalty to the principle of national freedom. This decision was not alone that of her King, or rulers, it was the nation's choice. All racial, religious, and political differences were put aside, and the nation presented a united front to the invaders. Inch by inch they defended their territory. When Liege was taken they withdrew to Louvain, Malines and the Forts of Namur. When this last stronghold fell- with a loss of 14,000 Belgian soldiers, the remaining 12,000 retreated through France, shipped from Havre to Ostend, and rejoined the army in Flanders. A United Nation.

And again, when Brussels, their capital, was captured, there was no thought of sur- render ; the army retreated, step by step, to Antwerp. They hesitated not to destroy their dykes and devastate their lands /by flood to keel; back the foe. Full credit has been given to Belgium for the initial resistance at Liege, but not enough for her continued resistance afterwards, when she still fought alone. After the fall of Liege our thoughts ,were so centred on the advance on Paris that little attention was paid to the struggle in Belgium, where, field by field, village by village, the advance of the foe was disputed. Termonde changed hands twice, at Malines the foe was beaten back three times, and it was bombarded five times, and all this when not a single French or English Army Corps was within striking distance.

When will their glory fade? "4Honour the stand they made!

Nor was it entirely a question of national independence with the Belgians. They grasped the truth that there was something greater involved than even their own national existence; they recognised that this was a crusade of national civilisation, of eternal right, against brute force. Nor was it alone the heroism of King Albert and his associates. Unless the same spirit had animated the army and the nation, the dispiriting conditions under which they fought the rearguard action in their retreat before the Germans, they cer- tainly would have yielded. To the coarse German mind Belgium committed national, suicide—but she saved her soul alive. In so doing Belgium laid us and the world at large under an obligation which should be honour- ably acknowledged, and no effort can be too great to succour, to heal and to restore. Not for long must Germany be allowed to press his brutal hoof on this courageous nation. Succour Due From Us.

It may appear a sudden descent from the realms of high idealism to the practical ques- tion of the homing of Belgian Refugees in this locality. But the thoughts which come from the higher reaches should find an abiding place in our minds and supply the power to face the practical problems which arise. With such a fount of inspiration the difficulties and disappointments disappear. I would not under-rate these difficulties. The conditions are of that character that such are sure to arise. We have the fact that the Belgians are dowered with about as much original -sin, ignoraire, and prejudice as a corresponding lot of our own people. We group different families together in the same home as best we can, but, after all, it is a haphazard ar- rangement. We expect them to settle at once and manifest a harmonious home life. These separate families have to adopt a common method of life, however much this may differ from what they are' accustomed to; to share with strangers the same common rooms' eat at the same table live the same life. Was there ever such a fertile soil for the growth of trouble ?

In a very little cottage one family will do, There never was a castle that was big enough for two.

Then again there are the problems of workers' contributions, catering,, pocket money allowance, clothing, and a hundred other things. It requires endless patience to cope with these, and to all is added the lan- guage difficulty, and different modes of thought and manner of life. ,It is only those who have endurance,. patience, and wisdom that ought to engage in this work. But the work is well worth doing, and very interest- ing. In the great majority of cases the doer is cheered by the gratitude, and often by the love of the people helped; but if not, then, what matter? If the consistent performance of our national and humane duty is made dependent on expression of gratitude from in- dividual lielgians, then we may expect cases of failure and disappointment.

If Not Lodgings, Then Money. In addition to our obligation to those who have .sought shelter on our shores, there is the pressing need of succour to' the much larger number who have been unable to leave Belgium. The pitiful condition of these, many of whom are on the verge of starvation, makes an imperative demand upon our assist- ance, and I should very much like to see a fund started in our town, and the proceeds to go to the national effort. Both of the need and of our obligation to meet this need there is no question.

Belgium.

lleartstruek she stands—Our Lady of all

Sorrows— Circled with ruin, sunk in deep amaze; Facing the shadow of her dark to-morrows, Mourning the glory of ,her yesterdays.

Yet is she queen, by every royal token, There, where the storm of desolation swirled; Crowned only with the thorn—despoiled and broken— Her kingdom is the heart of all the world.

She made her breast a shield, her sword a splendour, She rose like flame upon the darkened ways; So, through the anguish of her proud surrender Breaks the clear vision of undying praise. MARION COUTIWITY SMITS.

His Idea of Politeness.

"I hate this politeness," said a small boy, as the company filed into the dining-room and left him to feast on a picture book; "it makes zue furious."

"What is politeness?" asked one of the guests, as a quiz.

dearest owing to the foul crime of the enemy and that they were, for the time being, mad and blind with rage. "That," the Doctor added, "is not the usual temper of the English- man. Do forgive the outburst. Deplore it. and say that it must not happen again; but forgive it this once. The provocation was great! (Hear, hear.) I have lost some who were .near and dear to me through the sinking of the great ship. Died An Englishman's Death.

The last that was seen of one i'man was that he was giving his lifebelt to a woman. That man had only one lung; he had no chance at all after a cold submersion, and to-day he is dead, but he died an Englishman's death. This latest and crowning crime must, surely, make all Englishmen realise what it would mean were they to submit to being governed by a government that was capable of such a heinous offence as the torpedoing of a defence- less vessel. So far, thank God, our men's hands are clean! Let us keep them so!" (Cheers.) The country, the speaker continued, could not do what she had to do unless she had more men and more munitions of war, and she could not have the necessary men and the necessary munitions unless the women were willing to do their part. And that part was going to be no easy one! Potential Saints—and Real Ones!

The Doctor went on to talk of her work among the wounded " Tommies," and said: "They are not only Potential saints, but I have seen those who are actual saints, and I want to give the lie to the wretched slanders which are being poured forth about our men. (Cheers.) The British Army, of course, has its black sheep, but I say that, taken as a whole, a finer body of honourable men have never been put into the field." (Renewed cheers.) The speaker went on to contend that unless their country kept up a steady flow of men—greater and steadier even than the flow had already been—the Germans had a chance. War was very solemn, and one needed to be out among it to know what it really meant. This war involved carnage of the most hellish type, and it was not putting it too strongly to say that the Allies had yet to wade through seas of blood before Peace spreads her wings over Europe once more. The sights of war were awful in the extreme— young men of nineteen blind for life; young men of twenty-one with both legs off; young men Of twenty-four minus their arms; young men of all ages absolute wrecks of their former selves—and all this in order that the homes of England might be kept free from a ruthless enemy; that the women of this land should not be subjected to the horrors worse than death which had befallen the hapless women of .Bel- gium.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WOMAN'S PART.

Ways In Which She Can Help To Win The War.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK THAT WILL RELEASE MEN FOR SERVICE.

Men Must Not Sh

There is one particularly fine thing about the British Army, and that is that every man who has gone to the Front and every man who is going has done so, or is going to do so of his own free will; of his own choice. The men have done finely, and now the women are to have a chance of doing equally finely, for it is being made possible for them to take up much of the necessary work oi the country so that "more men and still more men" may be able to go to the Front. And, just as the men who have gone out have gone voluntarily, so are the women being asked to volunteer to carry on the work at home. And,surely,the women of England are not going.to be behind the men in doing what they can for their country; surely the women of Warrington are not going to be behind the men of Warring- ton.

These words were uttered in the course of an eloquent plea made by Dr. Phmbe Sheaveyn— senior women's tutor at Manchester University —at a mass meeting held in the Parr Hall on Monday evening, at which the various speakers explained the Government's scheme of war work for women, and made a spirited appeal to the women of Warrington to enrol them- selves for work should their services be required in order to release more men for military duties. The meeting was largely at- tended, mainly by women, of all classes, and the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock)—who had convened the gathering—presided, being supported by Drs. Plicebe Sheaveyn and Mary Davies (who until recently was attached to the military hospital at St. Jean de Luiz, Boulogne), Aldermen David Tinnion and Smethurst, Colonel Mocatta (who has charge of local recruiting), Captain Williams, Mr. W. P. Rylands, Mr. Hallaway (deputy town clerk) and others.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVILEGE WOMEN MAY ENJOY.

They Can Help To Avenge Lusitania Victims. In the course of a brief introductory speech, his Worship said:- 1 have never addressed a public meeting be- fore with a greater sense of the responsibility of my position. lam speaking not only to those who are present here to-night, but through you to all the women of Warrington, and I want each and every one of you to play the part of a missionary and help to bring home to every one of your friends the great and noble task in which the women of -.England are privileged to engage. You all know the great crisis through which we are passing, how the flower of our manhood to-day is engaged on the battlefields in Belgium and the East, every man nobly playing his part for us and ours, and everything we hold dear. - Each one of us knows full well that this is no easy task, but one which requires, and I am proud to say willingly obtains, the best blood of the nation. (Hear, hear.) Never before in the proud history of our Empire have we witnessed such a wonderful coneensus of opinion, or such concerted effort. But we want men and still more men. and to enable them to serve their country either on the battlefield, or in the ranks of organised in- dustry, substitutes must be found, and it is to the Women of • England we appeal. to the mothers, wives and sisters of those lads who are or will be engaged in the sterner duties of war.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
" Dastardly and Diabolical Piracy."

It is only by organised effort .we can beat our inhuman foe. England to-day stands aghast at the tale of the barbarous warfare exercised by our enemies. Only this week-end the whole world has been plunged into gloom by that dastardly and diabolical act of -piracy —the sinking of the Lusitania. It may not be to-day—it may not be to-morrow—but the time will as surely collie, as the night follows day, that the responsibility for all these wanton: and Wicked murders will be brought home, and on that day the penalty will be exacted to the full. (Hear, hear.) I appeal to you to help in this great work. I appeal to you for the sakes of our lads now in the trenches—(cheers)—and lastly in the name of, the women and the little babies on board that ill-fated ship—little chil- dren a few short hours ago playing happily in the sunlight, now lying stark and cold, silent witnesses calling you and me and every one of us to avenge them—and shall that cry fall on deaf ears—surely the women of England %Till answer "No." (Applause.)

UNPRECEDENTED LABOUR SHORTAGE. Urgent Work That Women Must Undertake. Captain Williams, who is connected with the Dockers' Battalion at Liverpool, said that at the commencement of the war it was antici- pated that the country would have to face serious unemployment, and, perhaps, one of the most extraordinary and surprising features of the war had been that not only had there been no depression of trade, but, on the contrary, there had been a ,tremendous boom in trade, with the result that never was there a time when there was less labour available than at the present. That, of course, was directly traceable to two causes; firstly, the fact that two or three million men had been called upon for military service, and, secondly, to the fact that there was an abnormal demand for war munitions and materials. As a result of the draining process that had gone on amongst the ranks of the workers, the country was to-day short of labour of the most vital description— short of just that labour that was necessary to them if they were to carry on and emerge triumphant from the present war. At the same time, also, the country was, undoubtedly, short of men for the Front.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
Women's Chance To Play A Greater Part

The Board of Trade was spending practically the whole of its time trying to cope with the problem of the shortage of labour—a shortage which gave the women of England a chance of playing *their part in the war. (Applause.) Perhaps, he ought to say that they now had the chance of playing a more prominent part, for tlt?3, had already accomplished much that was useful. (Applause.) The Government wanted, as speedily as possible, to get a census of women who were able and capable of working, should the need arise, at work at present done by men. They wanted the women to come for- ward and register their names and say that they would be prepared, if their country needed them, to do what they could; to do their best,

===== 0002601_19150515_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
rk—Nor Women !

in fact, to help her. Any employment that was offered would be employment at a wage, and, what was more, at a fair wage. There was to be no question of cheap labour; no question of undercutting men's wages. (Hear, hear.) The best trade union regulations would be kept in mind, the idea being that there should be an equal wage where equal value was given. (Ap- plause.) There were two classes, in particular, to whom the appeal was addressed, viz., to the women of the leisured class, and to the mar- ried women who in the past had worked for their living and who were now fit to resume their places for a time in the industrial army.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
Where Women Can Be Of Use.

The speaker went on to dwell upon directions in which female labour could be utilised to advantage, Mentioning, among other occupa- tions which they could undertake, lighter farm work, office work, shop work, &c. He also mentioned that various schemes for training the women volunteers were being evolved, and added that women and girls who offered their serviCes must not be discouraged if they were not offered a job. immediately they registered. One caution he desired to administer, and that was that women . should not hand in their names unless they meant to go through with the business. If they came forward they must stick to it. And, moreover, if the work they did displaced men, they must be prepared to relinquish that work at the end of the war, so that the men who had been out to fight for their country might have their jobs back when the fighting was all over. That, of course, was only fair. (Hear, hear.) He believed that the necessity for Organising all the industrial resources of the country was of paramount im- portance at the present time; indeed, he did not believe they could, successfully prosecute the war unless they organised and husbanded the labour resources of the country in every possible way. Industrial Conscription.

"Before we are through with this business " said the Captain, by way of conclusion, "I am certain we shall have the same sort of system applied to other industries as has been applied to the dockers in Liverpool. I belong to the Dockers' Battalion, and I am proud of it. In it, we have a battalion of working men organised to get the maximum output, with- out any waste." (Applause.)

WOMAN'S GREAT OPPORTUNITY. How She May Repay_The_Soldiers For Their

Great Sacrifices.

Dr. Sheaveyn then addressed the meeting, being accorded a splendid reception. The women of England knew now, she said, some- thing of what their men were suffering on their behalf. And they knew, too, that that .suffer- ing was endured in order to save them from the even worse suffering that women in Belgium and in France had had to undergo. They had read in the papers how brave English lads had had almost unbelievable tortures inflicted upon them—deliberate, calculated cold-blooded tor- tures—and they had during the previous day or so read of the cowardly murder of nearly 1.500 absolutely defenceless men, women and children. The women of England had safety— bought for them by their mankind at a terrible price--,and the question they had to'. put to themselves was what were they going to 'pay for that safety?. Were they going to accept it as something to which they were entitled and go on their way without doing their share? She thought not! She had heard of lots of men who said "I wish I could go; but I can- not, for my work must go on." And that was true.

Work Must Go On And Women Must Do Their Share.

The work, must go on, for what was the good of defending a • nation if that nation was starving or was :not carrying on its lifThere must be food, there must be clothillW, and there must be ammunition. And if the women did not come forward and help to carry on the .necessary industries of the country, it meant that men who might be at the Front, helping to bring the war to as speedy and successful a conclusion as possible, would have to stay at home. Another point that should be borne in mind was that every extra man sent to the Front *helped in the saving of the lives of some of the other men there. The work which women were now being asked to take up had, perhaps, little of the glamour of glory or romance about it and, therefore, all the more honour was due to the women who undertook it. (hear, hear.) What Will The Answer Be?

It meant discipline; it meant drudgery; it meant routine; it meant, in all probability, the leaving of home and the going to unfamiliar places; it meant the giving up of a life, in which, mayhaps, there was much leisure for a life that was a long round of hard work. But were any of those considerations going to deter the women of England from doing their plain duty? Not likely In France and Russia nearly all th 4 able-bodied men were bearing arms, with the result that the women were managing the shops, working in the factories and carrying on the farms; indeed, they were doing nearly all the work. The Women of England had a marvellous chance of showing the stuff they are made of. They had their opportunity now. What were they going to do with it? She thought she knew what the answer would be.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0212.txt | included=true | included =====
GERMANY'S CROWNING CRIME.

Lady Doctor and the Liverpool Lusitania Riots. Dr. Mary Davies, who belongs to Liverpool, prefaced a telling speech—which was fre- quently punctuated by outbursts of applause— by referring to "the crime of the Lusitania." Nine-tenths of the crew of that ill-fated vessel, she said, belonged to 'Liverpool, and it was well to bear that fact in ,mind when in the morrow's .papers they would read of the happenings that had taken place in the great Mersey port that day. They were happenings which they, in their calmer moments, would all deplore. She, that day, had witnessed the demolition of two shops, had seen the contents trodden under foot and .the furniture thrown from the, windows and smashed to pieces. The spectacle was not one on which English people liked to dwell, but she would ask them, of their charity, to remember that some of the People concerned had lost their nearest and

===== 0002601_19150515_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
. THE WORLD AGHAST. Germany Goes From Crime To Crime.

Mr. W. -Peter Rylands . moved a vote of thanks to the Mayor for presiding, and to the speakers, and, in doing so, said that if there was anything that could strengthen the appeal that was being made that evening it was the sinking of the Lusitania. The whole world stood aghast at the horrible crime. But, with the germans, crime followed hot foot on crime. They thought in the days of the horrors in Belgium that the cup of horror and iniquity was already full to overflowing, but since then Germany had gone from excess to excess. The sacking of a town by soldiery in the hot-blood of the fight might, perhaps, be understood if it could not be condoned, but the torpedoing of a hapless defenceless vessel, be it fishing smack or liner, could not. There was some- thing about this species of crime that was so unnecessary, so unmanly that the world out- side Germany stood aghasf. It was difficult to believe, with the record of German warfare be- fore him, that any young man capable and strong enough to bear arms could stay at home in comparative ease. (Hear, hear.) .

===== 0002601_19150515_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
00 WE REALISE?

Alderman Tinnion's Tribute To The Navy. Alderman Tinnion, in seconding, said that for some considerable time he had been think- ing% that because of their comparative safety— because of their supposed immunity from the worst horrors of war—the people of this country did not properly realise the terrible position in which she was placed. He went to Frodsham the other day to see a nephew of his who had been wounded and was now in hospital there, and in speaking to some of the other brave fellows who were also lying there he. asked the question "Do you want to go back?" The reply he received was "We don't want to go, but we are ready to go if it is necessary." (Applatise:) Had it not been for their gallant Navy, the Alderman went on, he and the other speakers might not have been there addressing that meeting; it would, in all probability have been impossible for them to meet together in the comparative comfort and with the sense of protection that they, as a matter of fact, enjoyed. To those at home— secure and comfortable--the horrors of war, as experienced on thè Continent, were almost un- thinkable; it was impossible to realise a half; nay, a quarter, of what the womenfolk and children of Belgium and part of France had had to endure.

I'll Never Respect a Man If -----. "It is terrible," the speaker continued, I'll neverrespeet a man—Ltlon't care who he is— if he does not Offer to go and fight for his coun- try, provided lie is of military age, and is fit, or unless he is producing something which may be asanecessary for the carrying on of the war as would be his presence at the Front. I'll never respect a man who shirks—even so little as one inch—from doing his duty. I have a Ind who has gone and eight of my nephews went. (Cheers.) One of them has been killed, and two have been wounded. If I had twenty sons I would send every one Of them." The men of England; aye, and the men of Warrington, had done splendidly, but there were still many left behind—young men, who ought to be ashamed to walk the streets. (Hear, hear.) There were many men who could go and had not gone; many men who ought to be made to go. There were other men who would willingly go, but remained ,behind because they felt that they were doing here work that was as important and vital as that which was being done by the men at the Front.

The Women's Task.

REGISTRAR OF WOMEN WORKE ES: "What can I -do for APPLICANT: "You probably want a forewoman; brought my husband to speak for me."

It was the women's task to release those men. (Hear, hear.) Some of those men- had said to "I have almost given up going out because I cannot go to the Front." Some were phy- sically unfit; others said, "I am afraid to go out; I could not be spared." If such did. their duty conscientiously at home, they were doing their "bit." As to the women, they could not do all the work that was so necessary at home, but they could do their share of it, and, if they .would volunteer to do what they 'could, they were doing all that, was expected of them. SPEED CALCULATION.

One frequently. hears it stated that it is possible to calculate the speed one is riding by counting the telegraph poles as they. are passed. This is ;perfectly. correct, .but .as a general rule riders are under a misapprehen- sion as to the distance separating the poles. saw it stated recently in a letter to. a cycling contemporary, that the poles were. generally 92 yards apart. This is quite incorrect. I have taken the trouble recently to find out what rule is followed by the G.P.O. as regards the weight bearing capacity of .the .various types of telegraph' poles, or, in other words, what distance apart they are erected. The. exact number of yards varies slightly, but the follow- ing is very near the mark :—Cin main ,roads, when carrying 60 or more wires, on double poles, they are about 50 yards aPart. Heavy single poles for 50-60 wires are equi-distant. Sixty yards are allowed between poles carry- ing 30-50 wires, and 65-70 yards for poles with less than 30 lines. Poles running across coun- try and only bearing a couple of wires, may be as much as 90 yards apart.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
VALUE OF GEAR CASES.

" Once used, always used," are words which conunonlydappear in .a large number of adver- tisements. The same might be said of a gear case, for, in my opinion, it is particularly true in this instance. It is impossible to say that this addition is a necessity, but it is certainly advantageous. With regard to gear cases the expression "cheap and nasty" may be applied. If one is to be fitted it musk, be tip-top .in quality, otherwise instead of being an advan- tage it will simply become a nuisance. The chain of a cycle requires careful attention, if sweet running .is to be experienced. RR uncov- ered chain will quickly pick up dust and grit, both of which militate against smoothness of action. For an all-weather mount a ,gear case becomes almost a necessity, since the dirtiest weather cannot affect the working of this part of the machine. The pleasure of riding is en- hanced, and the labour entailed in cleaning is decreased very considerably. If once tried, my belief is that a gear case will always be used.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0219.txt | included=true | included =====
ROAD COURTESIES.

Much his been written about road hogs with reference to motorists, but cyclists should ask themselves whether the same term should not be applied to some of those who ride an ordinary push bike. I have had- this in my mind-for some time; I mention it, now since I have received two or three letters on the subject from riders themselves. I- do not think the road hog cyclist is present to the same extent in London and other large cities, as he is in small, provincial towns, for the reason that the regulations respecting traffic are more firmly enforced in large centres of Population. Just a day or two ago I had an instance of road hoggism.. I was riding along at an average pace when ti cyclist overtook me and deliberately cut right across" my track, without indicating by any sign what his inten-

THREE-SPEED GEARS.

The introduction of the three-speed gear has proved of very great value in the case of the cycle and 'the motor-car. it is equally valul able when fitted to a motor-cycle. The number of machines now being fitted with a variable gear is increasing daily, and I do not think it will be very long before it .1.3 adopted on all motor-cycles. This system increases the range of the machine very greatly, and this is par- ticularly with low powered engines. A three- speed gear will enable a small horse-power engine to perform practically everything it is likely to be called upon to accomplish under ordinary, circumstances. With a medium powered engine, fitted with a coach-built side- car, stiff gradients cap be negotiated with care. For Tiding in congested streets the use of a low gear is very advantageous, and adds greatly to the comfort and safety of the rider., The strain, moreover, on the engine is less when a variable gear is employed. The three-speed gear does as much for the motor-cycle as for the ear, and more than for the cycle.:

===== 0002601_19150515_art0224.txt | included=true | included =====
AVERAGE SPEED.

Whenever I hear motor cyclists talking largely alitut the average speed they have maintained on certain fairly long runs, I knock off 25 or 50 per cent. It won't be long before riders attain the unenviable position now held by fishermen, with regard- to t 4 veracity of their statements. I do not say it is impossible to coVer upwards of IX miles in threee hours, but I do not believe thaNhis is accomplished very frequently. Tak- ing all in all, it takes a good deal to average 25 miles an hour for four or five consecutive hours. have done it myself, but it means that every Minute •is of importance, .and there is no time for dawdling on the road. Quite apart from all questions of the danger to other read users. a high: speed means two things. The real pleasure of motor cycling is done away with, and the engine will rapidly be worn out by such a continued strain. On good roads the wear and tear would be considerable; the present condi- tion of the highways would increase this enor- mously. Under suitable circumstances a burst

of speed will do no harm. It is the continued strain that does the harm.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0225.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CAPACITY OF SPARKING PLUGS.

The electrical capacity of sparking plugs is a subject that has come in for a large amount of discussion during the past few weeks. The sug- gestion is that it would be an improvement to make the capacity of the sparking plugs greater. This may be proved to be correct, and it is not at all impossible, but the most important point to be borne in Mind is not so much their effi- ciency as the simplicity of their construction. Present day sparking plugs are reliable, and this is due to a large extent to the fewness of the parts employed. There can be but little doubt that trouble will be encountered if addi- tions are made. To add on a condenser, to give an extra spark gap, or a multiplicity of insula- tors will detract from the simplicity and reli- ability of the sparking plug as we know- it to- day. There is not much difference between the various best makes advertised, no matter what are claimed for them by the manufacturers, since they are, all reliable and simple in struc- ture.

MOTOR SIGNALLING.

It has been felt for a long time that it would add to the safety of our traffic-laden streets it some simple form of motor signal was placed on the market. When one car is following an- other, the driver of the second is at a loss to know what are the intentions of the first. Hand signalling is all very well, but not being auto- matically worked valuable seconds may elapse before the sign is given. I bear that at last a good signal is to be placed on the miarket, and if_ it answers as well as the makers state, it should be adopted 'generally. Two white arrows on a red ground, pointing left and right respectively, enclosed in a box at the rear of the car, which are controlled by two switches similarly marked on the steering wheel, make up the whole mechanism: A touch on the switch brings the desired arrow into prominence. A more com- plicated outfit has also been designed, which in addition shows the words "slow," actuated by the clutch and footbrake, and "Stop," con- trolled .by the hand brake. Either of these should fill a long-felt want.

GERMAN ATROCITIES.

Massacres By Order.

TERRIBLE RECORD OF RAPINE AND MURDER.

,s The most appalling record of modern times 1 that contained in the report, issued on Wed- nesd the ay night, of the Committee appointed by , British Government to inquire into the aile 1-, ged German outrages in Belgium and ranee. ,

One had hesitated to, believe that troops of 11/13, civilised nation could be guilty of bar- Qarities such as were reported; but this official TePort—signed by men of such authority as •,-,4"°rd 13ryce, Sir Frederick Pollock, K.C., Sir xidward Clarke, K.C., Sir Alfred Hopkinson, Sir Kenelm Digby, K.C., Mr. H. L. .'Sher (Vice-Chancellor of Sheffield Univer- IPY) and Mr. Harold Cox—shows that the hhtfulness alleged has been proved to the ilt.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0233.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT HAS BEEN PROVED.

4. The Committee, indeed, definitely declare it proved:— That there were in many parts of Belgium deliberate and systematically organised by of the civil population, accompanied ny many isolated murders and other outrages. , That in the conduct of the war generally uMocent civilians, both men and women, were nlurdered in large numbers, women violated, and children murdered. That looting, house burning, and the wanton destruction of property were ordered and coun- tenanced by the officers of the German Army, that elaborate provision had been made for sYstematic incendiarism at the very outbreak of the war, and that the burnings and destruc- tion were frequent where no military necessity could of be alleged, being imileed part of vystem Aittutertablterrorisation. clue/My be r Jules and usages of , war were fre- Civilian K,en, particularly by the using of Shield f ',lll,7llding women and children, as a a less °,4.a'vancing forces exposed to fire, to Prisoners 'sree by killing the wounded and Red Cross ' and in the frequent abuse of the and the White Flag.

THE CONSCIENCE OF MANKIND. The f th rePort proceeds :—Sensible as they are 43_ . gravity of these conclusions, the Com- !]?.ittee conceive that they would De doing less "lan their duty if they failed to record them as fully established by the evidence. Murder, lust and pillage prevailed over many Parts of Belgium on a scale unparalleled in any War between civilised nations during the last three centuries. ~Tht Committee express their belief "that "lege disclosures will not have been made in /rain if they touch and rouse the conscience of Illankind, and we venture to hope that as soon as the pre4ent war is over. the nations of the World in council will consider what means can ,ne provided and sanctions devised- to prevent recurrence of such horrors as our genera- (ion is now witnessing."

THE DRAW FOR THE SECOND 'ROUND.

The draw for the second round of the County Club Championship took place in Manchester on Monday evening and as a result of it the Warrington Cemetery Hotel team will play the Flixton Roebuck Inn combination on the green of the Prestwich Liberal Club to-day, whilst the Warrington Bull's Head Club will meet the Prestwich Conservatives on the Roe- buck Green at Flixton.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
RUGBY FOOTBALL SEASON.

Warrington Suffers From The War, But Make Brave Show.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
PATRIOTIC RECORD,

Mr. T. P. Bradshaw (hon. sec. of the Warring- ton F.C.) in his report for the season 1914-15, to be presented at the annual meeting of the members next Thursday, says that the first team played 32 League matches and finished 13th in the League table, compared with the 11th in the previous season, the record being as under :

For. Against. Per P. W. L. D. G. T. Pts. GA T. Pts. cent. 32 14 15 3 ... 43 52 242 40 81 323 48.43 The war had a very great effect upon the posi- tion occupied by the club in the League table, and in reviewing the season this should be taken into account. On many occasions the number of players at the disposal of the committee was only just sufficient to make up a team. This was caused by enlistments; he ventured to sub- mit that no club in the League has contributed more men to the colours than has Warrington. Prior to the -opening of the season the situa- tion was for from promising, and the com- mittee, in view of the gravity of the situation abroad, and after careful consideration, sub- mitted a resolution to the governing body recommending the suspension of the season. A special meeting or the League was called to con- sider the recommendation. It was decided, however, that the season should proceed, but that no county or international matches should be played. Under the extraordinary conditions, continues the secretary, in which our programme has been completed, the position of the club in the final table is better than at one time appeared possible, or that we had a right to expect

sincere thanks toi the members of the com- mittee and the club officials for their ready assistance.

SCORERS.

Goals were scored by the following players B. Jolley, 18; E. Brookes, 17; J. E. Critchley, 5; 3. Taberner, 5; J. Tranter, 2; J. Daintith, 2; J. Fearnley, 1; total, 50 goals; 100 points. Tries were scored by :—J. Tranter, 11; E. Brookes, 7; B. Bradshaw, 7; J. Fearnley, 7; J. Taberner, 4; H. Cox, 4; J. Baker, 4; T. Cun- liffe, 4; J. Daintith, 3; W. Cunliffe, 2; H. Higham, 2; S. Young, 1; R. Mather,, 1; M. Green. 1; J. Waywell, 1; J. W. Chesters, 1; A. Skelhorne, 1; T. Laws, 1; total, 62 tries; 186 points; total, 286 points.

ATTENDANCE OF COMMITTEE.

Meetings held, 47.
