THE SEASON'S CHAMPIONS. Huddersfield won the Northern Union Cup. Huddersfield won the Yorkshire League. Huddersfield won the N.T.T. League Cham- pionship. Huddersfield. won the Yorkshire Cup. Rochdale Hornets won the Lancashire Senior Cup. Wigan won the Lancashire League. Northern Combination: No championship awarded.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0237.txt | included=true | included =====
Lancashire Cup.

- We reached the semi-iinal Of the Lancashire Cu il Competition by disposing of Barrow and Salford in the first and second rounds. In the semi-final, the visitors, Rochdak Hornets, by an all-round superiority, won comfortably by 13 points to 5.

Northern Union Cup.

In the first round against Leeds the team gave a capital display, and one of the most ex- citing cup ties ever seen on the Wilderspool enclosure ended in a last-minute win by) 5 points to 4. The second round against Salford walk disastrous, the team failing to reproduce the form shown against Leeds, and the visitors ran out easy winners by 11 points to 2.

"A" Team.

The "A" team played only eight matches, the committee being compelled to withdraw from the Combination owing to the number of oppos- ing clubs cancelling their "A" team fixtures and our shortness of players. The record at the time of withdrawal was :

For. Against. P. W. L. D. G. T. Pts. G. T. Pts. 8 2 5 1 ... 2 10 34 ... 15 23 99

New Players.

Thirteen players, principally juniors, were signed during the season, the majority of whom since registration have enlisted. The latter fact (enlistment) proves the wisdom of the com- mittee in refraining from engaging players of more experience and at considerable expense.

Works Competition.

After careful consideration the committee de- cided not to run a Works Competition. As is well known, many of the works in the town are engaged in the manufacture of war materials, and many firms, through enlistments, are shorthanded, and the remaining men are working overtime, and the committee felt that it would be unfair to employers to attract the workers to night matches and neglect their em- ployment. For that reason the competition was abandoned, and the action of the committee met with the unanimous approval of the em- ployers in the town.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0238.txt | included=true | included =====
School Boys' League.

This'--League has again-been very .successful, ten schools competing, as against seven the pre- vious year, the winners being St. James'; run- ners-up, Arpley-street. - The abandonment of the Cup Competition was a great disappoint- ment to the Warrington .boys, as the Committee of the South-West Lancashire Cup Competition had decided, at the commencement of the season. that the competition should be sus- pended.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0239.txt | included=true | included =====
Intermediate League.

Seventeen clubs entered the League at the commencement of the season, but during the course of the season five teams withdrew. The championship was won by the Parochial Old Boys; runners-up, Bolton Old Boys. The League, owing to many circumstances, has not been as successful as anticipated, though bet- ter results are expected next season. Every effort will be made to encourage those boys who are desirous of continuing football after leaving school. The committee desire to ex- press 'to Mr. E. J. Mee and his colleagues their sincere thanks for their efforts in connection with the Intermediate League. They consider that excellent promise has already been shown, and that senior football will eventu- ally profit from this movement.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
Benefit Match.

The benefit match voted to A. Naylor and E. Jordan at the last annual meeting had to be postponed, and will be played when cir- cumstances will allow.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0241.txt | included=true | included =====
Ground Purchase.

As formally mentioned at the last annual meeting, the freehold of the ground has been purchased and is held by trustees for the members of the club. -

===== 0002601_19150515_art0242.txt | included=true | included =====
St. John Ambulance Association.

The committee again desire to place ,on re- cord. their appreciation of the services ren- dered by the members of the above association to injured players.

Geneial.

One player was sent off the field and sus- pended for misconduct. No fines have been imposed for late starts or breach of rules.

The President (Councillor Osborne) was again the nominee on the Nathern Union and Lancashire .County Committees, and the thanks of the club are due to him for his services ott those committees.

The ground was selected for the semi-final tie Northern Union Cup—St. Helens and Rochdale Hornets. Financially, the match was a success, the total receipts being t2BB Os. Gd., and the attendance 9,268.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0245.txt | included=true | included =====
The War.

The number of players belonging to the club who have joined ihe colours since the outbreak of the war is 25, and the total num- ber of players belonging to the Northern *Union is 1,500. So far as the members and supporters of the club are concerned, there has been a ready response to the country's all, and, in a time of peril, players, members and supporters are nobly enkaged in a life-and-death struggle, bravely upholding the traditions of the nation in helping to defeat an unscrupulous foe, and fighting with a determination to bring the war to a successful issue. The .committee have decided that all mem- bers §erving with the Army or Navy, paying at the lower rate of subscription, will be allowed to continue at that rate on their re- turn; and, as the committee are desirous of completing a record of such members, they would welcome the names of anyone known to be serving in either brandies of .His Majesty's Forces. Membership.

The membership for the past season was : Life, 23; honorary, 116; ordinary, 772; total, 911.

The committee appreciate the action of the players in consen'-g to a voluntary reduc- tion of 15 per cent. ;r1 their playing terms, and desire to place on record the loyalty of the players throughout the season. ' In conolusi_n, I again wish to tender my

TREASURER'S "ANXIOUS MOMENTS."

===== 0002601_19150515_art0247.txt | included=true | included =====
The Question Of Professional Football During The War.

Mr. Thos. Pemberton (hon. treasurer), in his annual report, says:— . The past season has been full of anxious moments for your committee, and they feel that it would be disastrous to continue foot- ball on professional lines during the course of the war.

The net " gate " for the past season amounts to £.972 19s. 10d., against £2,527 19s. 11d. season 1913-14. Subscriptions are down £166 2s. These two principal source of income show a decrease of £1,721 2s. Id. The School Boys' competition and the Intermediate League com- bined have resulted in a loss of £B2 Bs. 6d., but there is no doubt that this money has been well spent. Depreciation has been fully allowed.

Your committee have endeavoured to meet these losses by carefully watching the expendi- ture, and considerable saving has been effected, with due regard to the maintenance of the ground and buildings. In- wages, refreshments, clothing, transfer fees, dele- gates' expenses, &c., there has been a saving of nearly £6OO, and there has been also a con- siderable reduction in field expenses, printing, stamps, match expenses, &c. The negotiations far the purchase of the ground were successfully carried out, and the committee recognised the efforts put forth by the Hoh. Secrettiry (Mr. T. P. Bradshaw). by conferring upon him the honour of a life membership. The thanks of the memberS are also due to Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., Ltd.. for the handsome manner in which they dealt with the club.

The profit and loss account shows that the receipts were £l,BOl 2s. Bd., including entrance fees and subscriptions £348 Gs., bar profits £36 10s., gate money (League and cups) 41.281 19s. 6d., Combination £32 10s. lld., practice matches £ll 125.• total £1,326 2s. 5d.; use of ground for semi-final £2B 16s. The expenditure included: Rent, rates and taxes, £357 18s. lid.; railway fares, £ll5 7s. 4d.; wages (players and attendants) and bonuses, £731 Os. 3d• ;. transfer fees and delegates' ex- penses, £194 is. 5d.; refreshments, £7l 10s. 4d.; interest of mortgage, £56 6s. Bd.; depreciation, £127 16s. 6d.: Intermediate League Competi- tion. £65 is. sd.

There was a loss of £521 10s. 9d. on the year's wo ;kings, which brought down the credit bal- ance from £2,316 17s. to £1,795 Gs. 3d.

LYMM AND LOCAL " TERRIERS" MEET.• In an interview wit h Lance-Corporal .Linley, of the sth Cheshires—which appears in our ,Lyiiim page—mention is made of the Warring- ton Territorials.

COMMISSION FOR MR. R. AMOS. - Mr. E. Amos, solicitor, of the firm of Messrs. Robert Davies and Co., Warrington, has been gazetted second-lieutenant in the 11th Battalion Royal Lancaster Regiment, and is now with his regiment in the South.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0249.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNTY PALATINE ARTILLERY.

Recruiting is agaii open for the County Palatine Artillery, Lord Kitchener having asked for a Aecond division. Lord Derby has written to the Mayor (Alderman Peter ,Peacock) asking him to bring the fact before the notice of the townspeople. _

===== 0002601_19150515_art0250.txt | included=true | included =====
" FAGS " FOR FIGHTERS.

One can imagine some flippant, Bernard Shawesque writer of the future describing the "Tommy Atkins" of to-day as "The Fag and 'Bacca Soldier." During .the last week-end quite a number of letters from local "Terriers" as well as from regulars, which Passed through our hands, contained appeals to the folk at home to send out the wherewithal for a smoke —" fags" being first favourites. "You've only got to light a "Woodbine "—(we make Messrs. W. D. and 11. 0. Wills a present of the advertisement)—says one of the gallant lads, and • it'll keep going jolly, Vhereas a pipe requires a lot of matches, and there aren't any automatic machines with wax testas in 'en f in the trenches."- ."A ht.'s the thing" writes another,. and a third describes how his comrades have tried to make their • own cigarettes, but with very indifferent .success. One puts his appeal on a higher plane; sci to speak, for he writes " For God's Sake, send us some Woodies "—Well: Tommy's • favourite brand of "fags " are still sold. in their green and gold wrappers at five a penny, and the Mayor's Tobacco Fund, is still open. Need more be said?

===== 0002601_19150515_art0251.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT SHALL WE DRINK?

Those who, in the .interests of the country have decided to. follow the aexamPle set- by the King in banishing wines, spirits and beer from the Royal Household during the war, will find an admirable substitute for their usual alcO- holic beverage in Barley Water made from Robinson's ". Patent " Barley.. Made accord- ing to the recipe of a famous chef (Mr. H. Hammond, M.C.A., formerly Chef de Cuisine, Bachelor's Club) it is at once .a .sustaining and delicious beverage :- "Putt the outside peel of two lemons into two quarts of water, add eight lumps of sugar and boil for ten minutes. To this add two dessert spoonfuls of Robinson's " Patent " Barley, Previously mixed to a smooth paste with a little cold water. Continue to boil for five minutes and allow to cool. When cool strain off through fine muslin and ad ice and lemon juice to taste." .

===== 0002601_19150515_art0252.txt | included=true | included =====
WITH THE WOODS.

More Matches hi First Round For " Fintayson " Cup. SOME CLOSE GAMES.

NOTES BY "THE JACK."

Last Saturday the weather was almost ideal for bowling and was in distinct contrast to that which obtained the previous week-end, when the season may be said to have formally opened. Bright sunshine and dry, fast turf brought out the bowling enthusiasts in their hundreds, and, so long as daylight lasted, the blithesome trundler was a-busy on the various local greens, save those which are controlled by the municipality and which will not be open for play until Whitsuntide, but which, by the way, judging from their trim appear- ance, should be found in first-rate condition when in a few days they are available for play. The Warrington Amateur Works' Bowling Association took advantage of the bright sunshine to make still further progress with the first-round games in the "Finlayson" Cup Competition, which, despite the war, promises to be as interesting and as popular as ever, the entry being a good one and the entrants as keen as ever on the good old game. Five cup-ties were played off on the Horse and Jockey green in Winwick-road, and four of them were pretty close affairs, but the fifth was something of a runaway match, Crosfield's Soap Department winning from Parkinson's by the substantial margini of 60 points.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0253.txt | included=true | included =====
ROPERY WIN BY TEN POINTS.

The closest contest was that between the Bewsey .Wire Ropery and Guest Brothers' Tannery, victory resting with the first-named by only ten points. . Seven of the rope-makers won their sets and had not two of their men "legged them down" pretty badly the Rope- men's margin of victory would have been a more substantial one. One of the victorious team who failed to shine was their captain, J. Looker, who found his master in the person of W. Graham. The individual scores were as follows:

ANGLING.

XX Xlt RINGTON XA7ORKINGAIEN'S ASSOC' ITION.

N.A. CENTRE.

That the way members are tied down during close season is not meeting with approval in Warrington, one need not go farther than the Gowy to see during a week- end. When conditions are ideal, there is a great falling off. However, protest is being laid by officials of most of the societies around War- rington. Let us hope that the'stringent rules will be lifted as soon as possible. Several members who paid the Gowy a visit last week-end were fortunate enough to get good sport, but the eel was the encumbrance. If this species could have been taken, those who visited the spot would have had at least several pounds each. A great number of perch were also hooked in the upper reaches near Little Barrow, one swim yielding a dozen in an hour's fishing. Then the member decided to go elsewhe, sd that he would not destroy any by hooking. Thu ranged.,fromioloz. to Boz., ' and we predia good sport in the open season with them, as they seem to be plentiful around here. The one great fault is, that there is not a special for perch caught from the Gowy in any of our Warrington Clubs.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0265.txt | included=true | included =====
Beech. 's Pills

MUNICIPAL HOUSING.

Objections To Block Tenements For AVarrington.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council, held in the'Vocialist Hall on Tuesday evening, the Secretary (Mr. Charles Dukes) 'referred to the discussion at the Town Council meeting the previous week rela- tive to the sending of delegates to a Town Plan- ning Conference at Liverpool. It was, said Mr. Dukes, a long time since the Trades Council first drew the attention of the councillors to the fact that there was a dearth of houses in Warring- ton. They were then told that private enter- prise 'would provide all that was necessary, but, as a matter of fact, private enterprise had done nothing of the sort. (Hear, hear.) Now, he was afraid, the town was to be persuaded to adopt something on the lines of the Liverpool rookeries; for he was afraid that rook( r-e's repre- sented the apex of the ideas of tJu local coun- cillors on the subject of housing. the workers must be housed let them be pad d one on top of the other in barracks That seemed to be the

Fair Rents and Fair Wages to Pay Them With.

He was not one of those who held that a municipal authority ought to supply houses at a rent which met the wage of the lower-paid workers, but he did think that they ought to supply them at a rent which was sufficient to Produce the sum necessary to repays the capital outlay at the Usual, rates allowed to munici- palities borrowing money. He did not want any form of subsidy, by which the higher-paid workers were made to help to pay the rent of the lower-paid Men. There would be no housing problem if the minimum wage paid to a worker was 30s. per week, instead of about 225., plus, nowadays, 'a small war bonus. They were not going to get out of the housing problem by building a few tenements. Are the Council Afraid?'

In his opinion, the Council were afraid that the force of public opinion might compel them to erect ordinary dwelling-houses that the better paid artisans could go into, leaving the houses— which they at present occupied, and which, in many cases, were little better than slum dwell- ings—for the lower-paid worker to occupy. The Council were afraid of municipal competition with the private property owners,- who to-day dominated the Council. If the Council did start any kind .of municipal housing scheme, the workers must see to it that the houses were Self- contained in character, fit to live in, and with healthy surroundings, and not block tenements. (Rear, bear.)

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 15, 1915.

ried to a Miss Smith, who resides in Thelwall- lane, and who has been terribly upset by the cruel blow. A Member of Latchford Conservative Club. Harold Newall received his school education at St. James', Latchford. He was first employed at the works of Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons, Ltd., but, at the time of the out- break of war, was engaged in the offices of the Warrington "Wire-rope Works, having in the interim gained his living for some while as a musician at one of the local picture palaces. He was a very talented pianist and violinist. The late Rifleman was well-known in the locality in which he lived. A member of the Latchford Conservative Club, and of the bowling team, he, enjoyed considerable popu- larity. The members of the club often had the benefit of his musical gifts. At billiards he was as keen as he was skilful. An allusion to the club was made by him in his last letter home. The letter read :—"Who do you think I saw yesterday ?—young P. Boardman, the footballer. He is in the Royal Irish Fusiliers. I bet the old town is the same as London and other places—quite miserable. Never mind, we shall have ,to liven things up when peace is proclaimed, which is not a great while off. There are plenty of my old chums here, and one or two Warrington lads as well. I expect things will be rather dull on the green this summer. Well, we may get back in time for the closing main. What a'do ' it would be! Remember me to all my friends at the old club." The father of, Rifleman Newall.is in the ser- vice of the Cheshire Lines Committee, as a joiner. Ile is engaged in the local goods department. The deceased soldier attended St. James' Church, and belonged to the Bibla Class con- ducted by the Vicar.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0283.txt | included=true | included =====
LUSITANIA SURVIVORS AT WARRINGTON.

Thrilling Interviews.

HOW A HAPPY VOYAGE ENDED IN DIRE CALAMITY,

===== 0002601_19150515_art0284.txt | included=true | included =====
BY HAROLD MAYBURY, Author of The Tulip Farms of Haarlem.

On Sunday morning last, whilst most people were abed, there arrived at Bank Quay Station a special train from Holyhead, conveying to their homes about 300 of the crew and passen- gers of the ill-fated Cunarder. It was unfor- tunate the hour was early, otherwise the news would have become more generally known, and a worthy reception accorded to the survivors. Through the kindness and courtesy of Mrs. Plank, of Lancaster, Mrs. Candlish, of Pres- ton, and a number of the crew, the following is narrated :-

A LIGHT-HEARTED COMPANY. New York harbour is fading gradually from sight, and the mammoth liner is under way. There .in the distance the gigantic Statue of Liberty towers majestically into the sky, the uplifted hand seeming to 'wish us God-speed on our journey. In spite of the warnings in the American Press, all on board were light- hearted and gay, for were not many of us journeying to the dear homeland, to see our own kindred, or our friends, and as most of us were Britishers, we placed our implicit faith in the seamanship and command of our captain. The second day at sea was beautifully fine, the sun shining up with great power, whilst the waters were untroubled. On deck were being played many games familiar to the ocean traveller, and every now and then prancing children of tender years would go by with their skipping ropes, whilst fond parents watched them at childish play. Yet through it all the gigantic liner ploughed her way, leaving a wake where the seagulls followed in its train, their shrill cries sounding through the clear air. So the hours passed on. The dawn of Friday, May 7th opened with a beautiful sunrise at sea, and many people, were on deck. Many had made acquaintances, and were Jelling each other who were to meet them or where they were journeying to, others were conversing on this terrible wa'r and its issue.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0285.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OMINOUS SHUDDER.

So the -hour of 2 p.m: approached when the majority of us went down to our mid-da.y meal. All were congratulating themselves on a suc- cessful voyage, when suddenly the boat shuddered. A Frenchman who sat opposite to Mrs. Plank said "We're torpedoed." No sooner, were the words uttered than an explosion took "Nee. Instantly we all rushed on deck to see what was the matter. The Lusitania still kept her course, although her Speed was diminishing. There was no panic on board, for the general impression 'prevailed that the vessel would not. founder. "Women and children first" was the cry as the boats were lowered.' It was a difficult task, as the boat was still moving..

===== 0002601_19150515_art0286.txt | included=true | included =====
A .TERRIBLE PERSONAL NARRATIVE.

Relating her experiences, Mrs. Candlish said: "As I stood on deck the scene was indescrib- able. One woman. was tearing the hair from her head, whilst two tiny mites were pulling at her dress. I never saw them again. _ _

"I became separated from my husband as I was pushed into one of the boats, which cap- sized owing to overcrowding. "All around the sea was strewn with human beings, many of whom had jumped from the sloping decks. "Many of those who were capsized in the first boat I never saw again, whilst I myself had a lucky escape. "I was jammed between two boats and bruised, but I still hung on to one of them until I was. pulled in. A few yards away I saw my husband clinging to an upturned boat, but we could not reach him. However, later, he was safely picked up' although•l did not know at the time. One boat I saw knocked over by a funnel of the Lusitania tts she heeled over and went down.

_ "The occupants were literally sucked down the funnel, and as the waters closed over her they were thrown out again.—

"HIT ME HARD."

Mrs. Plank, a Lancaster lady, whose age is over three scbre years, Raid: "I was very fortunate in being rescued, but the experiences I shall never forget. The. sea was beautifully calm. We picked up an old man who had been in the water for over an hour and a half. He was numb with the immer- sion, and kept repeating 'Hit me hard!' Hit me hard!' It was a scene I shall never forget. We fought for his life, but he was too far gone, and he died with his head between my knees." One of the crew, a typical British seaman, with curly hair and bronze countenance, who pulled Captain Turner into his boat, said he saluted his captain, who returned it. No words were spoken, for Captain Turner wns too exhausted. So ended one of the greatest maritime disasters of the age. None would have thought it possible that such a disagter could have occurred so close to the coast. There was none of the awesomeness of the night, with its bitter cold 'and mighty ice- bergs, as in the case of tke Titanic; instead, the sun cast its light o'er the, scene. This was not the workings of Nature inexplicable, but the deed of human beings who inhabit the earth;

===== 0002601_19150515_art0287.txt | included=true | included =====
IS LIBERTY A MYTH?

Out in the West still stands that Statue of Liberty, with its upraised arm, and one wonders whether or not it isn't a myth. Oh, the tragedy of it all!

===== 0002601_19150515_art0288.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW ABOUT THAT SUMMER

SUIT?

‘Vell-known Local Firm's Tempting

Offer.

Just now there is one question which looms large in the minds of a great many people, and that is "What about my new summer clothes; what style shall I have, what colour and what pattern?" This omnibus kind of question is occurring alike to many , men and to many women, and it is one that is capable of easy answer if one pays a visit to the establishment of Suits, Ltd., at 17, Iforsemarket-street. At that well-known clothing emporium one will find a huge variety of cloths to select from; in- deed, the customer may have the pick of over five hundred different materials, and he, or she, will be a difficult person to please if Suits Ltd's. stock is not found to include "just the right thing" for that suit or costume. Moreover, not only will you find that Suits Ltd.'s stock just the material you are looking for, but their prices, like their suits and cos- tumes, are the "real goods," eminently adapted to war-time purses. This enterprising firm undertake to fit a man out with a smartly-tailored suit at prices ranging from 20s. upwards—and one can hardly expect to be neatly dressed for less—whilst a lady can add to her wardrobe a neat and attractive tailor-made costume, which will be a joy of her heart, for so low a figure as 255. Not only—say Suits, Ltd.—will their prices be found all right, but they go further and guarantee their workman- ship, all their garments being designed to fit, to wear and to look stylish—and what more can the lady in search of a costume, or the man in need of a suit want?

Suits Ltd. not only array men and women in neat apparel at reasonable rates, but they are also by way of being benefactors to pater- familias, for they help him to solve the riddle, ever-recurring: "How to clothe our boys?" At prices ranging from 7s. 11d, up they retail good, durable and smart-looking suits for boys; just the sort of thing for school wear or for Sunday best. The firm also stock raincoats—most use- ful garments in our variable weather—in large variety and at prices which range from a sovereign upwards. The firm invite inspection, in sure and certain knowledge that once a prospective customer has seen the value on offer he becomes an actual customer.

Likes Low Voiced Women. Americans will be interested to know that Ring Edward thinks a sweet voice a thing to be prized and so much dislikes a woman with a loud voice that he refuses to allow her to be invited to houses where he is the guest, the list; of course, being always as he decides.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0289.txt | included=true | included =====
ON HIMOUR'S SCROLL.

LANCE-CORPL. H. WALL.

"Died As Good A Soldier As He Lived."

News was received on Monday morning that Lance-Corporal H. Wall, of "A " Company of the Ist Battalion Rifle Brigade, had been killed in action. The news was conveyed to his parents, Mr. and Mrs. J. H. Wall, of 118, Bostock-street, in a letter. written by a Second- Lieutenant belonging to the same platoon, and which ran as follows:

===== 0002601_19150515_art0290.txt | included=true | included =====
Death " Mercifully Instantaneous."

Dear Mrs. am writing to sym- pathise with you in the loss of your son, who was a Lce-Crp. in my platoon. I know that nothing I can say will bring him back, but you may be sure that he died as good a soldier as he lived. His death was mercifully in- stantaneous, and he was spared all pain. He was invaluable to me, and was shortly going to be promoted, for his cheerfulness and ability. I can only again sympathise with you in your deep sorrow, and I feel sure that he is happier now than he ever was, and died without knowing it.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0291.txt | included=true | included =====
Died in a Comrade's Arms.

The following letter was sent to the bereaved parents by Rifleman J. Norris:-- I am very sorry to inform you that your son, Harry, was killed in action on Monday, May 3rd, and I am requested on behalf of his comrades to send their deepest sym- pathy with you in your sad bereavement. ' I, myself, from the bottom of my heart feel very sorry for you, because I am sure he

was a good son. He always did his duty Very well, and was respected by everyone who knew him. I was with him when he was hit. He died in my arms, and one consolation is that his death was painless and instantaneous. I buried him. Two more Warrington lads—J. Carty and E. Morri•;—who were near at the time, send their 'deepest • sympathy. I Will forward his personal belongings in a day or two.

Shot Through the Head by a Sniper. A lady friend of the Wall family received a letter from her fiance, who is at the Front, in which he says that Lance-Corporal Wall was standing up in the trench to let a comrade pass, when he was shot straight through the head by a sniper.

Enlisted Last September.

Lance-Corporal Wall, who was 22 years of age -.and a single man, resided. with his parents. He enlisted on September 3rd, and, was at once drafted away for war training, since when his parents had not seen him. He went to the Front at the latter end of !January. He has cousin in the Army and another is a- sailor in the Royal Navy. The deceased formerly worked on a packet boat—the Pater—belonging to Messrs. Crosfield's, and plying between Warring ton and Liverpool. The deceased Lance-Corporal attended the Friars' Green Chapel.

RIFLEMAN H.

NEOALL.

Engaged To Be llarried.

News. was received on Sunday by Mr. and Mrs. George Newall, of 19, St. Mary-street, Latchford, that their only son, Rifleman Harold Neall, of the Ist Battalion Rifle Brigade, had been killed in action on Sunday, May 2nd.

The intimation came to hand in the form of a letter from C.Q.M.S. Horace Bradley. The letter was addressed to Rifleman Newall's little

• rtrierre,

sister, Ethel, who had a few days before en- closed a letter tO her brother in a parcel sent by Mrs. Newall. The message read as follows "A few days ago a parcel arrived addressed to Rifleman Newall, who, I regret to inform you, was killed in action on May 2. The con- tents of the parcel, being of a perishable nature, were distributed amongst his comrades, as is the usual custom in the company-. I wish to tender to you on ,behalf of the Men of the com- pany, their sincere regret and sympathy for the loss of your noble brother." The sad news was verified by a letter sent by Rifleman Edward Morris, to his relatives at 5, Hewitt-street, Latchford.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0293.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED.

LANCE-CORPORAL HARRY DOLAN.

Well-known Local Sportsman's Letter From Hospital.

Lance-Corporal Harry Dolan, of the sth Bat- talion King's Own Royal Lancaster Regiment (Territorials), youngest son of Mr. Joseph Dolan, of the well-known firm of Messrs. T. Dolan and Sons, builders, Napier-street, War- rington, who has been wounded in action, writes to his home at Westbourne, Lower Walton, from the Boulogne hospital, in which he is a patient. Luxury After the Trenches.

Having alluded to the nature of his injury—a shrapnel wound in the right side of the head— Lance-Corporal Dolan gives an interesting account of the routine of the hospital, so far as the convalescents are concerned. " 'Reveille,' " he says, "is sounded at 6.45, and breakfast is served an hour later. Those who are able do small jobs, such as Making up footpaths and attending to the general upkeep and decoration of the camp. Dinner is served at one o'clock, and tea at five; 'lights out' is signalled at nine o'clock. We sleep twelve in each large-sized double tent on camp beds—quite a luxury after having the bottom of a trench as a bed for the past three weeks. . . . Things have been pretty lively of late, and we have been amongst it with a vengeance. . . . We expect to parade before the commanding officer in the morning, when we shall learn where we are to be sent next." The letter concludes with the humorous remark: "I shall miss the post if I go on thinking out what will not be censored." Work and Play.

Lance-Corporal Dolan, who is well-known in the locality, enlisted in the Royal Lancaster Regiment early in September. He received quick promotion. Educated at St. Francis Xavier's College, Liverpool, he subsequently became a student of chemistry at the Manches- ter School of Technology. He is a member of of the Society of Chemical Industry. Entering the service of Messrs. Crosfield and Son, -Ltd., and working in their laboratory, he was after- wards appointed manager of the firm's Liver- pool works. Later, however, these works were closed down, and he then returned to Warring- ton and was given a manager's post in the re- search laboratory. An all-round sportsman, Lance-Corporal Dolan holds the captaincy of the Stockton Heath Hockey Club, and has on a number of occasions been included in the first XI. of the Warring- ton Cricket Club. His ability as a golf player is reflected in the fact that for the past two years he has held the Warrington Golf Club CUP. ' Lance-Corporal Dolan is a member of the Warrington Society. •

PRIVATE WALTER CAS WELL.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0294.txt | included=true | included =====
Lost An Eye, So Is "Like Old Nelson

Now."

News was received on Thursday of last week that Private Walter Caswell, a •Warringtonian who is now attached to "C" Squadron, 20th Hussars, had been wounded. A Shell-Splinter in the Eye.

Writing to his mother from No: 3 Surgical Ward, 3rd Southern Hospital, Oxford, the wounded man says : "I have only a little pain with iny wound now and again, and I am getting better very quickly. I was a very lucky man to get off so lightly. A Jack Johnson' burst about a yard and a 'half away, and a piece of it got me in the left eye, and smashed it to a pulp.

PRIVATE CASWELL. Wounded.

I had to undergo an operation in Boulogne Hospital, and have it taken out. So I am, something like old Nelson now. I get up a little each afternoon and stretch my legs, but I want to get nearer home, so that I can see you again.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0295.txt | included=true | included =====
The Mayor's ,Sympathy.

•On Thursday morning r. " and Mrs. Newall received the appended message from the Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) : " May I tender you my heartfelt sympathy at the great loss you have sustained by the death of your son? The knowledge that he gave his life for his King and country will, I am sure, make his name treasured amongst us. for all time. Trusting that you may be comforted from the source of all trite comfort.—Believe me, sincerely yours, _ -

"PETER PEAcocs (Mayor)."

No Leave.

Twenty years of age, Rifleman Newall enlisted early in September, and during the period of training that extended to January, when he de- parted for France, he was not once given leave to visit his home. He was engaged to be mar-

===== 0002601_19150515_art0296.txt | included=true | included =====
7` Finished My Soldiering Career."

"I think this has finished- my soldiering career; I know, at any rate, that it hasi finished my active service. " Hell On Earth" Round Ypres.

"It is afwful out there; indeed, round Ypres it is nothing but hell ,on earth, espe- cially now that the enemy are using poisonous gases. I have had just a slight taste of them. _

- "We are well looked after here, and can have anything we want. People visit us and bring us cake, fruit and cigarettes. You need not send me anything, only a good razor and brush, for I lost all my kit when I got wounded."

===== 0002601_19150515_art0297.txt | included=true | included =====
Seven Years a Soldier.

Private Caswell is a single man of 25 years of age, and his home is with his parents, at 13, Percy-street. After leaving St. Barnabas' School, he went to work at Monks, Hall and Co.'s Works, where he was employed as a roll-turner until the outbreak of war. He formerly worshipped at a local Wesleyan Chapel. He has been in the Army about seven years, and he was drafted to the Front on August 4th. Private Caswell has three brothers with the colours—one in the War- rington Territorials, one in the Royal Horse Artillery, and the other with the King's Own Scottish Borderers.

PRIVATE C. P. WILSON.

Wounded.

and at the base hospital, _going on well

Private Wilson wrote to' his wife on Wednes- day from an English hospital, saying that he was wounded in the head, and that he was to undergo an operation on Thursday for the removal of shrapnel: .

On the sth Of this month Private Wilson wrote to his wife:—

"I am," he said, "writing this letter to you in a little dug-out with shrapnel shells bursting all round us. We have just come down from the trenches after 21 days and we are all dead-beat. My feet are that sore I have been bathing them in a canteenful of cold water. I feel as if I could go to sleep and never wake up again.

"1 havien't received any letters from any- body for over a week, and I am beginning to think they are getting ' lost again. Tell the kiddies they must be good little girls until I come home again and don't forget to tell them that their daddy sends his best love to them.

"Ask Arthur Summerfleld if he has written to me yet. Remember me to Joe Davies and tell him I will soon be back in the shop. I am writing a letter to Mr. Virth (vicar of St. James') and am putting a piece of shrapnel in it that dropped on Billy Drury's letter when I was reading it, so if you want to see it you must ask him to show it you.

"I nearly stopped one this morning; it went right through my puttees, a shell bursting two yards off me, and the next two to me got hit." Private Wilson, who was well-known at the Liberal Club and in the Work's Football Com- petition circles, having played for Messrs. Fletcher, Russell's team, has a brother, John Wilson, serving at the Front. He has also

several brothers-in-law with the Colours.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0298.txt | included=true | included =====
RIFLEMAN G.

NEV ALL.

We're Giving The Germans Socks," Says A Wounded "Tomm3,"

Mr. and Mrs. Wm. NeWall. of 18, Miller- street, Warrington, have received news that their son, Rifleman George Newall. of the Rifle Brigade, has been injured at the Front A field postard bore the first message, and conveyed the comforting information that the injured man was in hospital and doing well. A letter followed, in which Rifleman Nowall gave an account of how he was injured. " Like An Earthquake."

"Just a line," he says, "to say we have had a big fight with the Germans. We have given them socks, but we have had plenty of casual- ties. . . . There was a terrific bombardment on both sides, just like an earthquake. The Germans lost heavily, and a lot of prisoners were taken. It's still going on as hard as ever. I don't know how Gosha has gone on. . . . A shell came over our trench and killed three and buried me and my mate. I am not seriously hurt; just going to hospital." Before the war broke out, Rifleman Newall was employed at Messrs. Richmond's as Stove and Meter Works, where be was well liked by his workmates. Ile was educated at St. James' School. His age was 22. Three Brothers In Arms.

Two other sons of Mr. and Mrs. Newall are at present in training for the Front. They are Lance-Corporal I. Nowall and Private Wm. Newa 11.

===== 0002601_19150515_art0299.txt | included=true | included =====
FIGHTING SHOPMEN.

Effort To Release A Larger Number.

MAYOR CALLS MEETING.

The Mayor has addressed the following letter to the wholesale and retail distributing trades in Warrington : "At the request of Lord Kitchener and the Home Office I am calling a conference of the wholesale and retail distributing trades in War- rington to consider how far and by what means it may be possible to re-adjust the conditions of employment so as to release a larger number of men of military age for voluntary service with the colours. I shall accordingly be very glad if you can make it convenient to meet me at the Town Hall on Tuesday, the 18th instant, at 8 p.m.

“Mr. F. W. Goldstone, M.P., of the Parlia- mentary Recruiting Committee, will attend and address the meeting, and knowing how vital it is at the present time that no effort should be spared to mobilise the national resources, both of men and material, I am sure that I may count upon your presence.—Yours sincerely, "PETER PEACOCK, Mayor. WHAT SHOPS HAVE DONE AND CAN DO. Speaking last week' in London to inaugurate a movement haring as its object the facilitat- ing of enlistment by men of service age who are engaged in the distributing trades, The Prime Minister dwelt first upon the pro- vision of women to fill the places of men who have enlisted and will enlist.' Roughly speak- ing the number of shop assistants who had joined the forces up to the middle of April was 260,000; another 220,000 men fit for military ser- vice still remained. If clerks, commercial travellers and other miscellaneous employes were included those figures would be approxi- mately 430,000 and 360,000. Fifty thousand women had registered themselves up to the middle of April. ,

PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS.

)NG PROGRAMME AT THE HIPPODROME.

There is a strong programme at the Hippo- drome this week. The best turn was that of Mdlle. De Dio, who gives a beautiful exposi- tion of prismatic dancing. The piece is a be- wildering feast of whirling light anil changing colour. Mdlle. De Dio's artistic performance is quite one of the best of its kind on the boards. The word-whistlink of Arthur Melrose is extremely good and immensely amusing; ilaley's Komets, a bevy of charming young ladies, delight the audience with their songs and dancing; Goodall and Knox are first-rate burlesque comedians, irresistible in their foolery. Thornbury., a brilliant lightning artist; Bobbie Thornton, .an accomplished card-manipulator; Roya and Rejane, novelty dancers; and the efficient and spirited orchestra, under Mr. Harold March, all con- tribute to the general enjoyment. The Gaamont Graphic, as usual, proves interesting.

"Big Turn" Next Week. The famous comedian, Ernest Shand—one of London's prime favourites—will top the bill at the popular Friars' Green house of amusement next week. The other artistes booked include the Caron Troupe of French and Belgian lady gymnasts, and Herbert La Martine, the novel dancer,, who will introduce his hvtert an cleverest staircase (lance. Others on thek,3-440; are Haigh and ITei-lbron„ in a sketch of the great North-West, entitled "The Straight Game"; Barrett and Elgar, in a new and 3 original vocal scene, "The Desert"; • Dess Desborough, the popular principal boy; and Delafo, a clover comedy juggler. Altogether the .programme bids fair to be a tip-top one.

DELIGHTFUL REVUE AT THE - ROYAL COURT. Irish and Proud of It" is the title 'of the revue being presented at the Royal Court Theatre this week, by Harry Tate, Joe O'Gorman and Wal. Pink's Co. Assuredly, the piece is Irish—lrish all over—and, just as emphatically, it is something to be proud of. It is an altogether delightful production, quite the most charming revue we remember to have , seen locally. Indeed, we are rather inclined to ' give it a better name than revue, which sug- gests " rag-time " and other incarnations of eccentricity. "Irish and Proud of It" is full of comedy and full of music, and with • little more of a plot about it might be termed a musical comedy. It is rich in sweet Irish music, in clever Irish characterisation ana dialogue. Mr. Joe O'Gorman is excellent as Patsy Branna a, whose conversations with Father O'Hea. (admirably portrayed by Mr. Breffin O'Rorlce) are delightfully humorous, and pointed with a good deal of truth. Mr. Chas. McConnell is ,good as Sergeant Hogan (R.1.C.), and there are some effective musical passages between Mr. Jas. Daly and Miss Mary Sweenby (as Tim Cassidy and Kathleen, res- pectively). The singing. of Miss Norah O'Gorman (as Eileen) is another popular feature. The whole company enters into its work as though it were its play, with the result that there is a swing and a spirit about the piece that is irresiitibly contagious, and makes it an • entertainment to be wholeheartedly recommended. -

War Play Next Week. "Joy—Sister of Mercy" is the title of a pow- erful war-play which is to he staged at the , Royal Court Theatre next week, by Mr. T. Edward Ward's Company, which includes that talented emotional actress, Miss Ward, in the title-role. The play is from the pen of Eva Elwes, who may be always relied upon to turn out a play that arrests the attention from the rise to the fall of the curtain, more than ono ot her melodramas having achieved phenomenal success.

Hl4; NoVELTY AT THE STAR

KINEVIA.

Next week patrons of this comfortable and popular picture house are to be introduced to one of the latest novelties in the cinema world, in the shape of Edison's Talking Pictures. The Kinetophone—the combined camera-cura- phonograph, through the medium of which the wonderfully lifelike effects are obtained—is a vast improvement of the old method of pro- ducing "talking-living pictures "; indeed it is one of the cleverest inventions of this age ef invention.

NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that, owing to many of the Medical Practitioners on the Warring- ton Panel having consented to assist the Military Authorities in the treatment of Wounded Soldiers at the Lord Derby Military Hospital (shortly to be opened at Winwick), it will be necessary to alter and probably curtail Surgery Hours. Such altera- tions will be displayed at the Surgeries. Messages for visits should, if possible, be sent to the Sur- geries not later than 9 a:m. Insured Persons and. the Public generally will doubtless realise the importance of relieving the Doctors as much as possible' under the abnormal circumstances, which will be occasioned by the opening of the Hospital. RIFLEMAN H. NEWALL. • Billed.

RIFLEMAN W. RICHARDSON. In Hospital At Folkestone. News was received by Mrs. J. Richardson, cf 113, Wellington-street, Warrington, on Wednes- day, that her son, Rifleman Wm. Richardson, of the Rifle Brigade, has been wounded. On Thursday there came a postcard from the Rifleman, saying that he was in Manor House Hospital, Folkestone, and getting on well. Rifleman Richardson was employed at Messrs. Richmond's Works up to the time a the outbreak of war, when he enlisted in the 2nd Battalion of the Rifle Brigade, as a member of Kitchener's Army. As a boy he attended Wycliffe Day and Sunday Schools. He has a married brother in the Grenadier Guards—Private Charles Henry Richardson, who, has a family of six children; he went through the Boer War. Rifleman Richardson is 36 years of age and is a single man who, in time of peace, lived at home with his widowed mother. The late Mr. John Richardson, Rifleman Richardson's fathei, was a wire-drawer at the Whitec,ross Works. He was a prominent Con- servative. WARRINGTON MOULDER, WOUNDED. After Twenty-one Days In The Trenches. Private Charles Frederick Wilson, 26, Clif- ton-street, • Warrington, King's Royal Rifles, who was a moulder at Messrs. Fletcher, Rus- sell's Works at the outbreak of the war and enlisted before the end of August, has written to his wife saying that he is wounded
